A stamp-only proof never authorizes a current byte hash. Absent children without retained history request an explicit reread instead of claiming they were removed. It captures outside the state lock, rechecks attribution before persisting the ledger batch, and installs evidence only after that transaction succeeds. Files beyond a hashing budget keep the length/mtime/mode identity, not a claim of byte-for-byte hashing. Attribution is best effort under exclusive path leases; a concurrent writer using another agent's leased path is not authenticated by this mechanism.

**Reading it.** `changes {project, since_seq?, path?, agent?, limit?}` returns the newest `limit` entries oldest first; `agentdocker changes [--project .] [--since N] [--path P] [--agent A] [-n N]` prints them with agent names, and `agentdocker blame <path>` is the same query for one file. An absolute path in a query is made relative to the checkout containing it, so callers need not know the root.

## Read sets and staleness

Staleness is grounded in the filesystem — what agents actually read and change — which covers the real case with no new concept for agents to learn; there is no separate key/document context store.

- **Observation.** Claude hooks call `observe` before Read/Grep/Glob and check `stale` before editing. MCP clients explicitly call `observe_paths` before reading and `check_stale` before editing. Read/Grep/Glob results are not intercepted by the daemon.
- **Identity.** Read marks contain absolute physical paths, SHA-256 content versions, times and optional HEAD context. The durable set is bounded by its capacity, below; overflow is refused rather than silently evicted. Separate worktrees and clones retain separate observations.
- **Capacity.** One agent's read set holds at most `[read_set] capacity` observations (distinct physical paths): 2,000 unless `agentd.toml` sets it, 100–10,000. An `observe` that would take the set past it is refused whole with `invalid` and `details: {capacity, held}`, and records nothing. Nothing is evicted, so a held observation is never lost to a newer one, and an `observe` that adds no new path is never refused, even after the capacity was lowered below what the set holds. A Claude hook's observation fails open: the read goes ahead unrecorded and an edit of that path is not guarded, so a session that fills its set checkpoints and continues in a replacement. The same capacity bounds every other way into a read set: an imported bundle's `read_set`, the union a `resume` acceptance seeds, and the observations an identity repair or a resumed session's fold joins (which also reads at most 4 MiB of stored observations, or 4 KiB per observation of capacity when that is more). One `observe` names 1–1,000 paths whatever the capacity, and a directory observation is bounded as an observation always is (20,000 entries, 256 MiB hashed). The daemon reads the setting at start and once a minute; a value out of range, or a file that does not read, leaves 2,000 in force and is said once in the daemon log.
- **Sizing.** A stored observation is about 250 bytes: an absolute path, a SHA-256 version, a time and a HEAD. Every `observe` rewrites the agent's whole set in one transaction under the state lock, so what it costs follows the size of the set, not the capacity: about 2 ms and 0.25 MB written at 1,000 observations, 3.5 ms and 0.5 MB at 2,000, 25 ms (50 ms at the 95th percentile) and 2.5 MB at 10,000. A `stale` check reads the whole set too, about 15 ms at 10,000, and one over every observation hashes each again. Read sets stay small: ninety-nine in a hundred hold under 20 observations and half hold one or two; the largest a real session has held is about 500, one batch of paths, and the largest built up read by read over a long session about 360; a session that observes every file of a checkout of 600 files one by one, with its source directories, holds about 580. Hence the default is three times both the largest real read set and that whole-checkout reader, rounded up; the floor is five times the 99th percentile; the ceiling keeps one observation within tens of milliseconds of lock time and a few megabytes of writing.
- **Verification.** Current content is compared directly, including uncommitted changes before watcher debounce. Queries do not acknowledge staleness. A newer read of a target can shadow an older directory observation when checking that target; broader directory checks still inspect the directory mark.
- **Watching and attribution.** The watcher covers registered Git/Agentfile checkouts, debounces events, honors hierarchical ignore rules, and emits explicit gaps for lost coverage. Attribution uses an unexpired exclusive lease on the physical checkout path; shared readers are not authorship evidence. Otherwise attribution is external, and all attribution is best-effort.
- **Notices.** Changes warn readers of the same checkout: an `agent_stale` event per change at once, and one inbox message of kind `stale` per reader on the next second's tick naming every path that changed after its observation since the last notice (`paths`, up to 200, `count`, and the last `changes` per path, up to 50), so a branch switch that touches hundreds of paths is one message, and created, removed and modified for one path within it are one path. While a reader still has its last notice queued nothing more is sent to it; the paths wait and the notice sent once that one has been acknowledged names them all (a queued envelope is never changed), so a reader that has not consumed a notice never accumulates a backlog of them. Up to 10,000 distinct paths wait per reader, and `count` is the number tracked, not a total beyond that cap; a notice is kept under 64 KiB by naming fewer paths and changes, down to the count alone. A notice that still cannot be queued is dropped: `stale` reads content and hooks deny a stale edit whether or not a notice arrived. Hooks surface queued notices and deny stale edits until the affected content is observed and reread. Live ledger events carry their own ledger sequence; replayed daemon events use the ordered event sequence.
- **Durability.** Read sets are stored as versioned-content documents, survive daemon restart and remain available after session exit for recovery, until the record is forgotten (`rm`, or `[agents] retention`): forgetting an agent deletes its read set in the same transaction, and a checkpoint keeps a copy of its own. A read set whose agent the store no longer knows, left by an earlier daemon, is deleted on the once-a-minute tick, a hundred at a time. The wire contract is in the [table](#wire-protocol).

The cycle is read → change → warning → stale-edit denial → reread, through supported hooks or explicit client calls. Generic adopted processes are not automatically observed.

## Project memory

Short facts a project's agents and the person keep for each other — a convention, a decision, where something lives — as documents of kind `memory` by project (`crates/core/src/memory.rs`, `daemon/tasks/memory.rs`), on the local machine only. A memory is added on purpose (`memory_add`, the `add_memory` MCP tool, `agentdocker memory add`), never taken from the journal, a message or a transcript, so nothing reaches it that nobody chose to keep; text that reads like a credential (a private key, a token of a known shape, also inside an assignment, a URL or a path, a long run of mixed letters and digits) is refused, in the text and in every link's target and note, a guard rather than a guarantee. One that is no longer true is superseded by a newer one, which keeps it but stops saying it, or forgotten for good by its author or the person; one kept with `forget_after` stops being said after that moment, as if forgotten, and stays until somebody forgets it. A `standing` memory (a convention or a decision rather than news) is said first. A memory naming `roles` (at most 8, from the project's role palette, any other refused `invalid`) is said only to agents holding one of them and to the person, and one naming none to everyone. A read names its reader in `from`, checked against its sender like every agent's word: the person reads every memory, an agent those for everyone and those for its roles, and a read naming nobody only those for everyone. Supersession is the reader's own: a memory replaced by one a reader cannot see is still said to that reader. A memory's author supersedes only a memory it can read. Memories naming roles never leave the machine: the mirror holds only what is for everyone. A new Claude Code, Codex or OpenCode session is told the project's profile through its own session-start hook, as context the provider takes and never as text typed into its terminal: 800 characters of memory, the standing ones oldest first and then the rest newest first (two kept at the same moment by id), a memory too long for what is left passed over rather than ending the list; one line each with its id and author and its text as a quoted, escaped string with every line break flattened, so none can open a section of its own, the whole block at most 4 KiB (`MEMORY_CONTEXT_BYTES`; a line that would pass it is passed over the same way); the block calls them data, never instructions. Claude Code takes it in `SessionStart`'s `additionalContext` after the journal digest; Codex in its `SessionStart` hook's `additionalContext`, which it adds as developer context, at a conversation's first turn (when Codex runs that hook), after `/clear` and again after a compaction; OpenCode in the system prompt of every request of the session, from the plugin's first `experimental.chat.system.transform` (see [the hooks adapter](#agentdocker-hook-cratesclisrchooksrs)). The bytes each hook writes are counted as AgentDocker's overhead (`hook` for Claude Code and OpenCode, `native_hook` for Codex); OpenCode's plugin repeats the start in each request's system prompt, and only the hook's one answer is counted. The MCP tools take Supermemory's names (`add_memory {content}`, `search_memory`, `get_profile`, `forget_memory`) and reach the caller's own project only. Search matches the query's words whole (`test` is not in `latest`), lower case, split at anything but a letter, a digit or `_` (an identifier such as `task_id` is one word, which `task` does not match); each word counts by its inverse document frequency over the current memories (BM25's, never negative), so a word few memories hold outweighs one most hold; a newer memory wins a tie, and between two kept at the same moment the lower id, so equal evidence always comes back in the same order. Nothing leaves the machine unless the person turns on the mirror in `agentd.toml`:

```toml
[memory.supermemory]
enabled = true
projects = ["<project id, prefix or root>"]
key_file = "/Users/me/.config/agentdocker/supermemory.key"  # regular file of this user, mode 0600
# base_url = "https://api.supermemory.ai"  # the default; https, or http on loopback for a server of one's own (Supermemory 0.0.9 or later)
# dreaming = "dynamic"  # or "instant"; absent by default, and then not sent
# supporting_context = "Decisions about the build, with their owners."  # at most 1500 characters; absent by default
```

Then, once a minute (`daemon/tasks/memory_mirror.rs`), each named project's copy in Supermemory (API v5, namespace `agentdocker_<project id>` limited to letters, digits, `_`, `:` and `-`, at most 100 characters) is brought in step with its current memories. What the copy may hold is a `memory_mirror` document per project *and destination* — a fingerprint of the server and of the key's own hash, never the key — so another server or account starts a copy of its own and never inherits another's: `ids` confirmed there, `processing` accepted and still being processed, `pending` about to be sent or sent without an answer. Each run first deletes what is no longer said (forgotten or superseded) from all three, `DELETE /ns/{namespace}/document` up to 100 ids a request, counting only ids the answer's `errors` does not list (one it reports not found is gone), so a failing addition never holds a forget back; then records the additions it is about to send as pending before any is sent (at most 50 a run), so a lost answer or a restart never leaves a forgotten memory unrecorded; then looks again at up to 20 documents already processing, in turn from where the last run stopped (`GET …/document/{id}`, `system.status`: `done` confirms, `failed` returns it to pending — it may still be there — and only a `404` drops it); then sends the additions, `POST …/document` under the memory's own id with `taskType: memory`, reading each answer's `status` (`done` confirms, a queued state is processing, `failed` stays pending). Two settings change what an addition carries, each sent only when written: `dreaming` (`dynamic`, which groups related documents before memories form, or `instant`, which forms them from each document at once and which Supermemory bills as one more operation per document) and `supporting_context` (sent as `supportingContext`, background that guides how memories are formed, refused when the file is read if it is over 1500 characters, counted as characters, not bytes, with a notice naming the limit; a `dreaming` other than those two is refused the same way). While a `dreaming` mode is set, a document is confirmed only once its memories are formed: an addition's `done` says only that the text is in, so it is held as processing, and a check whose `system.status` is `done` confirms it only when `system.dreamingStatus` is `done` too, keeps it processing while that is `dreaming`, takes any other value as an answer the contract does not have, and goes by `system.status` alone when the field is absent (a server older than it, or one of one's own). Without `dreaming`, `system.status` alone decides, as before. A server that does not take these fields may refuse the addition, which stops the run like any refusal: leave them out for it. An answer without the contract's fields — a delete without `count` and `errors`, a status the contract does not name — clears nothing and stops the run. Any other answer — `409` (a namespace conflict, not a duplicate, which the API answers `200 done`), `402` and the rest — or an unreachable server stops that run and is tried again the next minute. `memory_mirrored {project, added, removed}` says each change. The key is read through a verified 0600 handle, sent only as a bearer header and never logged; errors name neither the address nor the key. `base_url` is `https://`, or `http://` only to 127.0.0.1, [::1] or localhost, with no credentials, query or fragment. Each request has 15 seconds from connecting to the answer's last byte and follows no redirect, and TLS is verified against the roots the system trusts ([outbound TLS](#outbound-tls)), so a server of one's own behind an authority the system trusts is reached. Only memories go. Nothing depends on the mirror: without it the memory here is complete, and the hosted service's own terms and costs are the person's.

## The journal

Where the ledger records every file change, the journal records *what happened and why*, one line at a time, per project: coarse, readable by models and humans, and what a newcomer is handed instead of the event stream.

**Entries.** A `JournalEntry` carries project and per-project `seq`, time, the agent (or none, for a commit nobody is known to have made), its name and branch, the physical checkout and worktree, a kind, a summary with its source (`explicit`, `transcript`, `synthesised`), the released resources, up to 200 checkout-relative paths with the real count, HEAD before and after, and the ledger seq range for drill-down. Kinds: `release` (leases dropped; the entry says what changed under them), `note` (free text), `commit` (HEAD moved), `join`, `leave`, and `handoff`.

**Write path.** A `release` or `release_all` that freed at least one lease passes the *release barrier* when a released lease protects a path — the daemon asks the watcher to record whatever it is still debouncing, bounded at 500 ms including queue admission, so a change made a moment before the release is in the entry — then, for each released `path:` resource, runs one indexed prefix-range query on the ledger bounded by the lease's durable `change_seq` (numeric `acquired_at` fallback for legacy leases), restricted to the same physical checkout; unions, sorts, dedupes, and caps the paths; takes the explicit summary or synthesises one ("edited 3 files under src/: parser.rs, lexer.rs, mod.rs"); and writes the entry, its path index rows, and its search row **in the same transaction as the lease deletions and replay events**, so a crash can leave neither a released lease without its entry nor an entry for a lease still held. An explicit nonempty summary is journaled even when no lease was held. A release under which nothing changed and nothing was said writes no entry. Agents joining and leaving a project are journaled; so is a checkout's HEAD moving — once per checkout and HEAD however many agents share it, named through `git log`, attributed to the only agent in the checkout, else the holder of the `branch:` lease, else the live agent the daemon created the worktree for (`worktree_create`, `--isolate`; remembered in memory, so a restarted daemon falls back to the leases), else the holder of an exclusive `path:` lease over the checkout, else nobody — a commit in a checkout nobody made or holds is `external`. A checkout the daemon is committing in (`commit`) is marked while the commit is in flight, so the watcher does not also write its own entry for it. `journal_add` appends a note.

**Storage.** `journal` (rowid `id`, `project`, `seq`, `at`, `agent`, `branch`, `kind`, the JSON blob; unique on project + seq; indexed by project + branch + seq and project + agent + seq), `journal_paths` (project, path, seq — the index behind `--path`, a prefix range), `journal_fts` (FTS5, contentless, rowid = `journal.id`; when the SQLite build lacks FTS5 the daemon logs it once and `--grep` falls back to `LIKE`), and `journal_cursors`. Entries are kept forever unless a retention is set (below); `journal prune --before <seq>` deletes on demand, from all three tables together. The daemon keeps a ring of the newest 256 entries per active project, loaded on first use and dropped ten minutes after the project's last live agent leaves; a plain listing whose window lies in the ring never touches SQLite.

**Retention.** Entries are kept forever by default: the journal is the audit trail, a busy five-agent team writes roughly a megabyte a day at worst, and SQLite is comfortable at millions of rows. `agentdocker journal prune --before <duration|seq> [--project]` deletes on demand, and an optional `[journal] retention = "180d"` in `agentd.toml` (the daemon's own configuration file in its home, distinct from `policy.toml`) is applied by the once-a-minute tick: per project, the oldest entry is probed first so a project with nothing expired costs one index lookup, then at most 1,000 entries go per tick, `journal`, `journal_paths` and `journal_fts` rows together, so a backlog drains over several ticks instead of one long delete under the state lock. Each pass is announced as `journal_pruned` with its reason (`retention`, or `request` for the command). A cursor below the new floor needs no clamping: a digest shows what is after the cursor, and what is gone is gone. An unreadable or invalid file is logged once and ignored rather than read as "keep nothing"; unknown keys are errors. Freed pages are reused by SQLite; `agentdocker daemon vacuum` reclaims disk when someone wants it back. It holds the state lock while SQLite rewrites the file, which would stall a provider bridge waiting on the daemon, so it is refused while sessions are live unless `--force` accepts that. Checkpoints have their own `checkpoints prune --older-than <duration>`, which spares any checkpoint whose author, acceptor or handoff addressee is still live and deletes the rest with their announcement in one transaction. There are no roll-up summaries.

**Ledger coupling.** An entry inlines its paths (capped at 200, with `paths_total`) *and* carries the ledger seq range. Digests therefore never touch the ledger, and the range still expands to individual changes while the ledger rows exist; once the ledger is pruned (newest 20,000 rows per project) the range dangles harmlessly and the inline list is what remains.

**Reading it.** `journal {project, since_seq?, until_seq?, agent?, branch?, kind?, path?, grep?, limit?}` returns the newest `limit` entries oldest first with the resolved project id; `agentdocker journal [--project .] [--since N] [--until N] [--agent A] [--branch B] [--kind K] [--path P] [--grep TEXT] [-n N] [--follow]` prints them (by default the last 50 of the project containing the current directory), `journal add "…"` appends a note, `journal prune --before <seq|duration>` trims. `release --summary "…"` (and the MCP `release` tool's `summary`) is how an agent says what it did; the MCP `journal_note` tool is how it leaves a note. Every append is announced as `journal_appended`, which `--follow` streams (the stream is opened before the snapshot it continues, so nothing falls between them, and the listing's filters apply to streamed entries too).

**Reading it incrementally.** Every reader has a cursor per project — `journal_cursors (agent, project, seq, updated_at)`, cached in the daemon and written through only when it moves forward — recording the last entry it was shown. A registration seeds the newcomer's cursor: from a finished agent of the same name in the same project that left within seven days (a resumed Claude Code session keeps its `claude-<prefix>` name, so it continues where it left off), else at the newer of "24 hours ago" and "20 entries back". The human reads as `user`. A `journal` request with `digest: {reader, max_entries, max_chars, all_branches?, advance?}` answers `digest {text, head_seq, shown, collapsed, other_branches}` instead of a listing: entries after the reader's cursor (or after `since_seq` when given), the reader's own branch verbatim plus `join`/`leave`/`commit`/`handoff` from every branch, the newest within the budget rendered one per line, older ones folded into a leading "… N earlier entries" line, other branches into a trailing count; the reader's own `join`/`leave` lines are not news and are skipped. `advance` moves the cursor to `head_seq` when text was produced — everything the filter hid counts as seen too. An empty `project` means the reader's own. Served from the ring whenever the cursor lies inside it; otherwise the newest 1,000 entries after the cursor are read from the store. Every move is announced as `journal_read`. `agentdocker journal --new [--ack] [--all-branches] [--as AGENT]` prints the human's (or an agent's) digest.

A digest reads:

```
Since you last looked (9 entries):
… 4 earlier entries (agentdocker journal --since 1173)
- 1h ago   gemini-2 joined (worktree wt-2, branch feat/y)
- 18m ago  claude-a1b2 [main] committed <commit>: "Add lease transfer"
- 4m ago   codex-1 [feat/x] released src/parser.rs, src/lexer.rs (+3 more): "rewrote the tokenizer to handle unicode escapes"
3 entries on other branches: agentdocker journal --all-branches
```

When the budget bites, the *oldest* entries collapse into the leading "… N earlier entries" line and the newest stay verbatim.

**Adapters.** The Claude Code hooks hand the digest over as `additionalContext`: `SessionStart` with up to 20 entries or 2,000 characters (`--digest-entries`, `--digest-chars`), `UserPromptSubmit` only what is new and at most 5 entries or 500 characters (`--prompt-digest-entries`, `--prompt-digest-chars`), and nothing when nothing is new; `PostToolUse` never carries journal text. `Stop` reads the last 64 KB of the session transcript, takes the last assistant message with text — fenced code and headings dropped, markdown stripped, first paragraph, trimmed to 280 characters at a word boundary — and sends it as the `release_all` summary with `summary_source: transcript`; a transcript summary only ever describes leases actually released, whereas an explicit `--summary` is journaled even when nothing was held. The MCP `read_journal {since?, all_branches?}` tool returns the digest and advances the cursor. Renderers can quote a transcript summary rather than assert it; an explicit summary always wins and the ledger synthesis is the floor. The model is not asked for a summary by blocking `Stop`.

## Wire protocol

Transport: newline-delimited JSON over a Unix domain socket at `$AGENTDOCKER_SOCKET` (default `~/.agentdocker/agentd.sock`, mode `0600`). A socket name is limited by the kernel (104 bytes on macOS and the BSDs, 108 on Linux), so a home whose path leaves no room for `container.sock` keeps both sockets in a private directory (`0700`, ours alone, ownership checked before binding) under `/tmp` — never an environment-dependent directory, which a service, a cron job and a shell can each see differently — named `agentdocker-<hash of the home's bytes>` (removed again when the daemon stops and it is empty, as the session directory `s` under it is once the owners nobody holds are swept — each lock taken and held while its files go, and an owner that finds its lock on a file that was unlinked between its open and its lock (`Lock::is_at`) takes the lock again, so two owners never hold one id — so a daemon that is gone leaves nothing in `/tmp`; an unacknowledged exit report keeps its directory); the home is canonicalized once (`agentdocker_host::dirs::home`) so a symlinked path spells the same directory everywhere; the daemon and every client compute the same place without a pointer file, an installed service is pinned to the resolved path with `--socket` and its commands use that socket, and `agentdocker daemon status` prints both paths. A path that still does not fit is refused up front by both daemon and client, naming the limit. The restricted container endpoint is optional: it announces `restricted_endpoint_listening` when it serves; if it cannot be served the daemon announces `restricted_endpoint_unavailable`, `ping` stops reporting it, `grant-access` answers `unavailable`, and the host socket carries on. A client that starts the daemon on demand watches the child it spawned, so a daemon that dies on startup fails the command at once with the log's last lines rather than after the start timeout. One request object per line, tagged by `"op"`; responses tagged by `"type"`.

```json
{"op":"claim","agent":"writer","resource":"path:/repo/src","mode":"exclusive","ttl_secs":300,"note":"refactoring"}
{"type":"lease","lease":{"id":"3f1c...","resource":"path:/repo/src","holder":"9a2b...","mode":"exclusive","acquired_at":"...","change_seq":42,"expires_at":"...","note":"refactoring"}}
```

| Request | Response | Notes |
|---|---|---|
| `worktree_create {agent, path, branch, from?, seed_build?}` | `worktree {path, branch, seeded?, unwitnessed?}`, `error(identity_mismatch)` | host-only; new linked checkout on a new branch, at the agent's HEAD or at `from` (a branch, tag or commit; an argument-shaped `from` is refused). The daemon remembers the agent that asked, so a commit made there with git alone is journaled as that agent's while it lives, and witnesses those commits (see [the review gate](#channels)): it puts its post-commit hook in the repository's hooks folder and a witness file in the new checkout's own git directory, kept as a `witnessed_worktrees` row and announced as `worktree_witnessed`; `unwitnessed` says why not when it could not (the repository or the person sets `core.hooksPath`, a post-commit hook is already chained beside another, no `agentdocker` command beside the daemon), which never fails the worktree. `seed_build` (at most 16 directories relative to the agent's checkout, such as `target` or `node_modules`) seeds the new checkout with that build output, after it is made and off the state lock; an absolute name, `..` or `.git` is refused `invalid` before anything is made. Every step goes through directory descriptors opened `O_NOFOLLOW` part by part, in the source and in the new checkout, so a folder swapped for a symlink mid-seed redirects nothing; the seed is built under a temporary name in the destination's folder and published by a rename that never replaces (`RENAME_EXCL` on macOS, `RENAME_NOREPLACE` on Linux; there is no look-then-rename fallback, so a filesystem without one gets nothing published), and only what it made is ever removed. `seeded` has one `{dir, outcome, note?}` per name, in order: `cloned` (one `fclonefileat(2)` of the whole directory, copy-on-write on APFS), `reflinked` (every file by `FICLONE`, copy-on-write on Btrfs, XFS and the like), `copied` (file by file in chunks, stopping as soon as it would write more than half the space that was free when it began), `skipped` (the agent's checkout has no such directory), `refused` (in the source it is not a directory or has a symlink on its way, none being followed; the new checkout already has it or a symlink or file on its way, or another writer made it meanwhile, theirs left as it was; a filesystem with no such rename; or Windows, which seeds nothing yet) or `failed` (an error, or the 120-second limit for all of them, checked between chunks since a clone is one call; what it made is removed). Permissions and times are kept, symlinks inside are copied as symlinks, and no outcome fails the worktree |
| `worktree_diff {agent}` | `diff {text}` | host-only tracked diff |
| `worktree_remove {agent}` | `worktree_removed {path, branch_removed, note?}`, `error(conflict\|invalid\|not_found)` | remove an ended session's linked worktree: refused while the session runs or the worktree has uncommitted or untracked files; `git worktree remove` without force, then `git branch -d`, so Git checks the configured upstream (or HEAD without an upstream), not the UI review's commit list. `note` carries Git's deletion report with full object IDs or its refusal details; a missing report never falls back to a cached review identity. Emits `worktree_cleanup`. A session in the project's main folder has nothing to remove |
| `review_branch {agent}` | `branch_review {review}` | what an agent's branch would bring to the branch checked out in its project's main checkout: its branch and head, the target, the merge base, its commits (newest first) and files (status, lines added and removed) since the merge base, the unified diff (cut at 512 KiB, `truncated`), tracked files it has not committed, tracked files changed in the main checkout, files a merge would conflict in (`git merge-tree --write-tree`, nothing moved), paths someone holds in the main checkout, and `in_progress` — a merge, rebase, cherry-pick, revert or bisect git has under way in the main checkout, by its markers in that checkout's git directory (`other` for a sequencer or one a newer daemon names), which is then the one blocker. Read for a live or an ended agent while its checkout exists. An agent working in the main checkout on the target branch has nothing apart to review. Read-only |
| `merge_branch {from?, agent, head}` | `branch_merged {target, commit}`, `error(conflict\|not_found\|unavailable)`, `error(identity_mismatch)` | the person's one-step merge: re-reads the review and refuses before Git runs if the reviewed head moved, an operation is in progress, or dirty files, conflicts or held paths stop it. Rechecks operation markers immediately before execution; an unreadable check returns `unavailable`. Otherwise runs `git merge --no-ff` of that head into the main checkout's branch. On execution failure it preserves Git's state and asks the person to inspect the main folder; it never automatically aborts or resets, since another operation can start after the check. A hook rejection or concurrent change may leave a pending merge for the person to finish or abort. Journaled against the person when they have a record; emits `branch_merged` on success. Never pushes |
| `commit {agent, message, all?, push?}` | `committed {head, branch?, files, pushed}`, `error(identity_mismatch)` | host-only; commits the agent's checkout and journals it against that agent. `all` stages tracked modifications first; `push` pushes afterwards, and a push that fails answers `error(unavailable)` with the commit's sha, since the commit was made. A pending merge may commit unchanged files; an ordinary empty commit returns `conflict`. When Git cannot execute or finish the merge-state check, the response is `error(unavailable)` with the original error details. `branch` is read from the full ref HEAD names, so a tag of the same name never lengthens it to `heads/<name>`. As soon as git has made the commit, before any push, it is kept as that agent's authorship of the branch (`authorship_recorded`, witness `commit`; nothing on a detached HEAD). Nothing is written into the commit itself — the git author is unchanged and no trailer is added |
| `report_commit {checkout, token, head, branch?}` | `commit_witnessed {recorded, agent?, branch?, note?}`, `error(forbidden\|not_found\|conflict\|invalid\|unavailable\|storage_unavailable)` | what the post-commit hook of a witnessed worktree sends through `agentdocker report-commit`: the commit HEAD is at in `checkout`, the full ref HEAD names (none when detached) and the worktree's witness token. A checkout the daemon witnesses no worktree at is `not_found`; another token is `forbidden`. Over the socket the report must come from inside the session of one of the worktree's owners — a live agent holding an exclusive `path:` lease over the whole worktree, else the live agent it was created for — by the same walk up the sender's parents a verdict's sender is held to, and the commit is recorded as that owner's; from inside any other agent's session, or from none, it is `forbidden` (with the person check off, and for the daemon's own callers, the first owner). Who made it is never the client's to say. HEAD and the ref must be what git reads in the checkout now (`conflict`). A detached HEAD, a commit the daemon is making there itself (its `commit` records that), or a worktree with no live owner answers `recorded: false` with a note; otherwise the commit is kept once per project, commit and branch (`authorship_recorded`, witness `hook`; `note: "already recorded"` when it was). Host socket only |
| `integrate {agent, source, validation, apply?}` | `integration {source_head, applied, clean, text}`, `error(identity_mismatch)` | validated source; apply leaves merge uncommitted and target lease held |
| `grant_access {agent, container_root, ttl_secs?}` | `access {grant, token, socket, expires_at}` | host-only; TTL 1–86400 seconds, default 3600; CLI writes token privately and prints grant ID |
| `revoke_access {grant}` | `ok` | host-only; deny new requests, preserve leases |
| `authenticate {token}` | `ok` | restricted endpoint only; precedes one scoped request |
| `ping` | `pong` | version, uptime, restricted endpoint while serving, the serving process's pid and executable, which after a reload say which release actually serves, and the experimental switches as the daemon applies them (`experimental`: `reload`, `secret_input`, `native_codex`, from `[experimental]` in `agentd.toml` with the environment's word on top), which `daemon status` prints and the Codex bridge and hooks follow, and the [screen judge](#the-screen-judge-opt-in)'s status (`judge`: `off`; `on {judgments, model, calls_last_minute, calls_per_minute, problem?}`, `problem` the last call's failure in words that name neither the address nor the key; or `unusable {reason}`), which `daemon status` prints as its `judge` line; both absent from an older daemon |
| `build_image {spec: {engine, connection?, context, recipe, timeout_secs?}}` | `image_build {build}` | host-only Docker/Podman build from captured inputs; timeout defaults to 600 seconds, valid range 1–3600; immutable image ID and atomic provenance/event |
| `images` | `image_builds {builds}` | retained build evidence, including after restart |
| `daemon_process` | `daemon_process {pid, started_at?, successor?, successor_started_at?}` | which process is the daemon serving this socket, born when, and the successor a handover in flight names with its birth as read when it was named (held in memory only; taken away the moment the attempt is aborted, whether or not the abort is recorded). Read-only. A session owner asks it before it takes a controller connection, and accepts the serving daemon by pid and birth or the named successor by pid and birth |
| `run {spec}` | `agent` | spawns `spec.command`; a supplied `spec.workdir` must resolve to an existing directory and is stored as its physical path before policy loading or launch side effects (also for pane/container launches); unresolved paths, files and symlink cycles return `invalid`, and a resolver task failure returns `internal`; child gets `AGENTDOCKER_SOCKET`, `AGENTDOCKER_AGENT_ID`, `AGENTDOCKER_AGENT_NAME`; `spec.restart` starts it again after it exits (`no` by default, cleared by `stop`); `spec.restore` brings it back under the same id after a daemon restart; `spec.in_pane` starts it in a new `tmux` session and registers it instead, so tmux owns the process and there is no captured log — it requires `spec.workdir` (tmux needs a directory to start in) and tmux 3.2 or newer (`new-session -e`, which is how the agent is told its own id), and is refused with `run_container`; the record carries `launched_by=user` when the person launched it: the process that sent the `run` (or `run_container`, or `resume_session`) is outside every agent's session as the person check traces it (the app, the person's own terminal, the daemon's own process), traced whether or not that check is on — turning it off lets a launch through and says nothing of whose it is — and a spec's own `launched_by` is dropped at admission, so a launch from inside an agent's session, or from a process that cannot be traced, carries none |
| `resume_session {agent, spec}` | `agent`, `error(invalid\|conflict\|not_found\|ambiguous\|storage_unavailable\|unavailable\|internal)` | bring an ended session back under its own record: `spec` is checked against the record before anything starts (`identity::relaunch_check` — the record is not live, local, outside a container, has no input binding, a plain `session_id` label of 1–128 `[A-Za-z0-9_-]` starting alphanumeric, runtime `claude-code` (a Codex session is not resumed through this request) and the same in `spec`; `spec` is a plain process, not paned, isolated or restored, in the record's own `workdir`, and its command carries `--resume <session_id>`), a subscriber on the record answers `conflict`, and so does the old process when its pid is still alive with the recorded start time or alive and undatable (`same_process`, fail closed). The record becomes managed with the launch as its spec (its name and labels kept, `session_id` included), its process fields cleared, and the transition is committed with `session_relaunched {agent, session}` under the fence before memory changes — every check is run again under that lock, and a record that differs in any field from the one checked (it went live, ended again, took a role or a report meanwhile) answers `conflict` rather than being written over; a write that fails leaves the ended record as it was (`internal` is the checkout resolution not completing, before any of this). Then the process starts as `run` starts one, under the same id, so what was queued for the session, its aliases, questions and cards are its own; a launch that fails or exits at once leaves the record ended again with its queue, and never a second live process. CLI `reconnect`; the app's **Reconnect here**. Both clients preserve the recorded executable, supported Claude options and explicit environment, replace session selectors, omit initial positional input and regenerate only the exact owned channel prefix. Both clients also apply the `plain_session_id` rule before constructing the resume selector. Unknown/ambiguous options or altered MCP configuration refuse before sending this request; externally registered sessions with no recorded command keep provider defaults. |
| `run_container {spec, build, options?}` | `agent` | host-only; retained image with durable identity/intent; opt-in checkout/scoped endpoint mounts, Podman VM transport, and bridge networking |
| `restart_container {agent}` | `agent` | host-only; new identity from same build after confirmed exit; `conflict` while exit is uncertain |
| `session_attach {runtime, profile, session, workdir, adapter}` | `agent`, `error(invalid\|conflict\|internal)` | the one record of a provider session found or made by its key (runtime, verified profile, the provider's session id) and its contact noted; only a Codex conversation from its verified cached host, whose thread no binding names and no terminal proves. Contact is presence (`SESSION_PRESENCE`), never lifecycle. No CLI: the MCP server and Codex hooks send it. |
| `register {spec, pid?, session?}` | `agent`, `error(identity_mismatch)` | external process; PID must be positive and fit i32, and must name a process the sender may name (see the security model: itself, one of its parents or a process it started; a Codex terminal nobody holds resuming the named conversation from under its profile's proven shared server; a process a live agent holds only from inside that agent's session; any from the person), or the registration is refused `identity_mismatch` with nothing done; `spec.workdir` decides the project; `session` is the multiplexer the client can see it is in, read first-hand from its own environment; the record also carries `terminal`, the application its terminal is in, read by the daemon from the process's ancestry. A registration joins the live record of the same process (equal pid and known, equal birth time), runtime and project when both name the same provider session (a non-empty `session_id`), wherever each says it works — a session's shell moves, and Claude Code reports as a hook's `cwd` wherever its Bash tool last changed directory — and, where a side names no session, only when the physical checkout is the same as well (`identity::same_registration`). Where several live records match, a record that names the registration's own session is the session's and answers, and a record in the registration's own checkout that names no session is then neither joined nor taught the session, which would make a second record of it. Several that name the session are one session counted twice (as a registration from a moved shell could once leave it): the one that stays answers (`identity::keeper`: a record a live subscriber holds, then one input is delivered through, then the first registered), nothing folds the others while they live, and the offline `agentdocker identity-repair` reconciles them. Where none names the session, several records in the registration's own checkout that name none cannot be told apart, and the registration is refused `invalid`. A `codex` registration whose pid is Codex's detached app-server (`codex app-server … --managed-daemon`, the host of a Codex terminal session's MCP servers) is refused `invalid`. A registration that names a provider session (a non-empty `session_id` label, which only a hooks adapter vouches for) whose ended record is still held — same runtime, project and physical checkout, its process gone, neither side managed, contained, in a pane or bound to an input controller — is that session come back: the ended record that finished last stays canonical and takes the new pid, birth, status and last_seen, keeps its id, conversations, cursor and what was still queued for it; the fresh record and every earlier ended record of the session are retired into it — their queues one queue in durable sequence order with each message once (a broadcast that reached two lives), every document that named them rewritten the way the offline identity repair rewrites it (a question an earlier life asked is the canonical record's to cancel; open and closed channels keep their membership, opener and reviewer references under the canonical ID; duplicate members collapse to one; an access grant, a restore point or a rewrite that would make a self-review or self-question refuses), an alias that pointed at a retired record pointed at the canonical one, their cursors gone and their ids `identity_alias` documents — planned and written by the store as one transaction with the event (`plan_fold`/`write_resume`), announced by `session_resumed` naming every retired id. A record whose process still runs, or one that holds leases, waits on a lease, has stale changes owed to it, has a live subscriber, or a fresh record that already has input-delivery evidence (`fresh.input_delivery.is_some()`), is not folded (the fresh record then keeps its own); a duplicate message that differs in content, a store that cannot say or a refused rewrite leaves everything as it was. A registration sent over the socket resumes an ended record only from outside every other live agent's session — its parents read as the person check's trace reads them, past another user's process such as root's `login` above a terminal, so a sender whose parents still cannot all be read, or that has ended, cannot show it and is refused — judged as the agent check judges being inside agent A's session (A's recorded processes among the sender's parents, A's terminal's companion launcher, the proven shared server of A's profile), or when the sender may act as every ended record itself (the proven shared server of the profile they name), or from the person: a process inside agent A's session acts as A and never becomes ended agent B. A live record the registration joins (one its session's MCP server made first, naming no session) counts as another agent's too: neither record's labels nor its process's command line, which whoever runs in it can choose, are evidence that it may become the ended one. Otherwise it is refused `identity_mismatch` under the lock that would fold it, before anything is written: the joined record learns no session, and no record is made under B's session. A joined record that already named the session before the registration is answered as it is, and its ended lives are left as they are. So a session the person resumes from their own terminal (`claude --resume`, `claude --continue`, `codex resume`, `agentdocker codex --resume`) resumes its record when its hooks register before its MCP server does, and is otherwise refused and left for the person to resume; and so does the person's own `register`, while an agent that resumes another, ended session from inside its own session is refused: intended, since that process would be both. A Claude Code session's MCP server sees to the order: under a command line that resumes (`--resume`, `-r`, `--continue`, `-c`, the picker included), it waits, up to ten seconds shared with the channel's wait for the same hooks, for a live record of its host process (the same pid and known birth) and runtime naming one session, and registers naming that session, which joins it; past the wait, or where the host's records name two sessions, it registers naming none. Its own `CLAUDE_CODE_SESSION_ID` is not read, since under the picker it names a fresh session that never runs. Once registered (a new record, one joined, a keyed record proven, a session resumed), the record says who started it when nothing has yet, traced on its own task after the answer has gone (the reply waits for neither the process table nor a signature check, and no lock is held while they run) and written in its own commit only while the record is still that process: for a live, unmanaged session outside a pane or container, not a connector agent, whose process is still the one recorded (pid and birth), the daemon walks every parent from that process's own up to the top (the first process, or another user's). A live agent's own process (pid and birth), a provider's session process or a Codex server anywhere on the way makes it an agent's (`launched_by=agent`), above a terminal as much as below one. A root on the way is evidence for the person, never the end of the walk: by the executable the kernel names, `login`, `sshd` or `sshd-session` at the system's own paths, on macOS a known terminal or editor application (by its bundle's name, never any bundle) installed directly in `/System/Applications` or `/Applications` (or their `Utilities`), and elsewhere on Unix a known terminal emulator under `/usr`, `/bin` or `/sbin` — in every case with the file, and every folder from its bundle (or its own folder) down to it, owned by root and writable by nobody else, as the system's programs and an App Store or administrator's install are. On macOS a known terminal or editor application the person installed themselves, in `/Applications` (or its `Utilities`) or their own `~/Applications`, counts too when the program running is the bundle's own (in its `Contents/MacOS`) and `/usr/bin/codesign --verify --strict -R='anchor apple generic'` passes on the bundle within five seconds — its code and sealed resources intact (so a program added inside breaks it) and signed by a certificate chained to Apple (so an ad hoc or self-made signature does not do). It is checked afresh at every trace, never remembered, since a kept verdict would outlive a change to any sealed file; a timeout or a check that cannot run says nothing, and with no other root found the starter is left unsaid, to be traced again at the next registration. A copy elsewhere, an unsigned or self-signed bundle, or any other program is no root. The top reached through a root, with no agent's session anywhere, makes it the person's (`launched_by=user`); reached through none (an orphan, a shell whose agent exited, a multiplexer server, any session on Windows), or a chain that breaks first — a parent that has ended, a "parent" born after its child (a reused pid) — makes it `launched_by=unknown`. The label is written with `agent_launched_by {agent, project?, by}` in one commit and never again: a later registration traces only a record without it, so a lost ancestry cannot turn an agent's or an unknown session into the person's. A row this user owns that cannot be read now, a process that is no longer the one recorded, or the daemon's own process anywhere on the way, a terminal it opened included (its launches are said at launch), decide nothing, and the next registration traces again. A client's own `launched_by` is dropped at admission. `adopt` and `session_attach` do the same. The label is attribution for display and filtering, never authorization: a terminal an agent opens through the system's app launcher (`open`, `osascript`) is not told apart from the person's own, so nothing may grant rights from it. |
| `deregister {agent}` | `agent`, `error(identity_mismatch)` | marks an external agent exited |
| `discover` | `processes` | running processes of known agent runtimes that no live agent claims by pid, from the daemon's last scan (every five seconds while anyone subscribes to events — the app, an `events` stream, a webhook sink — and otherwise only when asked; a healthy scan older than four seconds is redone; failed scans return `unavailable` and retain the previous snapshot) |
| `runtimes` | `runtimes {runtimes: RuntimeInfo[]}` | the agent tools on the host — CLI and version, desktop apps, config directory, whether the MCP server and hooks are wired in, and when the hooks are missing which required events lack our command (`hooks_missing`, from which a client reads an earlier version's set as outdated when some but not all are missing; every event when there is no file or hooks are disabled wholesale, none when the file is unreadable, malformed or structurally invalid; those files are Unverified, including a non-Boolean `disableAllHooks`), for Claude Code whether the shell's startup file carries the channel flag (`shell`: Wired, Missing, Unverified, or Unsupported for a shell setup does not know) — with the unregistered running processes of each, and the vendor's browser extension per Chromium-family browser profile it is installed in (`extensions`: label, browser, profile when the browser has several, version, and the native messaging bridge registered for it, found by the extension's id among a manifest's `allowed_origins`). A runtime that has only an extension (`claude-browser`, `chatgpt-browser`) works inside the browser: its sessions are never listed, and every surface that shows the row says so (`runtime::IN_BROWSER`) |
| `adopt {pid, name?, runtime?}` | `agent`, `error(identity_mismatch)` | registers such a process; `invalid` if a live agent already has the pid, or if the pid is a known runtime's helper rather than a session (the message says which helper); the pid is checked as a `register`'s is, and the same facts are held to when it commits (see the security model), so the app and the person's own terminal adopt any process, and a process inside an agent's session only one it may name |
| `stop {agent, force?}` | `agent` | validated SIGTERM/SIGKILL; returns `stopping` until observed exit, retaining leases. The record's `stop_requested` turns `true` in the same commit as `stopping` and `agent_stopping`, and stays so once the agent has exited, until a run begins under its id again (see [A stop on the record](#supervision-policy-and-dashboard)). A terminal program AgentDocker did not start has the terminal modes a shell does not use itself (mouse reporting, focus reports, the keyboard protocol, a hidden cursor) turned off once it has exited, within ten seconds, through the terminal held open since before the signal, and only once the same shell (pid and birth) has its own job back in that terminal's foreground |
| `remove {agent}` | `ok` | forget a finished agent, its read set with it; `invalid` while it is live, saying `stop` for a managed one and `deregister --as` for a process AgentDocker did not start, so that forgetting an adopted record never signals its process |
| `list {all?, project?, labels?}` | `agents` plus optional `aliases` (former ID → canonical ID) for returned records | live only unless `all`; `project` is an id prefix or an absolute path inside it; `labels` must all match |
| `inspect {agent}` | `agent` | |
| `heartbeat {agent}` | `ok`, `error(identity_mismatch)` | bumps `last_seen` |
| `report {agent, vcs?}` | `ok`, `error(identity_mismatch)` | what an adapter observed; a changed `vcs` is stored and announced |
| `observe {agent, paths}` | `reads {reads: ReadMark[]}`, `error(invalid)`, `error(identity_mismatch)` | capture content immediately before reading 1–1,000 paths; a directory is one fingerprint of its tree, and a file past the 256 MiB hashing budget is fingerprinted by its length, modification time and mode instead of failing the observation. `invalid` with `details: {capacity, held}` when the paths would take the read set past its [capacity](#read-sets-and-staleness); nothing is recorded then |
| `reads {agent}` | `reads {reads: ReadMark[]}` | durable observations |
| `stale {agent, paths?}` | `stale {stale: StalePath[]}` | compare current content; querying never clears staleness. A covering directory observation remembers deleted children as well as unchanged and never-existing targets. Unchanged target entries remain fresh despite changed siblings. For a changed target, clearing an own edit requires retained baseline entries and matching attributed current-entry identities recorded with committed ledger rows. The watcher must confirm its pending application queue within 500 ms and have no known coverage gap since observation; neither condition proves OS event completeness. Content is captured again after that wait. An old own/sibling row alone cannot authorize newer bytes. Missing/evicted proof, a partial 500-entry page (counted before exact timestamp and checkout filtering), an unconfirmed flush, unknown attribution or a coverage gap stays stale. The returned path is the one to reread |
| `checkpoint {agent,key,task,assumptions?,next_steps?,links?,release_leases?}` | `checkpoint`, `error(identity_mismatch)` | persist context before optional release; retries are idempotent |
| `resume {agent,checkpoint,acknowledge?}` | `recovery`, `error(identity_mismatch)` | verify and optionally accept a same-checkout handoff; accepting a bundle also moves its leases to the recipient when the sender asked, seeds the recipient's read set, and sets its journal cursor to the sender's; `invalid` when the seeded set joined to the recipient's own would pass the read-set capacity |
| `checkpoints {agent?}` | `checkpoints` | list durable checkpoints |
| `checkpoint_prune {agent?, older_than_secs}` | `pruned {removed}` | forget checkpoints older than that whose author, acceptor and handoff addressee have all finished, with the handoff bundles carrying them |
| `handoff {agent, to?, task?, note?, links?, transfer_leases?, key?}` | `handoff {bundle}`, `error(identity_mismatch)` | a checkpoint addressed to `to` (an agent ref or `role:<name>`) with the sender's state bundled around it, announced to `to` as a `handoff` message; leases are released unless they are to move at acceptance; without `to` the bundle is an export; retries with the same key return the same bundle |
| `handoffs {agent?}` | `handoffs {bundles}` | bundles sent by or addressed to the agent, oldest first; all of them without one |
| `import {agent, bundle}` | `handoff {bundle}` | a bundle exported on another host, re-homed to the agent's checkout and addressed to it, to accept with `resume`; `invalid` when its `read_set` is larger than this daemon's read-set capacity |
| `validate {agent,command,timeout_secs?}` | `validation`, `error(identity_mismatch)` | execute and retain code-specific evidence |
| `validations {agent}` | `validations` | evidence for one session |
| `attach {agent, cols?, rows?}` | `events_ready`, then a stream of `output {data}` | connects to a managed agent's terminal; the client then sends `attach_input` and `attach_resize` on the same connection, and closing it detaches without disturbing the agent |
| `attach_input {data}` | — | keystrokes for an attached terminal, base64 because they are bytes |
| `attach_resize {cols, rows}` | — | the attached window changed size, so the agent gets `SIGWINCH` |
| `channels {project?, all?, agent?}` | `channels {channels: Channel[]}` | the rooms agents share in a project; open ones by default, `all` includes closed-but-unpruned, `agent` narrows to its own and lets `project` be empty |
| `channel_open {agent, task, members?, name?, project?}` | `channel {channel}`, `error(conflict\|invalid)`, `error(identity_mismatch)` | a room for a task; members default to every other live agent in the project. `project` (id or path) is the project the room belongs to when the opener is in none or in another — a person opening a room from the app names the one they are looking at — and the opener's own otherwise; an opener in no project with none named is `invalid`. `name` is the `#name` people use (lowercase letters, digits and hyphens, at most 40, unique among the project's open channels; made from the task when absent, so `Plan the release` opens `#plan-the-release`); the id stays the identity, a closed channel keeps its name in history and reuse makes a new id. A record of runtime `human` is never admitted to a collision room. |
| `channel_invite {agent, channel, member}` | `channel`, `error(forbidden\|invalid\|storage_unavailable)`, `error(identity_mismatch)` | an existing member adds one live agent to an open task channel; membership, the `channel_invited {channel, by, agent}` event and invitation notice commit together. A repeated member is a no-op. Closed and collision channels refuse invitations. The new member receives subsequent channel messages through the ordinary queue. |
| `conversations {project?, reader?}` | `conversations {conversations}` | what a reader (the person unless `reader` names an agent) sees: one summary per conversation, `{conversation, kind: everyone\|all\|channel\|collision\|dm\|notices, name?, title, members, unread, mentions?, last_seq?, last_at?, last_from?, last_line?}`, newest activity first. `mentions` (absent when zero) is how many of the unread rows name the reader with `@`: the reader's current name and names saved at retirement in optional alias metadata (`retired_name`), and `@user` or `@you` for the person. Legacy aliases without that metadata cannot reconstruct the former name; `@name` is a name where it stands on its own (`@agent-1,` counts, `a@b.c` does not), compared without case. Every archived message belongs to one conversation by its destination: `everyone:<project>`, `all`, `channel:<id>`, `dm:<a>:<b>` (the sorted pair of ids, whoever the parties are) or `notices:<agent>` (what `agentd` itself told one agent); a topic message is kept in the topic log for its subscribers, not archived as a conversation. A project lists its broadcast, its channels, the reader's direct conversations with its agents and anything archived; without a project, every project's plus `all`. A reader sees rooms and broadcasts by membership and direct conversations and notices as a party; the person, in no room, sees every room, direct conversation and notice of the project (to read: a direct conversation between two agents has no destination for anyone else); a reader is every identity it has had, so a record retired into it by an identity repair or a resumed provider session keeps its conversations under their old ids, the furthest cursor any of those identities left counts, and `unread` counts archived messages past that cursor that none of those identities sent. At the archive's backfill (schema 21) the person's cursors start at every conversation's head, so what was queued before there were conversations is not the first unread count. |
| `history {conversation, before_seq?, limit?}` | `history {messages}` | the newest `limit` (default 100, at most 500) archived messages before `before_seq`, oldest first, each as `{seq, conversation, ...envelope, replies?}` where `replies` counts the thread under a root. The archive is written in the same transaction as the inbox rows, beside them, never instead of them; a topic message has no archive row (it has its topic log row; see `subscribe`); a broadcast nobody was live to receive is archived (it was said), a send a policy refused is not. Retained per conversation to 5,000 rows and to `[messages] retention` in `agentd.toml` when set, pruned on the minute tick within one budget of 1,000 rows for the whole tick across the age cutoff and every crowded conversation (`messages_pruned`), never a conversation's newest message, so its head and last line survive. Search uses FTS5 while the index answers and falls back to LIKE from the first index error until the next start rebuilds it. At the schema 21 upgrade the archive is backfilled once, by message id, from the rows still queued: nothing acknowledged before the upgrade exists anywhere, so history starts there. |
| `delivery {message}` | `delivery {message, recipients: Recipient[]}` | where one of the person's messages stands with each agent it was queued for, in queue order: `answered` (a reply from it, in any conversation), `delivered` (taken from its queue), `received` (a provider receipt names it), `shown` (its session read it without taking it), else why it waits: `unreachable` (nothing connects its session to AgentDocker), `waiting_for_prompt`, `waits_for_check_in` (a browser agent, or a session known by its key alone and away for hours), `waiting_to_take`, `held_for_typing` (its receiver holds input while the person types in its terminal), `paused`, `blocked`, `silent`, `ended` or `gone`. Recipients are recorded only for the person's own messages, beside the queue rows in the same transaction (the `deliveries` table, pruned with the archive); anyone else's message answers an empty list. Nothing here says a model read a message. Read-only; refused on the restricted endpoint |
| `thread {message, after_seq?, limit?}` | `thread {root, replies}`, `error(not_found)` | one archived message and the replies threaded under it: same conversation, oldest first, after `after_seq`, at most `limit` (default 100, at most 500); page by the last reply's seq. A reply whose `reply_to` names a message in another conversation is a plain message that keeps the id as data. |
| `mark_read {conversation, through, reader?}` | `ok`, `error(invalid)`, `error(identity_mismatch)` when `reader` names an agent | the reader read the conversation through an archived `seq` of it (`invalid` otherwise): the cursor moves forward, never back (a lower `through` is a no-op), later arrivals stay unread, and only the reader's own queued rows that the cursor covers are acknowledged, in the same transaction as the cursor and its `conversation_read {reader, conversation, through}` event. A reader whose queue has an owner (a bound controller, the daemon's own bridge) moves its cursor and drains nothing: the owner's receipts do that. Reading never closes a question: a question closes through an accepted answer, its asker's cancellation or its expiry; other agents' queues are untouched. |
| `search_messages {query, project?, reader?, before_seq?, limit?}` | `history {messages}` | archived messages whose text matches (FTS5, or LIKE without it), newest first, paginated by `before_seq`, among the conversations the reader (the person unless `reader` names an agent) could list with `conversations` for that project or everywhere: an agent searches only what it is in. |
| `channel_close {agent, channel, resolution?}` | `channel {channel}`, `error(identity_mismatch)` | the work is final; members are told and the journal says what it settled on |
| `channel_prune {project?, before_secs?}` | `pruned {removed}` | forget channels closed longer ago than that (a fortnight by default); an empty `project` prunes every project |
| `review_request {agent, channel, note?}` | `channel {channel}`, `error(identity_mismatch)` | ask the other members to look at this agent's work |
| `review {agent, channel, of?, verdict, note?}` | `channel {channel}`, `error(identity_mismatch)` | `approve`, `changes` or `comment` on another member's work; `of` defaults to the only other member; the reply carries the reviews so far |
| `code_verdict {agent, project?, branch, head, from?, verdict, findings?, note?}` | `code_verdict {verdict}`, `error(forbidden\|invalid\|not_found\|conflict\|unavailable\|backpressure\|storage_unavailable)` | a reviewer's word on a branch at the full commit it read (`head`; `from`, an earlier reviewed commit, when it read only what came after): `approve`, `changes` or `comment`, with `findings {p1, p2, p3}` and a note (at most 4000 characters). Given by an agent holding at least one role, in its own project, and only from its own session: over the socket the nearest process at or above the sender that is any live agent's own process (pid and recorded birth) or a provider's session process must be the reviewer's, so neither a stranger nor an agent the reviewer started gives its word (`forbidden`; the nearest session is a live agent's own process by pid and birth, or any provider's session process, which is the reviewer's only as its own process, the app-server its supervised bridge started, or the dedicated server `agentdocker codex` starts — a loopback server whose port the reviewer's terminal speaks to, a sibling under one launcher that is this daemon's companion CLI; a process on the way whose birth cannot be read refuses, and a Codex conversation whose tools run in a server several conversations share gives no verdict; the reviewer is named by its id once authenticated, and a name that resolves to nobody is refused there; its record must be unchanged — the same process, project and roles — when the verdict is committed after its checkout was read (`conflict`); never on the branch its checkout is on as git reads it now (`forbidden`; a checkout git cannot read is `unavailable`), and never on a branch the daemon made or witnessed it commit to, wherever its checkout is now: an `authorship` row for its id, or an id folded into it, on `refs/heads/<branch>` in the project (`forbidden`; see [the review gate](#channels)). Commits the daemon neither made nor witnessed are covered only by the current-checkout check, so authors and reviewers are still kept separate agents. The person gives one too, naming the project (`invalid` without one). The reviewer's roles at that moment are kept on it. An approval with a P1 finding, a short or upper-case commit id or a bad branch name is `invalid`. A `code_verdict` document by project, numbered one past the project's last (`seq`), which alone decides which word is a reviewer's latest; a project keeps 2000, forgetting the oldest that stand for nobody (comments, and words a reviewer has since replaced in every role a gate may be asked about) and never a word some role's gate still reads — when only those are left, the new verdict is `backpressure`. Emits `code_verdict_given` and a `review` journal line on the branch with `head_before`/`head_after`. CLI `verdict`, MCP `review_verdict` |
| `review_gate {agent?, project?, branch, head, role?}` | `review_gate {gate}`, `error(invalid\|not_found\|ambiguous\|storage_unavailable)` | whether `branch` may land at the full commit `head`: `approved` when a holder of `role` (`reviewer` when absent) or the person approved exactly that commit and nobody who counts asks for changes; `changes` when one does (each reviewer's latest approve or changes on the branch is their word; a comment is neither); `stale` when the latest approval read another commit; `missing` otherwise. A `head` that is not a full commit id is `invalid`; a project or agent that cannot be resolved is `not_found` or `ambiguous`; verdicts that cannot be read are `storage_unavailable`. Read-only. CLI `gate` (exits 1 unless approved; `--publish` sets the `agentdocker/review` commit status on GitHub), MCP `review_gate` |
| `overlap {project, since_seq?, agent?}` | `overlap {overlaps: Overlap[]}` | paths changed in more than one physical checkout of the project, from the newest 50,000 ledger rows: per path, each checkout with the agents attributed there, the count, the last change and its HEAD; with `agent`, only overlaps involving its checkout, and an empty `project` means its own |
| `changes {project, since_seq?, path?, agent?, limit?}` | `changes {changes: Change[]}` | the ledger, newest `limit` entries oldest first; `since_seq` is exclusive (`seq > since_seq`); `limit` defaults to 50 and is clamped to 1–10,000; empty, `.` and absolute checkout-root paths select all paths |
| `shutdown` | `ok` | the daemon exits after replying; managed agents get SIGTERM, as on Ctrl-C |
| `reload` | `ok`, `unavailable`, `backpressure`, `conflict`, `storage_unavailable` | with `[experimental] reload = true` in the daemon's `agentd.toml` (or `AGENTDOCKER_EXPERIMENTAL_RELOAD=1` on the daemon): reads the candidate executable's `--build-info` (`AGENTDOCKER_RELOAD_CANDIDATE`, else the release its managed installation has activated since it started, else its own executable; asked, and its successor started, as the daemon whatever the file is named: see [one executable](#one-executable)), refuses another host or an older state schema before offering anything, offers the coordinator transfer, hands the listening socket, the daemon lock and the container endpoint to the successor, and answers `ok` once the successor says it is serving, then exits without touching agents; a refusal, a death, or no readiness within 30 s answers `unavailable` with the reason and leaves this daemon serving; without the gate, refuses without changing the daemon or agents |
| `vacuum {force?}` | `vacuumed {before_bytes, after_bytes}` | SQLite `VACUUM` on the state database; nothing else is answered while it runs, so it is a `conflict` while sessions are live unless `force` |
| `send {from, to, kind, payload, reply_to?, links?}` | `sent {message, subscribers, recipient_readiness?}` or `error(backpressure\|forbidden)`, `error(identity_mismatch)` | `pause` and `resume` are reserved for committed project lifecycle requests and are refused here, including on the restricted endpoint; `to` is an agent ref or `role:<name>`, `project:<id prefix or absolute path>`, `topic:<name>`, or `all`; a topic message is kept in the topic log in the same transaction, for its sender's project when the sender is an agent working in one; an agent is refused `forbidden` for another project's agent, project or channel, and its `all` is its own project (see [Messaging](#messaging)); a full addressed inbox rejects the entire send without publishing or evicting previously accepted messages; each `recipient_readiness` detail names where the recipient stands as its `issue` and the one `fix?` the readiness decision gives (see [Derived activity](#derived-activity)) |
| `subscribe {agent?, topics?, after_seq?}` | stream of `message {message, seq?}` or `lagged {skipped: u64, replay?: true}`, `error(identity_mismatch)` with `agent` | replays unacknowledged inbox messages, then the kept messages of every topic the patterns match that the subscriber could have had live, oldest first — past the agent's cursor on each topic (from the oldest kept, on a topic it has never acknowledged), or past `after_seq` when given; at most 500 per topic and 5,000 in all, the newest, with one `lagged {skipped, replay: true}` before them counting what was left out (a live lag has no `replay`) — then streams live; nothing replayed is consumed. A topic message carries `seq`, its place in the topic log, replayed or live. Without `agent` and without `after_seq`, topics are live only (see [Messaging](#messaging)) |
| `inbox {agent, drain?}` | `messages`, `error(conflict)`, `error(identity_mismatch)` | snapshot; only explicit `drain: true` acknowledges its message IDs. A provider-blocked destructive read returns the legacy-compatible conflict shape without consuming input. |
| `ack_inbox {agent, messages: MessageId[]}` | `ok`, `error(identity_mismatch)` | idempotently acknowledge specific delivered messages; emits `inbox_acknowledged`. An id that is a kept topic message the agent could see moves its cursor on that topic to it instead, never back (`topic_cursors_advanced`); one of another project's moves nothing |
| `peek_input {agent}` | `messages` | look at a queue without consuming it or counting as a delivery, whoever consumes it: for a person or the app, never wired into a session's own reader; records no offer |
| `provider_inbox {agent, acknowledge?: MessageId[], token?}` | `messages`, `input_batch {agent, messages, uncertain, answers_routed}` | the exclusive consumer's read: acknowledge exact provider receipts (never mere queue acceptance) and return the same retained Send queue. For a managed Codex bridge session the answer is `messages`. For an agent with an input binding the `token` is required (`error(forbidden)` without it or with another) and the answer is `input_batch`, whose `uncertain` names the queued messages a legacy reader had been offered before the binding, so the controller reconciles them against the provider before enqueueing anything. During a provider block, return an empty offer without draining retained rows. Legacy consumers of either kind of owned queue are answered `input_owned`. |
| `bind_input {agent, provider: {process: {pid, started_at}, session, profile}, controller: {pid, started_at}, token, launch?: {executable, args, cwd, env}}` | `input_bound {agent, binding, resumed}`, `error(invalid\|forbidden\|conflict)` | make one external controller process the sole consumer of the agent's queued input, bound to one exact provider generation. The provider process must be the agent's registered pid and birth, the session its registered `session_id` label, the profile an absolute path, and both processes running; a managed Codex bridge session cannot be bound. The token is the controller's own (32 to 128 printable characters), made and kept before the first bind; the daemon stores only its SHA-256, which is visible on the agent record and in `input_bound`. The token tells one cooperating process of the same OS user from another, so a lost reply and a restart are recoverable; it is not a security boundary: whoever can open the socket already owns the state directory that holds the digest, so nothing is hidden from them that the record does not hold anyway. The same provider, controller and token bind again as a no-op, so a lost reply is recoverable; a different controller with the token resumes the binding once the bound controller has ended (`resumed: true`, `controller_generations` counts up, the queue is untouched) and is refused with `conflict` while it still runs; another token is `forbidden`; another provider generation is `conflict` even after that provider ended, until an explicit unbind. Queued messages a legacy reader had been offered become the binding's `uncertain` set. One thread of one profile has one queue: a bind for a session and profile another record is already bound to is `conflict` (two receivers would feed one persisted conversation); the route for a dead predecessor is `resume_input`. An optional `launch` descriptor (absolute `executable` and `cwd`, at most 64 `args` and 64 `env` entries, kept on the agent record in the open, so no secret belongs in it) is how the daemon starts the controller again once it has ended: it is fixed at the first bind, a resume sends the same one or none, and a different one is `invalid` until an unbind. Once a second the daemon checks the bound controller by pid and birth. When it is gone it emits `input_controller_ended`, marks the agent's `input_delivery` paused (a still-fresh `ready` heartbeat from the dead controller must not show delivery verified), and, while the provider process is still running, starts the descriptor in its own process group with stdin closed, stdout and stderr appended to `logs/<agent>.controller.log`, the daemon's environment plus the descriptor's and `AGENTDOCKER_HOME` set: `input_controller_launched` with the attempt number, or `input_controller_launch_failed`. Launches back off 0, 2, 4, 8 and 16 seconds from the last end, at most five per episode, after which `input_restarts_exhausted` is emitted and the binding stands, dead, until a controller binds by itself, a person retries (`retry_controller`) or somebody unbinds; a controller that stayed bound for a minute starts the count over. The started process binds itself with the same token, resuming the binding (the daemon never rebinds, never touches the provider generation, and restarts receivers, never provider sessions); one that has not bound within 30 seconds is sent SIGTERM, then SIGKILL five seconds later. The daemon holds a shared installation pin on the descriptor's executable for the binding's life, so release retention cannot remove the version a dead controller will be started from; a bind or resume whose release cannot be held answers `unavailable` while the release is being removed, `not_found` once it is gone, else `invalid`. The restart record (`binding.restart`: attempts, launched process, last end, exhausted) is on the agent record, so a replaced daemon continues the count. Emits `input_bound`. |
