| `upgrade_controller {agent, provider, controller, previous: {executable, args, cwd, env}, launch: {executable, args, cwd, env}, token}` | `input_bound {agent, binding, resumed: false}`, `error(invalid\|forbidden\|conflict\|transferring\|storage_unavailable)` | schema22: authenticate the existing binding and compare the exact provider/controller/previous launch. Only the executable may change. A live receiver requires two successful equal canonical executable lookups and a regular expected file; unavailable lookups refuse with `Conflict` and the captured path/error observations in `details`. Retain the new release pin and commit replacement intent before stopping the old receiver through normal supervision; a fence or failed write leaves it running. Pending inputs, token and receipts remain in the same ledger. A repeated accepted descriptor is a no-op, including after a lost reply; success accepts replacement intent, while CLI readiness polling proves a new receiver is running. |
| `resume_input {agent, predecessor, provider: {process, session, profile}, launch}` | `input_resumed {agent, retired, binding}`, `error(invalid\|conflict\|not_found)` | a provider session that came back as a new process registered as a new record (registration matches by pid and birth), while its thread's queue, binding and controller ledger sit on the record of the process that ended. The caller is the new record, live and unbound, and `provider` must be its own registered pid and birth and its `session_id` label; `predecessor` names the prior record, which must be of the same runtime, bound to the same thread and profile with the same canonical checkout, must be the only record so bound, and whose provider process, bound controller and any launched replacement must all be gone; a caller that already holds leases, sits in a channel or has recorded observations is refused rather than merged. One transaction: the prior record stays canonical and takes the caller's pid, birth, status and last_seen; its binding takes the new generation and this launch descriptor with the restart episode reset so the next tick launches; the token digest stays, what was `uncertain` stays and the caller's own legacy offers join it, nothing else is flagged (the controller's ledger proves what it never prepared); the caller's queued messages move across in `sent_at` order; the caller's id becomes an `identity_alias` of the prior id, the same document the offline repair writes, so every later request naming it resolves to the prior record. Emits `input_resumed`. A lost reply asked again arrives through the alias: the same predecessor, generation and descriptor get the same `input_resumed` with nothing recorded; anything else is `conflict`. |
| `retry_controller {agent}` | `ok`, `error(not_found\|invalid\|conflict)` | a person's retry after the daemon gave up starting a bound controller (`agentdocker provider retry <agent>`; the desktop app's **Restart message delivery** on the selected session, shown once the binding's restarts are exhausted): the restart episode starts over on the binding as it is, so the next tick launches the descriptor again; the queue, the token and the provider generation are untouched. `invalid` for a binding without a launch descriptor (its controller is started by the runtime's own hook), `conflict` while the bound controller or a launched one is running. Emits `input_restarts_reset`. |
| `unbind_input {agent, token?, force?}` | `ok`, `error(forbidden\|conflict)` | release a binding with its token, or by `force` only once the bound controller process has ended; a running controller is `conflict`. The queue stays; a process the daemon launched that has not bound yet is sent SIGTERM, and the installation pin is dropped. Emits `input_unbound`. |
| `me {workdir?}` | `agent` | register the person at the keyboard as the agent `user`, runtime `human`, or return the one already registered; `workdir` moves them to that project. No pid, so liveness never expires it |
| `ask {from, to, question, timeout_secs?}` | `answer {message, from, text}` or `error(timeout/cancelled)`, `error(identity_mismatch)` | sends a `question` message and waits for the exact answer confirmed by its committed closure; timeout defaults to 300 s and is clamped to 1–86,400. While it waits, an answer to its question is held: queued durably but shown to no read of the asker's queue, and nothing queued behind it is shown either, so the queue keeps its order for an answer that comes back to it. On hand-over the daemon does not take its written reply for a receipt: the answer is released to the queue recorded as offered (`legacy_offers`, and `binding.uncertain` for a bound asker, durable), so a controller settles it against the provider's own record of the tool call before delivering or acknowledging it, and a legacy reader gets it again rather than never. An `ask` that ends without the answer (timeout, cancel, a client that went away) releases it to the queue. |
| `open_secret_review {agent, owner, recipient, request: {thread, turn, fields}, token}` | `secret_review {review}`, `error(invalid\|forbidden\|conflict)` | Volatile local broker for an exact live managed Codex bridge generation and its registered thread, addressed to a human. At most 32 routes/one per agent, eight fields/32 KiB prompt text. Capability is 64 hex characters, hashed only in RAM; it is separate from the public daemon-generated route ID. Five-minute monotonic lifetime and 15-second owner heartbeat. No ordinary message, stored question or durable event; emits live-only `secret_review_changed` with opaque ID/agent only. Restricted endpoints refuse it. Managed-provider routing, a durable metadata fence and masked desktop entry are experimental, behind `[experimental] secret_input = true` in `agentd.toml` (the bridge asks the daemon; `AGENTDOCKER_EXPERIMENTAL_SECRET_INPUT=1` on the bridge also turns it on); without it, for native sessions and on unsupported consoles the request is refused. Overlapping reviews reject only the new request, preserving the first route and held queue. |
| `secret_reviews {recipient}` | `secret_reviews {reviews}` | Human-recipient metadata only; submitted answers are omitted. Expired, replaced or ended owners are removed before observation. |
| `answer_secret_review {from, review, answers, retention_acknowledged}` | `ok`, `error(invalid\|forbidden\|conflict\|not_found)`, `error(identity_mismatch)` | Exact human recipient, explicit provider-retention acknowledgment and every field in the mixed bundle required. Up to 16,000 bytes/field and 64 KiB total; duplicate IDs/unknown fields/partial bundles refused. Answers exist only in bounded memory and debug/errors are redacted. Already-submitted answers cannot be replaced or automatically resubmitted. |
| `cancel_secret_review {from, review}` | `ok`, `error(forbidden)`, `error(identity_mismatch)` | Recipient removes the volatile route/answer; already-closed IDs are idempotent. Cannot recall an answer already taken. |
| `poll_secret_review {agent, owner, review, token, close}` | `secret_reply {reply: {state: waiting\|answered\|closed, answers?}}` | Exact owner generation/capability required. Pending poll renews heartbeat. Answer is removed before the socket reply; a lost reply is not replayable. `close` cancels without returning values. Restart/replacement loses all routes; no migration or persisted payload. The local socket's same-OS-user trust boundary is unchanged; capability protects against accidental cross-route consumption, not a malicious same-user process. |
| `post_question {from, to, question, presentation?, timeout_secs?}` | `sent`, `error(identity_mismatch)` | creates the same durable question and returns its ID immediately; optional structured command/choice controls must exactly match the full fallback text; answers use the shared inbox; CLI `ask --no-wait` |
| `cancel_question {agent, message}` | `ok`, `error(identity_mismatch)` | only the asker closes an open question; repeats are no-ops; accepted answers and original inbox messages remain; emits `question_cancelled` and finishes an active ask with `error(cancelled)`; CLI `cancel-question` |
| `answer {from?, message, text}` | `sent`, `error(identity_mismatch)` when `from` names an agent | an addressed recipient may reply once to an unexpired question by its id, including after restart; atomically closes its route; `from` defaults to `user`; an unrelated peer comment in Send remains an ordinary message and does not close the question. Emits `answer_routed {question, answer, route: queue}` at once when no `ask` is waiting, else `tool_result` or `queue` once the wait settles: one answer travels one way. |
| `questions {agent?}` | `questions {questions: Question[]}` | what is still waiting, newest first; `agent` narrows to the ones put to that agent |
| `claim {agent, resource, mode?, amount?, ttl_secs?, note?, wait_secs?, priority?, automatic?}` | `lease`, `error(conflict)`, `error(deadlock)`, `error(forbidden)` or `error(paused)`, `error(identity_mismatch)` | a `conflict` says by whom, by name: `<resource> is held by <name> (<id>; <mode> on <resource>)`, with the leases in `details.held_by`; `amount` spends a `quota:` resource and is ignored for every other kind; policy is checked once, before the first attempt; `path:` uses canonical physical absolute keys; `file:` is a validated checkout alias; conflict `details.held_by` lists the blocking leases; `wait_secs` (max 600) queues and retries when it is this waiter's turn; `priority` (-9 to 9, 0 when absent; anything else is `invalid`) orders the queue, higher first and then by arrival; a wait that would close a cycle breaks it at its victim (the lowest priority, then the youngest, the newcomer counting as the youngest of all): a newcomer chosen is refused at once, an older wait chosen is ended and its claim answers `deadlock`, both with `details.cycle`; `automatic` marks a lease an adapter took for the edit in hand rather than one the agent asked for — the hooks adapter's per-file lease — and a holder's re-claim of its own lease renews it, deliberate if either claim was |
| `activity {agent?, project?, all?}` | `activity {activity: AgentActivity[]}` | `unknown`, `starting`, `working`, `blocked {resource, held_by, since}`, `waiting {since, reason?, judged?}` (stopped for the person in its own terminal; `judged {permille}` when the opt-in [screen judge](#the-screen-judge-opt-in) read it from a quiet screen rather than its adapter reporting it, with `reason` `permission`, `question` or `draft`), `idle`, `finished`; waiting and blocked first. Presence or configuration alone never proves idle. `queued_inputs` is the durable inbox count and `awaiting_receipt` how many of those no receipt from the current process names (both absent on older daemons). |
| `role {agent, role?}` | `agent`, `error(invalid)` | give a live agent one role in place of whatever it held, or take its roles away with no `role`: one word of lowercase letters, digits and hyphens (at most forty), kept in the `role` label on the record and committed with a `role_set {agent, project?, role?}` event; the same role again changes nothing; a finished agent has none. |
| `role_assign {from, agent, add, remove}` | `agent`, `error(forbidden\|invalid\|not_found)`, `error(identity_mismatch)` | the person's (`forbidden` for an agent, and for any request sent from inside an agent's session — see the host socket's boundary): give a live agent roles from its project's palette and take others away; the set is kept sorted in the `role` label (`reviewer,tester`) and said as `role_set` with the joined set. A role the palette lacks is `invalid` and names the palette. Each role taken up or put down is said in its room by `agentd`. CLI `role`. |
| `rename {agent, name}` | `agent`, `error(invalid\|name_taken\|not_found)` | give a live agent the name a person chose: one to sixty-four characters with no control characters or surrounding space, and not `project`, `all`, `user` or anything starting `project:`, `topic:` or `role:` (those address something else); unique among live agents (`name_taken`); the generated-name label is dropped so the name reads as chosen, the id and every reference by id are unchanged, and the change is committed with an `agent_renamed {agent, project?, name}` event; the same name again changes nothing; a finished agent keeps its name. Refused on the restricted endpoint. CLI `rename`. |
| `report_activity {agent, observation: {activity, observed_at, reason?, detail?, source?}}` | `ok`, `error(invalid)`, `error(identity_mismatch)` | explicit `working`, `idle` or `waiting` observation (`waiting`: the provider stopped for the person — a permission prompt or question dialog in its terminal; optional `reason` `permission` or `question` says which, from the provider's own event kind and never its words, kept on the record and carried as `Activity::Waiting.reason`; an unknown reason reads as `other`, and a daemon predating the field ignores it; `draft` is the screen judge's alone and no adapter sends it; optional `detail` is what the wait is about in one line, `<Tool>: <what>` or a tool alone, kept only on a wait and only as `wait::wait_detail` leaves it, whoever sent it — the hooks adapter's line passes the same function, so a client sending a raw command gets exactly the hook's treatment: the first line with text, no control or invisible formatting characters, none at all when that whole line reads like a secret; what follows a file tool's name (`Edit`, `Write`, `MultiEdit`, `Read`, `NotebookEdit`) or `WebFetch`'s is kept up to a flag that takes a credential or a `user:password@host`, every address reduced to scheme, host and path; what follows any other tool is dropped, its name kept alone — a search's query or pattern (`Grep`, `Glob`, `WebSearch`) is free text, where a short plain password slips past every check, and an MCP or unknown tool's argument is unknown — except a shell tool's (`Bash`, `PowerShell`), which, like a line with no tool, is a command, reduced by `wait::command_detail` as an allowlist with arguments left out by default: nothing for a command that reads like a secret or names a credential word, else its first line's program by name (directory and leading `NAME=value` assignments dropped) and nothing more, except that `cargo`, `git`, `go`, `npm`, `pnpm` and `yarn` show the one word directly following when it is exactly one of their own subcommands, written out in `wait::SUBCOMMANDS` (`cargo run`, `git push`, `npm run`), and nothing after it, ever — an argument in a position only the program knows (`smbclient //server/share <password>`, `isql <dsn> <user> <password>`) or a value a subcommand passes on (`cargo run <args>`, `npm run <script> <args>`, `go run <file> <args>`, a `make` target's `VAR=…`) is never shown, since neither a word's shape nor its position can tell a subcommand from a value; at most 120 characters — and carried as `Activity::Waiting.detail`; a wait that sends no detail, for the same reason within 60 seconds of a detailed one, keeps that one's detail, since it is the same prompt said again); rejects future/stale reports, ignores older/equal reports, expires after five minutes (`waiting` after twelve hours, since a person may be away); for an agent whose hooks have run in its current process, later contact (its MCP server polling for messages) never supersedes a report, and past expiry its activity is unknown rather than working; commits record and `agent_activity_reported` event together. An observation naming a `source` is refused (`invalid`): `source: herdr` marks one the daemon made itself from herdr and `source: codex_server` one it made from Codex's shared app-server (see [Derived activity](#derived-activity)); either lasts five minutes whatever it says and, while it lasts, stands over contact as a hook report does, and a current `codex_server` observation keeps herdr's off the record. A source a reader does not know reads as another source (`other`). CLI `report-activity --as`, MCP `report_activity`, and lifecycle hooks share this operation. |
| `report_adapter {agent, adapter, contact: {process_started_at, observed_at}}` | `ok`, `error(invalid)`, `error(identity_mismatch)` | recent `mcp` or `hooks` contact, separate from activity and input delivery. Requires a live matching process generation, a nonfuture observation younger than five minutes and a supported hook runtime. Coalesces observations less than 30 seconds apart per adapter/generation; atomically commits the agent and `adapter_contact_reported` event. The hidden CLI is `report-adapter --as AGENT --process-started-at RFC3339_TIMESTAMP mcp\|hooks`. Adapter diagnostics add at most a 250 ms wait to a throttled request; failure or an older daemon does not refuse the provider operation. |
| `report_input {agent, process_started_at, observed_at, report, token?}` | `ok`, `error(invalid\|forbidden)` | adapter-only ready/paused or received evidence; while an input binding stands the bound controller's `token` is required and its reports are accepted for any runtime, so nobody else can report readiness or a receipt for that queue; matches the live process birth and provider, rejects stale/future observations, older reports and conflicting equal-timestamp observations, validates bounded receipt data and records only exact IDs still queued for that agent; repeated/unknown IDs are no-ops, and commits the agent plus `input_delivery_reported` before the adapter's separate ACK. Paused reports retain a nonblank plain reason of at most 2,048 bytes; adapters use the outer error context, not the full provider error chain. Ready and receipts for still-queued input clear the pause and retain receipt history. An already-ACKed receipt is a no-op even if it exactly matches the latest receipt: it cannot prove a later pause has recovered. `held_for_typing {until}` says the receiver is holding its next input while the person types in its terminal ([Holding input while the person types](#holding-input-while-the-person-types)): it is accepted only when `until` is after `observed_at` and at most one quiet window (`typing::QUIET`) after it, records `typing_until` on the delivery and commits `input_held_for_typing` instead of `input_delivery_reported`; a receipt or a pause ends the hold, a ready heartbeat leaves it standing, and it lapses at its own time. It is no evidence that a pause is over. CLI `report-input --as --process-started-at --json` is hidden from normal help. |
| `report_provider {agent, process_started_at, observed_at, report}` | `ok`, `error(invalid/conflict)`, `error(identity_mismatch)` | generation-bound, fresh structured `blocked {issue}` or `recovered {blocked_at}` report. Persists the record and `provider_availability_reported` atomically; identical blocks are coalesced. No receipt or task completion is implied. CLI `provider report AGENT --kind KIND`; MCP `report_provider_status` binds the caller. |
| `resume_provider {agent, blocked_at}` | `ok`, `error(conflict)` | explicit user recovery for the exact observed block; refuses stale views and backward clock changes. Clears only that block, preserving queue IDs, receipts, questions and uncertain-input protection. CLI `provider resume AGENT`; desktop **Resume delivery**. |
| `delivery_queue {agent}` | `messages`, `input_waiting {agent, blocked_by, availability, queued}`, `input_owned {agent, owner, controller?, since?}`, `error(identity_mismatch)` | non-destructive adapter read of the shared Send queue, gated by local or explicitly shared provider limits. An owned queue, a managed Codex bridge session's or a bound controller's, answers `input_owned` rather than an error: there is nothing for a legacy reader to deliver. Every legacy offer is remembered per message (first time only, on the agent record) for a controller that binds later, and while a binding stands the offered messages join its `uncertain` set at once. `inbox` (draining or not), `ack_inbox` and `subscribe` on a bound queue answer `input_owned` the same way, except that `ack_inbox` is still accepted for exactly the messages named `uncertain` by the binding, a legacy reader finishing its in-flight delivery. A non-draining read is refused on a bound queue because it may be the session's own reader putting the text in front of the model, which the daemon cannot tell from a person, and an exposure that lands between the controller's snapshot and its enqueue could never be reported to the controller in time; a person or the app looks with `peek_input`. A managed bridge session keeps its non-draining `inbox` read for the app, and any legacy offer recorded while unbound joins a later binding's `uncertain` set. While blocked, `delivery_queue` returns `input_waiting`; legacy `inbox {drain: true}` returns `error(conflict)` and retains all messages. Administrative non-draining `inbox` reads remain available. The exclusive `provider_inbox` path acknowledges proven receipts first, then returns an empty `messages` offer while blocked, retaining pending input. A new adapter connected to a pre-schema18 daemon falls back to its legacy non-destructive Inbox only on an explicit unknown-operation response; other errors never bypass gating. Proven receipts can still be acknowledged. |
| `waiting` | `waiting {waiting: Waiter[]}` | the claim queue in the order it is served: higher `priority` first, then oldest; a `Waiter` is `{ticket, agent, resource, mode, since, priority}` (`priority` 0 from a daemon predating it) |
| `task_create {from, project?, board?, lane?, column?, title, acceptance?, description?, priority?, size?, due?, external_blocker?, parent?, blocked_by?, tags?, links?}` | `task {task}`, `error(invalid\|not_found\|ambiguous)`, `error(identity_mismatch)` | a card on a board: the project's main board unless `board` names another (slug or name), in `lane` (slug, name or category; `review` is the first Review lane), else the first lane of `column`, else the board's first lane, ranked last in it. A title (1–200 characters), what done means (`acceptance`, at most 4000), a `description` (at most 16,000), `priority` (`high`, `normal`, `low`; normal when absent), `size` (`s`, `m`, `l`), `due` (a date), `external_blocker` (what off the board it waits on, at most 500), `parent` (the card it is part of) and `blocked_by` (up to 16 cards that must be done first; both by key, id or unique prefix, and a loop through either is `invalid`), and up to 8 `tags` (32 characters each). `project` is an id, root or unique prefix, the caller's own when absent. A card is a `task` document, its id twelve hex characters, and gets the next display key from its project's main board (`AD-12`; the prefix is the project name's initials). `task` is `{id, key, project, board?, title, acceptance, description, column, lane?, rank, assignee?, priority, size?, due?, external_blocker, parent?, blocked_by, tags, links, created_by, created_at, updated_at, archived_at?}`; `column` is the lane's category (`backlog`, `ready`, `in_progress`, `review`, `blocked`, `done`, `cancelled`), and a card from before lanes, with no `lane`, sits in the first lane of its column. Its history gets a `created` entry. Emits `task_created`. |
| `task_create_many {from, project?, board?, lane?, tags?, cards: BatchCard[]}` | `task_batch {tasks, refs}`, `error(invalid)`, `error(identity_mismatch)` | up to 50 cards in one commit, all or none: each `{ref?, title, acceptance?, description?, lane?, priority?, size?, due?, external_blocker?, parent?, blocked_by?, tags?, links?}`, where `parent` and `blocked_by` name another card of the batch by its `ref` or a card already on the board. `lane` and `tags` apply to every card that does not say otherwise. `invalid` lists every problem at once; `refs` maps each ref to the key it got. One `task_created` per card. |
| `task_get {task}` | `task_detail {task, board, comments, history, blockers, children, lease?}`, `error(not_found\|ambiguous)` | one card in full, by key, id or unique prefix: its board's lanes, its comments (oldest first, replies one level under the comment they answer, a deleted one kept in place without its words), its history (`task_entry`: created, moved, edited fields, assigned, pulled, commented, archived, unarchived, with who and when), the cards it waits on and those that are part of it, and the lease holding it. A read. |
| `task_pull {agent, task?, take_over_from?, project?, board?}` | `task_detail {…, lease}`, `nothing_ready {project, board, source, total, held, blocked}`, `error(conflict\|paused\|invalid\|not_found\|ambiguous\|storage_unavailable)`, `error(identity_mismatch)` | a running agent takes a Ready card nobody holds: it becomes theirs, in the board's pull target (`in_progress` on the classic board), and the pull takes an exclusive `task:<id>` lease (four hours, the title as its note) in the same commit as the card — the lease is the holding, so `leases` shows who has which card, `renew` extends it, an exit or `release` ends it, and a second claimant is refused like any lease. Under the daemon's state lock, so two agents never get one card: the second is told who holds it (`conflict`, `details.assignee`, `details.column`, `details.hold: live`). A pull of one's own held card renews the lease (a retry after an uncertain reply changes nothing). A card in `in_progress` or `review` whose holder's lease has lapsed (expired, released, or its agent exited) is **not** taken by a plain pull: `conflict` with `details.hold: lapsed` and the holder to name. Recovery is explicit: `take_over_from` names the holder the caller expects to take it from (itself, re-taking its own lapsed hold); the card passes where it sits and the event says `from` whom, and the pull is refused (`conflict`) when the card names somebody else, sits in another column, or that hold is live after all. A lease on the card held by hand by somebody the card does not name refuses any pull (`conflict`, `details.held_by`). A Backlog or Done card is `conflict`; the person does not pull (`invalid`), they assign with `task_update`; a paused project refuses the pull like any lease (`paused`). `task` is an id or a unique prefix, looked up as one (two rows read, not the board). Emits `lease_claimed` (or `lease_renewed`) then `task_pulled {task, project, agent, from?, lease}`.. With no `task` named it pulls the next card: the first in the board's pull source (`ready` on the classic board), in board order, that is free or already the caller's and waits on nothing — no outside blocker, no open card in `blocked_by` (open is neither done, cancelled nor archived); finding none is `nothing_ready`, not an error, with how many cards the source holds, how many are held by others and how many wait. A pull of one's own card again renews its lease. |
| `task_move {agent, task, to?, column?, after?, before?, comment?}` | `task {task}`, `error(forbidden\|invalid\|storage_unavailable)`, `error(identity_mismatch)` | to a lane of the card's board: `to` by slug, name or category, or `column` (the older form) for that category's first lane; `after` or `before` another card in that lane places it there (a fractional rank between its neighbours, so no other card is rewritten), else it goes last; a move within its lane only reorders. `comment` says why, on the card, in the same commit. A move to where it already is changes nothing and emits nothing. An agent moves only a card it holds with a live *exclusive* lease (a shared claim on the card, made by hand, is no hold) — a lapsed hold is `forbidden` with `details.hold: lapsed`, and the card is pulled again by name first; the person needs no hold. A move that ends the holding — back to a Backlog or Ready lane, or to Done or Cancelled — releases the card's leases in the same commit (`lease_released` follows `task_moved`); `done` keeps the assignee for the record. Otherwise: the card's assignee moves it, and the person moves any; a card the person moves back to a waiting lane is released for the next taker. A lane's `owner` and `wip` are the board's to show, not refusals. When the person moves a card an agent holds, the agent is told by a `task` message linking the card. Its history gets a `moved` entry. Emits `task_moved {…, lane}`. |
| `task_update {agent, task, title?, acceptance?, description?, priority?, size?, due?, external_blocker?, parent?, blocked_by?, tags?, assignee?, links?, expected_updated_at?}` | `task {task}`, `error(forbidden\|invalid\|conflict\|storage_unavailable)`, `error(identity_mismatch)` | an agent edits only a card it holds with a live lease (`forbidden`, `details.hold: lapsed` otherwise). A hand by the person is a confirmed reassignment: the old holder's lease ends and, for a running agent, the new holder's `task:<id>` lease is taken in the same commit (`lease_released`/`lease_claimed` follow `task_updated`); handed to an agent that is not running, or taken away (`assignee: ""`), the card has no hold until somebody pulls it by name. Otherwise: the person edits any card's words and hands it to an agent (`assignee` an id, name or prefix; `""` takes it away); an agent edits only the words of a card it holds. Emits `task_updated`.. Each field left out stays; `size`, `due` or `parent` given empty clears it, and `links`, `tags` and `blocked_by` replace the card's whole. With `expected_updated_at`, the card's `updated_at` as the editor read it, an edit to a card changed since is `conflict` and writes nothing, so an edit made against an older reading never puts back what somebody else changed. The app's card editor sends only the fields the person changed since it took the card or last saved it, so a save never touches a field somebody else changed while the person left it alone. The same limits as `task_create`; a `parent` or `blocked_by` that closes a loop is `invalid`. The history gets one `edited` entry naming the fields that changed, and the event says them (`fields`). A card the person hands to an agent is said to that agent by a `task` message linking it. |
| `task_archive {agent, task}` | `ok`, `error(forbidden\|storage_unavailable)`, `error(identity_mismatch)` | releases the card's leases in the same commit; an agent archives only a card it holds with a live lease. off the board, kept for the record: the person's to do, or the assignee's for a card in `done`. Archiving twice is `ok`. Emits `task_archived`. |
| `task_unarchive {agent, task}` | `task {task}`, `error(forbidden\|not_found)`, `error(identity_mismatch)` | the person's to do: back on its board where it was, with no holder. Emits `task_unarchived`. |
| `task_archive_lane {from, project?, board?, lane}` | `archived {count}`, `error(forbidden\|invalid)`, `error(identity_mismatch)` | the person's to do: every card in one lane off the board in one commit (the end of a sprint's Done), their leases released. One `task_archived` per card. |
| `task_comment {from, task, body, reply_to?}` | `comment {comment}`, `error(invalid\|not_found)`, `error(identity_mismatch)` | anybody says something on a card (1–4000 characters), or answers a comment on it, one level deep. Stored as a `task_comment` document; the history gets a `commented` entry. When the person comments on a card an agent holds, the agent is told by a `task` message linking the card. Emits `task_commented`. |
| `task_comment_edit {from, comment, body}` | `comment {comment}`, `error(forbidden\|invalid\|not_found)`, `error(identity_mismatch)` | its author rewrites a comment; it says it was edited. Emits `task_comment_changed`. |
| `task_comment_delete {from, comment}` | `ok`, `error(forbidden\|not_found)`, `error(identity_mismatch)` | its author or the person deletes a comment; its place in the thread stays, without its words, so replies keep their parent. Emits `task_comment_changed {deleted: true}`. |
| `boards {project?}` | `boards {boards: Board[]}` | a project's boards, its main board first, made from the classic lanes the first time it is asked for (whose slugs are the old column names, so no card moves). A `board` is `{project, slug, name, template, lanes: [{slug, name, category, owner, wip?}], pull_source, pull_target, key_prefix, last_key, created_at, updated_at}`; `owner` is `any`, `human`, `agent` or `role:<name>`, whose turn a card in that lane is. A read. |
| `board_create {from, project?, name, template?}` | `board {board}`, `error(forbidden\|invalid)`, `error(identity_mismatch)` | the person's to do: a board from a template, `classic` (Backlog, Ready, In progress, Review, Done), `agents` (Backlog, Approved, Analyst, In progress, Testing, UAT, Done, Blocked, Cancelled, with roles in Analyst and Testing) or `simple` (To do, Doing, Done). Its slug comes from its name. Emits `board_changed`. |
| `board_update {from, project?, board, name?, lanes?, pull_source?, pull_target?}` | `board {board}`, `error(forbidden\|invalid)`, `error(identity_mismatch)` | the person's to do: rename a board, or reshape its lanes (1–16, unique slugs of at most 32 characters, names of at most 40, at least one lane per category its cards are in) and which lanes a pull takes from and into. A card in a lane that goes sits in the first lane of its category. Emits `board_changed`. |
| `tasks {project?, board?, lane?, column?, assignee?, agent?, open?, query?, tag?, archived?, offset?, limit?}` | `tasks {tasks: Task[], more}`, `error(invalid\|not_found\|ambiguous\|storage_unavailable)` | one page of the board, by lane category from Backlog to Cancelled and in board order (rank, then age) within a lane, from `offset`, at most `limit` cards (1–500; 100 by default) and within a page's byte budget (768 KiB of serialised cards; a page holds at least one card whatever its size), with `more` when the board goes on past them — the next page starts at `offset + tasks.len()`, so every card is reachable without a mutation. Read as a page from the store, so a board of long cards never fills a frame or holds the lock; archived cards only when asked; every project's when none is named. A read: served during a coordinator transfer.. The filters narrow it: a `board`, a `lane` (slug, name or category), whose it is (`assignee`: an agent, `me` with `agent` naming the caller, `mine`, or `none`), only `open` cards (not done or cancelled), words in its key, title or description (`query`), a `tag`. `mine` is the person's cards together with those held by agents the person launched or started (`AgentRecord::persons`: the person's own record, or a record carrying `launched_by=user` — see `run` and `register`; a holder looked up by its canonical record, and one the registry no longer has, or that nobody says the person launched, is not counted) when `agent` names the person or nobody; when `agent` names an agent it is that agent's own cards, as `me` (an `agent` that names nobody known is `not_found`). |
| `task_search {query, project?, archived?, limit?}` | `tasks {tasks, more: false}`, `error(invalid)` | cards that answer some words, best first (core `search`): a key (`AD-12`) or id answers only its card; otherwise every word asked must match a word on the card — whole, as a prefix, inside, or one typing slip away (two in a word of eight letters or more) — the title counting most, then tags, then the key, then the description, acceptance and outside blocker. Open cards before ended ones among equals. At least two characters; every project's cards when none is named; archived ones only when asked; at most `limit` (20 by default, 100 at most). A read. |
| `task_start {from, task, runtime, args?, workdir?}` | `agent {agent}`, `error(forbidden\|conflict\|not_found\|invalid\|unavailable)`, `error(identity_mismatch)` | the person's to do: start an agent of `runtime` (as `runtimes` names it) on a card nobody holds that waits on nothing — what a pull would take: no outside blocker, no open card in `blocked_by` — in `workdir` or a checkout of the card's project the daemon knows, in its own worktree in a git project. Before launch, Codex is wrapped in the managed input bridge and Claude Code receives its channel configuration, using the CLI beside the loaded daemon. Unsupported idle delivery or a missing sibling CLI is `unavailable`; incompatible provider arguments are `invalid`, before any agent or card assignment. Provider consent and permissions still apply. Once it runs, the card is handed to it in one commit — moved into the board's pull target unless it is already being worked on, held by the agent's `task:<id>` lease — and the agent is told, by a `task` message linking the card, what to do with it. The agent, started by the person by hand or by a rule of theirs, carries `launched_by=user`. A card already being started on, held, or waiting is `conflict` (`details.blocked_by` names the open cards it waits on); readiness is checked again after the runtime inventory, before the launch, and after it, before the hand-over (an agent whose card was taken or blocked meanwhile is told to pull another). The card stays free if the launch fails. |
| `rules {project?, board?}` | `rules {rules: Rule[]}` | a project's board rules, or one board's. A `rule` is `{id, project, board, name, trigger, action, enabled, created_by, created_at, updated_at}`: `trigger` is `{kind: enter, lane}` (a card filed or moved into the lane, by anyone but a rule) or `{kind: idle, lane, days}` (a card in the lane for 1–365 days); `action` is `{kind: comment, text}`, `{kind: move, lane}`, `{kind: archive}` or `{kind: start, runtime, args?, max}`. A read. |
| `rule_create {from, project?, board?, name, trigger, action}` | `rule {rule}`, `error(forbidden\|invalid)`, `error(identity_mismatch)` | the person's to do; at most 32 rules a board. Lanes are named by slug, name or category and stored as slugs. A rule acts as `rule:<name>` on the card's comments and history, emits `rule_fired {project, rule, task}` beside what it did, and marks the card (`fired`) so it acts once while the card stays in that lane; a card's stay starts when it comes into a lane (`lane_since`). Enter rules act at once, in their own commits after the move or filing; a rule's own move sets off no rule. Idle rules, and start rules catching up on cards that came in while they were full, act on the minute's sweep (at most 50 actions a minute). A start rule runs at most `max` (1–8) of its agents at once, counted by the `agentdocker.rule` label, and only on cards `task_start` would start on (nobody's, still to do, waiting on nothing); a waiting card is started on by the sweep once what it waits on clears. Emits `rule_changed`. |
| `rule_update {from, rule, name?, trigger?, action?, enabled?}` / `rule_delete {from, rule}` | `rule {rule}` / `ok`, `error(identity_mismatch)` | the person's to do: change a rule or turn it off or on; delete it. Emit `rule_changed` (`deleted` on a delete). |
| `tags {project?}` | `tags {tags: Tag[]}` | a project's tags, each `{project, name, color, created_by, created_at, updated_at}`, `color` one of `gray`, `red`, `orange`, `yellow`, `green`, `teal`, `blue`, `purple`. At most 200 a project. A card carries only the project's tags: the person's new words become tags with the card, coloured round the palette; an agent's unknown tag refuses the filing or edit (`invalid`, `details.tags` the catalog) until somebody makes it. A read. |
| `tag_save {from, project?, name, color?, rename?}` / `tag_delete {from, project?, name}` | `tag {tag}` / `ok`, `error(identity_mismatch)` | make a tag (anybody), recolour it, or (the person) rename it: a rename follows it onto every card that has it, in one commit. Deleting a tag is the person's and takes it off every card. Emit `tag_changed {project, name, by, renamed_to?, deleted}`. |
| `role_catalog {project?}` | `role_catalog {roles: RoleDef[]}` | a project's roles, each `{project, role, name, color, description?, tools?, created_by?, created_at, updated_at}`, `tools` the MCP tools its holders are offered (absent, every tool; see [Roles](#messaging)), `color` one of Claude Code's eight (`red`, `blue`, `green`, `yellow`, `purple`, `orange`, `pink`, `cyan`): the five every project offers (`lead`, `implementer`, `reviewer`, `explorer`, `tester`) as the person left them, then the person's own in the order made. The five are built in and stored only once the person changes one, so a project that never uses roles stores nothing; reading changes nothing. A role is an address (`role:reviewer`) and is never given to an agent automatically. |
| `role_save {from, project?, role?, name, color?, description?, tools?}` / `role_delete {from, project?, role}` | `role_def {role}` / `ok`, `error(identity_mismatch)` | the person's (`forbidden` for an agent): with `role`, rename, recolour or describe it (the address never changes); without, make a role whose address is made from `name` (`conflict` when the project has it; a built-in one taken out comes back). `tools` names the MCP tools its holders are offered, kept sorted with repeats dropped; an empty list lifts the limit, and absent leaves it as it was. A tool's name is one to 64 lowercase letters, digits and underscores, at most 128 a role (`invalid` otherwise); which tools exist is the MCP server's to know, not the daemon's. A name is one to 32 characters, a description one line of at most 120; at most 24 roles a project, the five built in counted whether changed, taken out or not. A store that cannot be read is `storage_unavailable`, never the bare five. Deleting a built-in role keeps it as `removed` so it stays out; the person's own is deleted. Emit `role_changed {project, role, by, deleted, tools?}`, `tools` present when the save changed them (empty when it lifted the limit). |
| `plans {project?, archived?}` / `plan_get {plan}` | `plans {plans}` / `plan {plan, tasks}` | a project's plans — specs in Markdown that cards carry out — newest change first, without their bodies, archived ones only when asked; one plan in full with the open cards whose `plan` names it. A `plan` is `{id, project, title, body, status, created_by, created_at, updated_at}`, `status` `draft`, `active`, `done` or `archived`. Reads. |
| `memory_add {from, project?, text, links?, supersedes?, standing?, forget_after?, roles?}` | `memory {memory}`, `error(invalid\|not_found\|ambiguous)`, `error(identity_mismatch)` | keep a fact for a project's agents (`project`, else the author's own): at most 2048 characters and 8 links, refused when it reads like a secret; `supersedes` names a memory of the same project it replaces; `standing` says it first in the profile; after `forget_after` (a time later than now, else `invalid`) it is no longer said; `roles` says it only to their holders and the person, each a role of the project's or `invalid`. At most 1000 per project |
| `memory_search {from?, project?, query, limit?}` / `memory_profile {from?, project?, max_chars?}` | `memories {memories}` | a project's current memories as `from` reads them (the person all; an agent those for everyone and for its roles; nobody named, those for everyone; none another the reader can see supersedes, none past its `forget_after`) that hold the query's words whole, the best first by the words' inverse document frequency, a newer one winning a tie and then the lower id (`limit` 1–100, 20 by default); or, within `max_chars` (4000 by default), the standing ones oldest first and then the others newest first. `from` names the project when `project` does not |
| `memory_forget {from, memory}` | `ok`, `error(forbidden\|not_found)`, `error(identity_mismatch)` | forget a memory for good, by id or unique prefix: its author's, from the memory's own project, or the person's. A memory it superseded is said again |
| `plan_save {from, project?, plan?, title?, body?, status?}` | `plan {plan, tasks}`, `error(invalid\|not_found)`, `error(identity_mismatch)` | write a plan: a new one (a title needed) when `plan` is absent, else a change to it. Anybody may; a title is at most 200 characters, a body at most 512 KiB, at most 500 plans a project. A card names its plan by id or title (`plan` on `task_create`, `task_update`, `task_create_many`; empty clears it) and its detail carries it without its body. Emits `plan_changed`. |
| `files {project?, task?}` / `file_get {file}` | `files {files}` / `file_content {file, text?, base64?}` | a project's files, or one card's; a file by id, unique id prefix or (in the caller's project) name, with its bytes as `text` when its type reads as text, else `base64`. A `file` is `{id, project, name, media_type, size, sha256, tasks, created_by, created_at, updated_at}`; its bytes are kept apart (`file_body`), and each link to a card is its own document (`file_link`), so a card's files are found by the card. Reads. |
| `file_put {from, project?, name, media_type?, text?, base64?, task?}` | `file {file}`, `error(invalid\|not_found)`, `error(identity_mismatch)` | keep a file in the project (at most 4 MiB; a name of 1–200 characters without slashes; at most 500 files a project): a name the project has already is a new version of that file, its links kept. `media_type` is guessed from the name when not said; `task` links it to a card. Emits `project_file_changed`. |
| `file_link {from, file, task, unlink?}` / `file_delete {from, file}` | `file {file}` / `ok`, `error(forbidden)`, `error(identity_mismatch)` | link a file to a card of its project, or unlink it; delete a file — its uploader or the person — with its bytes and links. Emit `project_file_changed` (`deleted` on a delete). |
| `usage {project?, agent?, since?, until?, by?}` | `usage {rows, by, as_of, effective_since, effective_until, coverage, overhead}` | read hourly local usage grouped by agent/model/provider/project/hour with explicit collection and counter coverage. Defaults to the last 24 hours; strict duration/RFC3339 bounds, retention rounding and unknown counters follow the contract below. More than 10,000 buckets or an overflowing grouped total refuses the query without disabling coordination. Optional `coverage.tracking` reports logical tracking bytes, capacity and a capacity gap for the requested range; admission is bounded without discarding dedupe evidence. Served through the transfer fence. |
| `record_emission {agent, emission:{id, channel, bytes}}` | `ok` / error, `error(identity_mismatch)` | After a completed output write, retain metadata only when usage collection is enabled. Exact ID replay is idempotent; conflicting metadata refuses. Project is resolved from the agent, never supplied by the caller. Restricted credentials can report only for their agent; mutation observes the coordinator fence. |
| `pause {from, project?, reason}` | `pause {pause: {project, by, reason, at}}`, `error(invalid\|forbidden\|not_found\|ambiguous\|backpressure\|storage_unavailable)`, `error(identity_mismatch)` | the person tells a project's agents to hold: one `pause` message from `from` reaches every live agent in the project (archived under `#everyone`, payload `{text: "Pause: <reason>", reason}`) and the pause is written as a document in the same transaction — neither exists without the other, and a refused send (backpressure, storage) pauses nothing. Until it is lifted, `claim` from an agent in that project answers `paused` with the reason, a waiter queued before the pause included (what an agent holds, it keeps; the person's own claims are not held). A declared human sender may pause (`forbidden` for a nonhuman identity); the host socket trusts its owning OS user and `--from user` is not proof of human presence. The authenticated restricted/container endpoint cannot pause or resume a project; the reason is 1–400 characters; `project` is an id, root or unique prefix, the caller's own when absent; a second pause replaces the reason. The document is schema 23's: a daemon older than that refuses to open the database rather than open it and quietly not hold anyone, so rollback requires restoring a compatible pre-upgrade state backup while stopped, or keeping the newer daemon. Lifting a pause does not downgrade schema 23 and does not make that state readable by schema 22. Emits `project_paused`. |
| `resume_project {from, project?}` | `ok`, `error(forbidden\|not_found\|ambiguous\|backpressure\|storage_unavailable)`, `error(identity_mismatch)` | the person lifts the pause: a `resume` message reaches the project's live agents in the same transaction as the document's removal, and their leases are theirs again. `ok` for a project that is not paused. Emits `project_resumed`. |
| `pauses` | `pauses {pauses: Pause[]}` | the projects that are paused, and why, oldest first |
| `contest_open {agent, project?, task, metric, entrants?, channel?}` | `contest {contest, standing}`, `error(identity_mismatch)` | announces a task and fixes the measure; opens a channel for the entrants unless told not to |
| `contest_enter {agent, contest}` | `contest {contest, standing}`, `error(identity_mismatch)` | join an open contest; a latecomer is admitted to its channel too |
| `contest_submit {agent, contest, validation, score?}` | `contest {contest, standing}`, `error(identity_mismatch)` | the validation must be the submitter's own and must have passed; a `validation_seconds` contest ignores `score` and uses the daemon's own timing |
| `contests {contest?, project?, agent?, all?}` | `contests {contests: Contest[]}` | newest first; open ones unless `all`; one `contest` is a lookup by id that ignores the other filters and answers `not_found` rather than an empty list |
| `contest_close {agent, contest, winner?, resolution?}` | `contest {contest, standing}`, `error(identity_mismatch)` | opener or entrant only; without `winner` the ranking decides, and refuses with `conflict` when the entries are inside the noise floor |
| `renew {agent, lease, ttl_secs?}` | `lease`, `error(identity_mismatch)` | responses may include `change_seq`, the durable acquisition boundary; absent on legacy leases |
| `release {agent, lease, summary?, summary_source?}` | `lease`, `error(identity_mismatch)` | holder only; `summary` becomes the journal entry's text; `summary_source` is `explicit` (default) or `transcript` |
| `release_all {agent, summary?, summary_source?, only_automatic?}` | `leases`, `error(identity_mismatch)` | every lease the agent holds, or with `only_automatic` just the ones an adapter took automatically, so a worktree, branch or build-campaign lease the agent asked for outlives a turn; the reply lists what was released |
| `journal_add {agent, summary}` | `journal_entry`, `error(identity_mismatch)` | a note in the agent's project journal |
| `journal {project, since_seq?, until_seq?, agent?, branch?, kind?, path?, grep?, limit?, digest?}` | `journal` or `digest`, `error(identity_mismatch)` for a digest that advances an agent's cursor | newest `limit` entries oldest first, with the project id and durable `head_seq` read under the same state lock (including pruned entries; absent on older daemons); with `digest {reader, max_entries, max_chars, all_branches?, advance?}` the reader's digest since its cursor instead (empty `project` = the reader's own) |
| `journal_prune {project, before_seq?, older_than_secs?}` | `pruned` | drops entries below `before_seq`, older than `older_than_secs`, or both (whichever keeps more); one is required |
| `leases {agent?, resource?}` | `leases` | `resource` filter uses overlap, not equality; `file:` inputs resolve through the same physical checkout alias |
| `webhook_deliveries {webhook, limit?}` | `webhook_deliveries {webhook, attempts}`, `error(not_found)` | a webhook sink's delivery log, by its configured name: the newest `limit` attempts (50 when absent, 1–200), oldest first, each `{webhook, delivery, seq, event, attempt, at, duration_ms, result: delivered\|retrying\|gave_up, status?, error?, next_retry?}` (see [webhooks](#events)); a name no running sink has and no kept attempt carries is `not_found`; read-only, refused on the container endpoint |
| `events {replay?,ready?}` | optional `events_ready`, then stream of `event` or `lagged {skipped: u64}` | replays the last `replay` stored events, then live until the client disconnects |
| `resume_events {after?: {log, seq, digest}}` | `events_ready_at {cursor}`, contiguous `event_at {cursor,event}` replay, `events_caught_up {cursor}`, then live `event_at` | retained coordination events only; subscribe and validate history under one state lock; missing/changed/oversized history gives `event_history_lost` before readiness; storage failures give `storage_unavailable` |
| `logs {agent, follow?, tail?}` | stream of `log`, then `end` | containers: verified engine snapshot, max 10,000 lines/4 MiB, no follow |

Any agent reference (`agent`, `from`, `to`) accepts a full id, a unique id prefix, or a name. Names resolve to the live agent with that name, or failing that to the most recently created finished one (so `logs` works after exit).

Errors: `{"type":"error","code":"conflict|deadlock|paused|not_found|ambiguous|name_taken|forbidden|identity_mismatch|invalid|backpressure|storage_unavailable|event_history_lost|engine_unavailable|build_failed|unavailable|timeout|cancelled|transferring|internal","message":"...","details":{...}?}`. `identity_mismatch` answers a request that acts as an agent from a process that is neither inside that agent's own session nor the person's, and a `register` whose `pid` names a process its sender may not name (see the security model); `details` names the agent (`agent`, `name`) — for a `register`, the process (`pid`), with the agent holding it when one does — and nothing was done. `paused` answers an agent's `claim` while its project is paused; `details` carries the project, the person's reason, who paused and when. `transferring` answers a mutating request advertising `handover_retry: true` while this daemon has offered coordination to a successor: nothing was applied, reads still answer (`peek_input` among them; `delivery_queue` is a mutation, since it records the offer it makes), and the client may retry the same request unchanged once the transfer settles; the same socket path answers throughout, by the successor once it serves or by this daemon if the offer is aborted. The CLI client (and so MCP and hooks) and the desktop app's client retry it every 250 ms for 35 s, longer than the 30 s a successor may take to say it serves, before showing it. The offer, acceptance and abort themselves are not wire requests; `reload` is the only request that starts a transfer. The CLI and desktop clients advertise this capability on each request; older daemons ignore the extra field. Without it, clients receive `unavailable` and a not-applied explanation. They can retry after handover without a decode error, but automatic retry requires a client that advertises the capability. Other errors are not translated, and transport loss after submission is uncertain. `timeout` answers an `ask` that saw no answer in time, `deadlock` a `claim --wait` that would close a cycle or whose wait was ended to break one, and `transferring` any mutating request while the daemon has offered coordination to a successor: nothing was applied, retry against the daemon that answers next.

Lease admission and renewal commit the holder's liveness, lease row and ordered replay event atomically. Conflict replies commit their liveness and initial conflict event together. Planning does not mutate the live lease table; memory and publication advance only after commit. Release deletion, journal and replay commit before memory protection is removed. Storage failure freezes coordination and retains its prior protection; a failed liveness write does not make an agent appear more recently active in memory.

## Leases

A **resource key** is `kind:value`. The daemon interprets two kinds, `path` and `file`, which overlap hierarchically — `path:/repo/src` overlaps `path:/repo/src/lib.rs` and `path:/repo` — so claiming a directory protects everything under it. Every other kind (`branch:`, `task:`, `db:`, or anything you invent) overlaps only on exact match.

**Physical protection and logical overlap.** Write leases use canonical absolute `path:` keys independently of the holder's project. A directory claim covers its physical descendants, including files claimed by agents outside the project. Canonicalization resolves existing symlinks and normalizes missing suffixes, including `..`. An explicit `file:<project id>/<relative path>` input is an alias only when `agent` identifies a matching checkout; unsafe relative paths are rejected. Queries use the same normalization. Linked worktrees and clones can edit independently because they are different physical checkouts. Project id plus relative path describes logical overlap for the ledger and worktree integration; it is not a second write-lock namespace. Containers use an authenticated mount mapping before their paths participate.

Two **modes**: `exclusive` conflicts with any lease on an overlapping resource held by someone else; `shared` conflicts only with exclusive leases held by someone else. An agent never conflicts with itself, and re-claiming a resource you hold in the same mode renews it instead of failing.

**TTL.** Every lease expires. The default is 300 s, the cap is 24 h. Long-running work should `renew` periodically rather than ask for a long TTL: a TTL is a liveness bound, not a reservation. A reaper runs every second; `leases` also expires before listing so its output is never stale.

**Exit.** Process-backed `stop` validates the PID and recorded process identity, sends the signal and records `stopping`. It retains leases until the supervisor or liveness check observes exit; force stop follows the same observation rule. New claims/renewals require `running`. An external agent may explicitly deregister; managed agents finish through supervision and cannot deregister themselves. PID zero and values beyond the positive signed PID range are rejected. Exit releases held leases once.

**Conflicts are informative.** A refused claim returns every blocking lease including its holder, mode, expiry, and note, and emits a `lease_conflict` event. Agents are expected to read the note, message the holder, or wait.

**Waiting.** `claim` with `wait_secs > 0` subscribes to the event stream *before* its first attempt, and on conflict takes a place in the wait queue and sleeps until an overlapping lease clears, a waiter ahead of it leaves, or the deadline passes. One `lease_conflict` event is emitted per request no matter how long it waits. Waiters are served by priority, then arrival — a waiter attempts only when no waiter served before it wants something overlapping that would exclude it — so a newcomer of the same or a lower priority cannot starve one already waiting; two shared waiters do not block each other. Closing a waiting connection cancels its request and gives up its place, so a vanished client never holds the head of a queue. A wait that would close a cycle is broken at once at its victim, which answers `error(deadlock)`. Liveness is checked under the state lock before every acquisition. Claim and renew expiration effects are persisted and announced using the same timestamp as the core operation.

## Messaging

An **envelope** carries `from` (an agent id, or `user` for CLI-injected messages), `to`, a free-form `kind`, a JSON `payload`, an optional `reply_to`, and a timestamp. Most payloads are opaque; built-in question/answer, handoff and notification paths interpret their documented fields.

Five destinations:

- **Agent** — one recipient, resolved by id/prefix/name before publishing. An agent's `send` to `role:<name>` goes to that role's room in its project (below), published as a **Channel** message; for `ask`, `handoff`, `inspect` and a sender with no project of its own (the person), `role:<name>` is the one live agent holding that role in the sender's project (anywhere, when the sender has no project), `not_found` when none does and `ambiguous` when several do.
- **Project** — every live agent in a project except the sender. `project:<selector>` takes an id (any unique prefix) or an absolute path inside the project; the CLI and MCP server turn a bare `project` into the caller's current directory, so `send --to project` needs no ids at all.
- **Topic** — a `/`-separated path like `repo/backend/reviews`. Subscribers give MQTT-style patterns: `+` matches one level, `#` matches the rest. A topic keeps its messages for subscribers that join later (below).
- **Broadcast** — every live agent except the sender, when the person or the daemon says it.

**Projects keep their own.** An agent working in a project reaches that project's agents, its channels and the person — nobody else. Its `all` is published as its own project; another project's agent, that project, or one of its channels is refused `forbidden` with nothing queued, for a send, an `ask` and a `post_question` alike. Its word on a topic is not refused but kept for its project: only subscribers who are that project's agents, the person, an agent in no project or a subscriber with no agent hear it, live or replayed; another project's agent subscribed to the same topic gets nothing of it. The person (a record of runtime `human`) and senders with no record, such as `agentd`, address anyone; an agent in no project is unrestricted and reachable. `list_agents` shows an agent its own project and the person, and the session-start note names no other project's agents. A question may be put to a project; it is open to that project's agents and to the person.
- **Channel** — members of a named channel except the sender, with durable inbox retention even while subscribed.

**Delivery.** For agent, project, channel and broadcast destinations, the daemon durably queues the message for every addressed recipient before publishing it to matching live streams. Opening a subscription replays unacknowledged messages without removing them; stream setup and publication share the state lock. Reconnecting may repeat an unacknowledged message ID, so consumers must deduplicate and explicitly acknowledge processed IDs. A topic has no recipients to queue for; its messages are kept in the topic log instead.

**Topics keep their messages.** Each topic message is written to the topic log (`topic_messages`) in the same transaction as the send's other effects, under one sequence shared by every topic that only grows and is never reused, and with the project it is kept for (above). A topic keeps, per project, at most `[messages] topic_limit` messages (1,000 unless set, at most 100,000) and none older than `[messages] topic_retention` (seven days unless set; `30m`, `12h`, `7d` or plain seconds) in `agentd.toml`; there is no setting that keeps one for ever, and a value that does not read is reported once while the defaults hold. Retention runs on the minute tick within one budget of 1,000 rows for the whole tick, age first, oldest out first, and records the exact count as `topic_messages_pruned` in the same transaction; a burst can hold a topic over its limit until the next ticks catch up.

A subscriber identified as an agent keeps a cursor per topic it has read (`topic_cursors`). On `subscribe` with topic patterns it first gets the kept messages after its cursor on every topic the patterns match, oldest first, then the live stream; on a topic it has never acknowledged it starts from the oldest message kept. Acknowledging a topic message's id (`ack_inbox`, `inbox --ack`, `acknowledge_messages`) moves that topic's cursor to it — never back, so earlier messages on that topic are not replayed again, whether acknowledged or not — and records `topic_cursors_advanced` in the same transaction; an id the agent could not have seen moves nothing. A subscriber with no agent has no cursor: it gets topics live only, unless it names `after_seq`, the topic-log sequence to replay after (each topic message it is sent carries its `seq`); an agent that names `after_seq` replays from there instead of its cursors, which it leaves as they are. One subscribe replays at most 500 messages per topic and 5,000 in all, the newest; when it leaves any out, one `lagged {skipped, replay: true}` frame before the replay counts them, and acknowledging what follows moves past them. A live lag says `lagged {skipped}` with no `replay`, as before; clients that predate the field read both as a lag. The replay is chosen as the topics are read, one at a time under the lock: each kept topic name is found by one indexed seek past the last, never gathered into a list, and its waiting messages are offered newest first to a bound that never holds more than 5,000 candidate sequences, however many topics match, and stops reading a topic as soon as nothing more of it could be kept; per matching topic that is one count and at most 500 rows, and only the chosen messages' bodies are read. The inbox backlog, the topic replay and the bus receiver are all read under the state lock every publication commits and goes out under, so each message is in the replay or on the live stream, never both and never neither; a reconnecting reader still deduplicates by message id. The log and the cursors are durable across a restart. A forgotten record's cursors go with it; a resumed session's record takes the cursors of the record it folds in, each topic at the further of the two; an offline identity repair leaves them, and the kept record resumes from its own. These are new tables, not a schema-version bump: an older build keeps no topic messages and moves no cursor while it runs, so a reader may later be replayed less or again, never anything wrong.

**Where the person's message stands.** For each message the person sends, the daemon records which agents it was queued for and when each took it from its queue; `delivery` combines that with what it already knows — the session's first read without taking it (`legacy_offers`), the latest provider receipt naming the message, a reply from that agent, its readiness and any provider block — into one state per agent. A session caught up after the message is followed the same way. Agents' own traffic is not recorded.

**Catching up a session that opens late.** A project message reaches the agents live when it is sent. A session that opens in the project within an hour of the person's message to everyone there (`everyone:<project>`) still gets it, queued as its first input with one `messages_caught_up {agent, messages}` event in the same transaction. Only messages from the person count; a pause or resume notice, another project's message, and an agent's own word to everyone do not. The newest 50 of the room are looked at. Only a record new to the project is caught up: a session that takes up its own earlier record keeps its own queue, and a record that shares a session or a process with another is not caught up again. A queue without room is caught up as far as it has room, and registration never fails for it.

Each addressed inbox admits at most 1,000 messages and 4 MiB of serialized envelopes. If any recipient lacks room, the entire send returns `backpressure`: no recipient, sender activity or message event changes, and no previously accepted message is discarded. Acknowledgement frees count and byte capacity. The byte total is rebuilt from retained envelopes on restart. A topic message queues for nobody, so no topic send meets backpressure; the topic log's bounds are its retention, not admission.

Explicit `ack_inbox` and destructive inbox reads commit removal of exact message IDs and one `InboxAcknowledged` event in the same SQLite transaction before changing memory. Failed removal/event persistence returns `storage_unavailable` and retains queued messages. Unknown or already acknowledged IDs are a no-op and emit no receipt event; repeated IDs name each removed message once. That event records server-side queue removal; it does not prove the provider consumed the message.

The live bus reports `lagged {skipped}` when a slow subscriber falls more than 1,024 messages behind. Addressed messages remain recoverable through a non-draining inbox read or a new subscription; topic messages through a new subscription, within their topic's retention and the replay bounds. `watch` displays messages without consuming them. The CLI exposes selective acknowledgement as `inbox --as <agent> --ack <id>...`, mutually exclusive with `--drain`. `inbox --drain` is an explicit destructive operation and can still lose delivery if its response connection breaks. Consumers requiring recovery must use non-draining reads and `ack_inbox` only after processing. Event streams exit on lag with an error directing the caller to recover retained history.

**Typed links.** A card, a message, a checkpoint and a hand-off bundle may carry `links`: up to sixteen `{kind, target, note?}`, where `kind` is `path`, `commit`, `pr`, `url`, `task`, `message` or `memory` and the daemon checks only the shape its kind requires (a commit is seven to sixty-four hex digits, a pr a URL or `#<n>` or `owner/repo#<n>`, a url `http(s)://` with no spaces, a task a card id or a prefix of at least four hex digits, a message a message id, a memory its own text; a target is one line of at most 2,048 characters, a note of 200). A malformed link is `error(invalid)` before anything is routed or written. The links are the affordance — a reader knows what kind of thing to open without parsing prose — and the daemon opens nothing: whether a path exists or a pull request is open is the reader's to find out. `task_update {links}` replaces a card's links whole; the app shows them under a message and on an open card. Emitted by the same events as the thing they are on.

A keyed handoff retry returns its original stored bundle before checking current
roles, recipient membership or sender/recipient liveness. A cleared, reassigned
or ambiguous role cannot redirect the saved handoff or hide an uncertain result.
The retry emits no new event or recipient message, including after reopening the
database. New handoffs still validate their current sender and destination.

**Roles.** A role is a word from the project's palette (`role_catalog`) an agent is given — `reviewer`, `implementer` — kept in the `role` label on its record, several sorted and comma-joined (`reviewer,tester`), said as `role_set` with the joined set. Roles are the person's to give: `role_assign {from, agent, add, remove}` refuses an agent (`forbidden`) and a role the palette lacks (`invalid`, naming the palette); the older `role {agent, role?}` replaces the set with one role or clears it. Nothing is given a role automatically. A policy rule on `labels = { role = "x" }` covers each agent holding `x` among its roles.

**A role's tools.** A role can name the MCP tools its holders are offered (`RoleDef.tools`, set by `role_save`); one that names none offers every tool. An agent is offered every tool when it holds no role or any of its roles names none (a role the palette lacks names none), and the person is never limited; otherwise it is offered every tool its roles name, together, and the few every agent keeps to know itself and be reached (`role::ALWAYS_TOOLS`: `whoami`, `send_message`, `read_inbox`, `wait_for_messages`, `acknowledge_messages`, `list_agents`, `list_roles`). The MCP server applies it (`role::tool_limit`): see [`agentdocker mcp`](#agentdocker-mcp-cratesclisrcmcprs).

**A role's room.** An agent's `send` to `role:<name>` is said in that role's room in its project: a channel (`Channel.role`) whose members are every live agent holding the role there, with every live agent that wrote to it, so the sender hears the answers and one thread carries the role's conversation for its whole life. Staging the room with the message (one commit) makes it, opens it again, or brings its members up to who holds the role (`channel_opened`, `channel_joined`); a role nobody holds is `not_found`. The room is called by the role's address (`#reviewer`) when no other open channel in the project is. It follows who holds the role, not invitations: `channel_invite`, `channel_close`, `review_request` and `review` refuse it (`invalid`), its members are recomputed whenever it is read, and its holders leaving never close it; it closes when the role is taken out of the palette (`role_delete`, which also takes the role from every holder). An agent's roles and the members of the rooms they touch are one commit (`role_set` with the rooms' `channel_opened`/`channel_joined`): a holder that puts a role down is out of its room even when nobody is left in it, so it is never later taken for a guest, the older `role` request included. Giving or taking a role is then said in its room by `agentd` ("X now holds Reviewer: …"), so the agent hears what it now holds; a word a full inbox refuses is left unsaid and changes nothing decided. A policy's `send:role:<name>` action governs an agent's word to a role, whether addressed `role:<name>` or by the room's `channel:<id>`. Taking a role out of the palette first takes it from its holders and closes its room, so a retry after a failure still finds the role. `ask` and `handoff`, which go to one agent, still resolve `role:<name>` to its one holder: several holders are `ambiguous`, so a stale record that has not yet been reaped makes the role ambiguous rather than silently choosing. A finished agent's role is not an address. On the restricted endpoint a role is the credential's project's, as it is for a local sender. An agent's name cannot start with `role:` — that is how a role is addressed, and such a name would be reached as the role or shadow it; a record from before that rule which is live under such a name makes the reference `ambiguous` (neither reached nor bypassed) until it is renamed.

### Conversations and the archive

People read messages as a workspace in the shape they already know from Slack and Discord: a sidebar of channels and direct messages with unread counts, one pane that always has a composer, threads, mentions, search. The daemon's routing is untouched by it (agents keep their durable queues and receipts; nothing here changes what an agent is delivered): a *conversation* model for people to read sits on top of the delivery model agents consume.

**Conversations.** Every archived message belongs to exactly one conversation, derived from its destination: `everyone:<project>` is the project broadcast (`Destination::Project`); `all` is `Destination::Broadcast`; `channel:<id>` is a `Channel`, whose id is immutable and which gains a `name` as a label (lowercase `[a-z0-9-]`, at most 40 characters, unique among the project's *open* channels, chosen at `channel_open` or derived from the task; a closed channel keeps its name and id in history and reuse makes a new id; collision rooms the daemon opens keep no name, and a record of runtime `human` is not admitted to one, since it edits nothing; the desktop app lists only `everyone:<project>` and the person's `dm:` conversations, reading `all`, channels and collision rooms through as they arrive, and leaves them to the CLI); `dm:<a>:<b>` is the sorted pair of stable agent ids for `Destination::Agent`, whoever the parties are, so the person's conversations with an agent and two agents' with each other are each one conversation; `notices:<agent>` is what the daemon itself (`agentd`) says to one agent, a system conversation shown as *AgentDocker*. Topics are not conversations: a topic message is kept in the topic log for its subscribers, not in the archive. A message with `reply_to` is a thread reply under its root only when the root is in the same conversation; otherwise it is a plain message that keeps `reply_to` as data. `send --reply-to` is the whole thread protocol; a reply whose root was pruned shows as such. Mentions are `@name` in text and change routing not at all; they are found, not delivered.

**History.** Delivery queues hold only what is not yet consumed, so a workspace needs a bounded archive: a `messages` table appended in the same transaction that publishes a message (a monotonic `seq`, the message id, conversation, from, kind, payload, `reply_to`, `sent_at`), retained per conversation to a count and to `[messages] retention` in `agentd.toml` the way the journal is, pruning keeping each conversation's head sequence, with FTS over the text for search. A broadcast nobody was live to receive is archived (it was said); a send a policy refused is not (it was never sent). At the schema upgrade the archive is backfilled once, by message id, from the rows still queued; nothing acknowledged before that exists anywhere, so history starts there. Questions stay questions (cards with controls) and are also in the archive as messages.

Project-scoped search includes archived direct conversations and notices involving finished records; selected-project conversation lists apply project membership independently of reader membership. An unindexed archive insertion or deletion invalidates the durable FTS completeness marker in the same transaction, including after a prior rollback restored that marker while leaving the index disabled in memory. Equal archive/index row counts alone cannot prove completeness.

**Read state.** The person has a read cursor per conversation (`read_cursors`: reader, conversation, `through` seq, at). Unread is what the archive holds past the cursor; opening a conversation moves the cursor forward, never back, to a seq that belongs to that conversation, and acknowledges only the person's own queued rows in it, so *Clear* per message becomes *read* per conversation; later arrivals have higher seqs and stay unread. Reading never closes a question (a question closes through an accepted answer, its asker's cancellation or its expiry); agents acknowledge their own queues; cursors are for readers, not receivers.

Channel close/review commits the channel record, notification queues/archive,
sender heartbeat, journal and their events in one transaction. A full recipient
queue refuses the action before commit; storage failure rolls back every effect,
so a retry after recovery cannot duplicate a partially committed review.

**Archive during handover.** Conversation lists, history, threads and search
remain readable while writes are fenced. A read cursor changes its queue and
publishes its event only after the cursor/acknowledgement transaction commits.
Retention removes archive rows and records the exact-count `messages_pruned`
event atomically, leaving delivery queues intact. Schema-21 history backfill
waits for the successor's acceptance transaction with the schema bump; refusal,
failure or abort leaves both rows and version unchanged. Immediately after a
successful deferred backfill, search uses the complete literal path until the
next start rebuilds the index. Search fallback changes only in-memory index
availability; later unindexed writes clear the completeness marker atomically.

**Not Elixir.** Phoenix Channels and OTP are the right inspiration (a process per conversation, PubSub, presence tracking) and the model above takes those shapes; a second runtime inside a native desktop app is a cost with no return at a handful of agents on one machine, and the Rust daemon already has the bus and the presence facts.

**App.** A project opens its shared *Chat* with a compact list of current agents and visible project and agent terminal actions. *Agents* provides session details; *More* holds Board, History and technical controls. The cross-project *Messages* screen retains search, unread filters, threads, mentions and named channels. Canonical identities have one current direct-message row; older alias conversation keys remain reachable under *Earlier* with their own history, unread state and drafts. System notices and contested-path rooms are folded under *From AgentDocker*. Temporary projects and earlier sessions are folded and paged rather than filling the primary view.
