Custom agent names are preserved; generated names use the tool, adding a stable short identity when that project contains multiple sessions of the same tool. Branches are context, not identity. The composer stays visible while delivery details scroll beneath it; Enter sends. Project changes preserve conversation drafts and retarget the shared chat. A managed terminal attaches inside the app; an external macOS Terminal action verifies the process and focuses its existing tab. The project terminal opens a shell in the project folder. These presentation changes do not delete registry records, combine distinct concurrent sessions or change queue delivery semantics.

## Channels

A lease keeps two agents out of one file. A channel is what happens when they are in it anyway.

The ledger already records which checkout changed which path. The second checkout to change a path is a collision, so the daemon opens a room for the agents behind those checkouts rather than wait for one of them to notice: `channel_opened`, a message to each member, and a `review` journal entry. One open contested channel per project, not one per file — agents colliding on `src/parser.rs` and then on `src/lexer.rs` are having one conversation, so paths accumulate on the same channel and any newly involved agent is admitted (`channel_joined`). The paths that widened the channel are told to it once a second as one message naming them (up to 50 of at most 2,000 distinct paths kept between ticks, then a count), not one message per path: a branch switch in one of the checkouts touches hundreds of paths in a moment. A channel can also be opened deliberately for a task (`channel_open`), which is the case where agents are told to work on the same thing rather than discovered doing it.

A channel is a message destination, `channel:<id>`. Unlike a topic it has a membership rather than a subscription: an agent put in a channel hears it without asking, and offline members get the message in their inbox like any other. Nothing outside the membership sees it.

**Review is the tie-break.** Inside a channel an agent asks for review (`review_request`) and the others answer (`review`) with `approve`, `changes`, or `comment`. Only a reviewer's latest word on a given author counts, nobody reviews their own work, and a request for changes blocks until that same reviewer says otherwise — so `decision(author, required)` is `Blocked`, `Approved`, or `Pending`. That is deliberately the tie-break rather than a race: when two agents have both done the work, what settles it is what the other agents say about it, not who finished first. Verdicts record the reviewed checkout's HEAD, so a verdict can be read against the code it was actually given.

**The review gate.** A review that decides whether work lands is bound to the commit it read. A reviewer — an agent holding a role the person gave it, or the person — gives `code_verdict` on a branch at a full commit id, and `review_gate` answers for a branch at a commit: only an approval of exactly that commit passes, so a commit added after it, a merge of the target branch included, leaves the gate `stale` until the reviewer has read what came after (`from` the commit it last read). A request for changes blocks until the same reviewer says otherwise. Whose word counts is asked by role (`reviewer` unless the asker names another); the gate names no provider and no repository. `agentdocker gate --publish` also sets the answer as the `agentdocker/review` commit status on GitHub, which belongs to that commit alone, so a repository can require it in branch protection; nothing sets it unless asked.

**No agent reviews what it wrote.** An agent's verdict is refused on the branch its checkout is on now, and on any branch the daemon knows it committed to, wherever its checkout has gone since. The daemon knows only commits it made or witnessed itself, never one reconstructed from git's history (which records no agent when agents share one git identity): a commit it makes for the agent (`commit`), and a commit made with git in a worktree it created (`worktree_create`, `run --isolate`), whose post-commit hook reports it (`report_commit`). That hook is put in the repository's own hooks folder, by one installer at a time (a lock file, `post-commit.agentdocker-lock`, held across threads and processes for the whole look, move and write); a post-commit hook already there is kept, moved to `post-commit.agentdocker-chained` by a link that never replaces a file there, and run after ours in every checkout — one carrying our mark is ours, brought up to date and never chained, and ours never runs a chained copy of itself — and a repository whose hooks live where `core.hooksPath` says gets none. It reports only in a checkout whose own git directory holds the witness file written when the worktree was made — a token readable by this user alone, of which the daemon keeps a hash — through the `agentdocker` command beside the daemon (else the one on `PATH`), never starting a daemon and never failing the commit. The daemon checks the token, reads HEAD and the full ref it names from git itself, and records the commit as the agent whose own session sent the report, which must be the one the worktree was made for or a live agent holding an exclusive `path:` lease over all of it: another agent cannot make a commit somebody else's. A detached HEAD names no branch and records nothing; a checkout, a switch or a fast-forward runs no post-commit hook, so going to a branch to read it is never authorship. Each commit is kept once per project and branch in the `authorship` table, by the branch's full ref (`refs/heads/<name>`, so a tag of the same name hides nothing), announced as `authorship_recorded`, and never pruned with the journal or by its retention; it goes only when the agent's record is forgotten (`rm`, or agent retention), when nothing can ask for that agent's word again. What is not witnessed: commits made with git in a checkout the daemon did not create or in a repository with `core.hooksPath`, and commits git makes without running post-commit (a merge commit, `git am`); for those only the current-checkout refusal stands.

A channel closes when the work is final (`channel_close`, with a resolution that goes in the journal) or when its last live member leaves, which closes it as "everyone left". Closed channels stay readable until `channel_prune` forgets them, a fortnight by default. Close and review commit their document and event before changing memory or sending notices. Pruning deletes the selected set and its `channels_pruned` event in one transaction; failures retain the full set.

## Contests

Several agents attempt one task, and the evidence decides. A channel is what happens when two agents collide by accident; a contest is the deliberate version, and it is the answer to "should they race, or should they review?" — they do both, in that order.

**Correctness gates first.** An entry is a *passing `validate` run of the entrant's own*, in the checkout it is submitting. A failing run is refused with the reason (`it timed out`, `the code changed while it ran`, `it exited 1`), and so is somebody else's passing run: the evidence has to name the submitting agent. So "best" can never mean "fastest to produce something broken", and never "quickest to borrow a green result".

**The measure is fixed before anyone starts.** `contest_open` records it and nothing changes it, so nobody picks the flattering number after seeing the results. Where the daemon can take the measurement itself it does: `validation_seconds` is how long the entry's own validation ran, as the daemon timed it, and a score reported alongside it is ignored — there is a test that submits `-999` and gets the real duration. Anything else is a `reported` measure with a name everyone uses, which the daemon cannot check and review can. `agentdocker contest show` says which kind it is, because one score is evidence and the other is a claim.

**A margin inside the noise floor is not a win.** Also declared up front. Everything within it is a tie, and closing a tied contest on the ranking is refused: the metric has said all it can, so the channel settles it and the closer names the winner explicitly. A contest opens its channel at the same time it opens, because a tie has to be argued somewhere and asking for a room after the numbers are in looks like a loser asking for a rematch. Entrants who join later are admitted to it, so a latecomer can argue its own case.

`contest_open {agent, project?, task, metric, entrants?, channel?}`, `contest_enter`, `contest_submit {validation, score?}`, `contests {project?, agent?, all?}` and `contest_close {winner?, resolution?}` are the protocol; `agentdocker contest open|enter|submit|show|close` and `agentdocker contests` are the CLI — `open` prints the new id and nothing else, as every other creating verb here does, so `contest=$(agentdocker contest open …)` captures an id rather than a report; MCP exposes `contests`, `enter_contest` and `submit_entry`. Closing is restricted to the opener and the entrants, the same boundary `channel_close` draws: settling somebody's work belongs to the people in it. Every step is announced (`contest_opened`, `contest_entered`, `contest_submitted`, `contest_closed`), the channel is told where things stand after each entry, and the result goes in the project journal, because a contest result is a decision somebody will want to read back.

## Events

The complete variant definitions and payloads are in [`EventKind`](../crates/core/src/event.rs). They cover discovery availability and PID/start-time changes; watcher and restricted-endpoint readiness; agent/process/restore transitions; messages/inbox acknowledgements; lease acquisition, waiting/deadlock and release; journal/read observations; worktrees and integration; channels/reviews/contests; credentials, images and containers; validation and handoff; and the opt-in screen judge's standing, promotions, clearings and failures (`judge_status_changed`, `agent_judged_needs_you`, `agent_judgment_cleared` with reason `screen_changed`, `process_changed`, `screen_unreadable` or `judge_off`, `judge_failed`; see [the screen judge](#the-screen-judge-opt-in)). Each event carries a timestamp. Persisted events can be replayed; readiness and lag handling must follow the stream contract above. `agent_id` and `project_ref` are record types, not event variants.

**Webhooks.** A signed copy of chosen events can be posted to an address the person writes in `agentd.toml` — nothing is posted anywhere otherwise:

```toml
[[webhooks]]
name = "team-slack"                       # 1–40 lowercase letters, digits, hyphens; what logs and events call it
url = "https://hooks.slack.com/…"         # https; plain http only to 127.0.0.1, ::1 or localhost; no credentials or fragment in it
secret_file = "/Users/me/.config/agentdocker/slack.secret"  # a regular file of this user, mode 0600, at least 16 bytes
events = ["question_asked", "agent_exited", "lease_deadlock"]  # wire names; empty posts nothing; a webhook's own events are never posted
project = "…"                             # optional: only this project's events
format = "json"                           # or "slack": {"text": "agentdocker: …"}
```

At most eight sinks, each listening to at most 64 kinds. The file and every secret are read off the runtime at start and every five seconds (the secret through a handle that follows no symlink, so what is checked is what is read); a change to the file or to a secret stops every running sink and starts anew under the next generation — nothing queued is carried over, so an event queued under one address and secret can never go to another — and an unreadable file or secret keeps the last good sinks and is said once, without the file's text (a TOML error is given by position, never by snippet). A project-scoped sink posts only events that name that project; an event that names none is not its. Each sink is two tasks off the state lock: an intake that never waits on the receiver and keeps a bounded queue (256 events, 1 MiB; the oldest is dropped and counted), and a sender making one request at a time under a 10-second deadline for connecting and sending together, no redirects, TLS verified against the roots the system trusts ([outbound TLS](#outbound-tls)). What is posted is a projection, never the raw record: `{delivery, event, seq, at, text, ids}` — the kind, the sequence, the time, one line built from identifiers, and the identifier fields the kind carries (`agent`, `project`, `by`, `from`, `to`, `resource`, `message`, `task`, `question`, `requester`, `held_by`, `channel`; a lease's holder and resource); no payload, answer, reason, environment or free text of anyone's. A body over 64 KiB is dropped as `too_large`. Headers: `X-AgentDocker-Event` (kind), `X-AgentDocker-Delivery` (one id for a delivery across its retries), `X-AgentDocker-Timestamp` (Unix seconds) and `X-AgentDocker-Signature: sha256=<hex>` of HMAC-SHA256 over `"{timestamp}.{body}"` with the file's secret, so a receiver can refuse both a stranger and a replay. A 2xx is delivered; a 429, a 5xx or a network failure is tried three times in all (after 1 s, then 5 s, or a `Retry-After` of at most 60 s when longer); any other 4xx is dropped at once. Every attempt is kept in the sink's delivery log in the state database, which `webhook_deliveries` and `agentdocker webhook deliveries <name>` read: `{webhook, delivery, seq, event, attempt, at, duration_ms, result, status?, error?, next_retry?}` — the sink's configured name, the delivery id the receiver saw, the event's sequence and kind, which attempt (from 1), when it started and how long it took, `delivered`, `retrying` (with when the next attempt is due) or `gave_up` (refused, or the last retry failed too), and the receiver's status code or, when there was none, a word for why (`timeout`, `dns`, `connect`, `tls`, `io`, `protocol`, `other`) — never the address, the secret, the body sent, or anything the receiver said beyond its status code. The log keeps the newest 200 attempts per sink and 2,000 in all, the oldest dropped first whichever sink they were for; it survives a restart and a change to the file, and a removed sink's log stays readable until newer attempts push it out. It has no event of its own, since one per attempt would let a failing sink fill the event log the throttle on `webhook_failed` protects, and an attempt made while coordination is being handed over is not kept. An attempt already on its way when its sink stops — the file or a secret changed, or the sink was removed — is still logged when it ends, and is never tried again: nothing of a stopped generation starts a request, and a failure then is `gave_up`. What could not be delivered — refused, unreachable, too large, or dropped for room while the receiver stalled — is counted and announced as `webhook_failed {name, kind, reason, dropped}` at most once a minute per sink — a loss inside that minute is said when it lifts, whether or not anything else arrives — even after deliveries succeed again, committed through the ordinary event path, never posted to any sink, with the sink named by its configured name — the address and the receiver's words never appear in logs or events. This is best effort by design: there is no outbox, so a sink that is down longer than its retries, a daemon restart or a handover loses deliveries. The record stays in the event log and the journal; a webhook is a way to hear about the floor, never the record of it.

`file_changed` and `agent_stale` are also emitted on the live event stream with `seq:0`; they are not persisted in ordered event history. `changes` reads retained ledger observations, and `stale` checks current content directly after a missed live notification.

Schema 15 adds `codex_files` question presentations: directory, reason and an
ordered list of paths, add/delete/update operations, optional rename destinations
and complete diffs. The same 16,000-byte question limit applies, with at most 16
files and no truncated approval text. The native app opens the full review before
enabling Allow once; CLI answers retain the same complete fallback text. Provider
item/turn correlation, one-time decisions and receipt handling are described in
[Codex input](CODEX-INPUT.md#questions-and-approvals).

Schema 16 adds `codex_permissions` question presentations: directory, reason,
concrete read/write/deny paths and network access. The complete fallback text and
native controls describe the same turn-scoped grant, limited to 16 paths and
16,000 bytes. Unknown or ambiguous permission selectors are refused. The
question publication, closure and answer events carry these presentations.

Local `codex_command` reviews include concrete additional permissions and managed
connection context in their checked reason/fallback text. They use the same
presentation and exact human-answer/provider-resolution receipts. Unknown access
selectors, remote environments and prompts without a one-time approval and a
negative response are refused before question publication; policy amendments are not
selected.

Where Codex offers to remember an approval for the rest of its session — a
command or network review whose `availableDecisions` lists `acceptForSession`,
any file change, any permission request — the `codex_command`, `codex_files` or
`codex_permissions` presentation carries `for_session: true` (a network review,
a choice list, carries the option itself), and a third exact answer is
permitted: `Allow for session`. It sends `acceptForSession`, or for a permission
the reviewed profile with `scope: "session"`; Codex then lets the same command,
destination, files or access through without asking until that Codex session
ends. The fallback text names the answer. Without the flag the answer is refused
as any other unknown one, and the flag is omitted when false, so presentations
stored before it read the same. Schema 28 carries it: an older
reader refuses the field.
The private Codex ledger's version 8 binds `decline` or `cancel` to the review;
when only cancellation is offered, the complete question text explains it.
Command-approval callbacks with `networkApprovalContext` reuse `choices` with
the exact host and protocol and Allow/Deny for the pending managed-network
request. The prompt discloses provider grouping of pending connections to the
same destination, preserves any supplied command/directory/access context, and
never invents absent metadata or chooses session/policy grants. Network context
selects this route even when command metadata is present. Private ledger version 9 records the distinct
network review kind; versions through 8 cannot supply it. Command records cannot carry choice presentations, and optional network command/directory metadata cannot inject display controls into the review. Question and receipt
ordering are those of every other review.

Local `writeStdin` command-approval callbacks use a separate private review kind
and the `choices` controls. The complete shell-quoted `write_stdin` argument
vector is decoded without execution and displayed as terminal session, command
item, launch directory and escaped exact input, with the reason and concrete
retained permissions. Callback/item identity, local environment, bounded complete
input and the provider's one-time/negative decisions are checked before publishing
the question. Network context cannot be mixed into this route. Only exact human
`Allow` sends `accept`; other answers send the retained negative decision. Private
ledger version 11 gates this meaning in both open and closed history. The
question events, exact answer correlation and no-replay uncertainty rules apply.

Schema 24 adds `mcp_url` question presentations: requesting server, opaque
elicitation ID, complete provider message and original web URL. It prevents older
daemons from losing these controls when restoring pending questions. The
`post_question`, `answer`, `cancel_question` and question/answer events carry the
review. The shared
answer/send publication path admits only a single `text` property with exact
`Accept`, `Decline` or `Cancel` for a pending addressed URL question, before
persisting the reply. The desktop provides a copy action and these three choices,
without a text field, page fetch or browser inspection. HTTPS addresses and
canonical loopback HTTP addresses are supported; credentials, display controls,
encoded/ambiguous authorities and non-web schemes are rejected. Punycode domains
are shown with a caution, and the entire original URL remains visible.

Managed Codex accepts `mcpServer/elicitation/request` URL and bounded nonsecret
form modes on its bound thread. A supplied `turnId` must match an active input;
an absent one uses that turn when present. An idle request without turn
correlation is retained against the owned thread, with no active or uncertain
input allowed alongside it. Normal input remains queued until resolution.
Threadless, device-verification and secret callbacks are unsupported. Private
ledger versions 12/13 gate URL/form receipts; version 14 additionally gates idle
MCP reviews in both open and closed history. Other review kinds require an active
input turn. Exact human and daemon answer receipts precede the response; `serverRequest/resolved`
precedes queue acknowledgement. Expiry/dismissal sends `cancel`, never consent.
An uncertain response is retained without replay. `accept` means consent to the
external interaction, not browser completion; there is no automatic tool retry.

**Events the sections above do not name** (every variant and payload is in [`EventKind`](../crates/core/src/event.rs)):

- Policy and supervision: `policy_updated` (effective rules or load diagnostic changed), `policy_denied` (what was asked and which rule refused it), `agent_restarted` (a managed agent started again by its policy, with the attempt number), `agent_restored` (a managed agent brought back after a daemon restart, with how many of its reads went stale), `agent_renamed` (a person gave a live agent a name; the id is unchanged), `agent_launched_by` (who started a session that registered itself, as its process's parents say: `by` is `user`, `agent` or `unknown`, and its record carries it as `launched_by` from then on).
- Leases and contests: `lease_waiting`, `lease_wait_ended`, `lease_deadlock` (with the `victim` whose wait was ended); `contest_opened`, `contest_entered`, `contest_submitted`, `contest_closed`.
- Containers and the ledger: `container_updated` (durable container transitions), `image_built`; `file_changed` (ledger observations) and `agent_stale` (stale readers) are live-only (`seq:0`) and cannot be recovered through event replay, and the inbox notification for them uses the separate message kind `stale`.
- Journal and messaging: `journal_appended`, `journal_read`, `journal_pruned` (a project's entries below a sequence were deleted, on request or by retention), `checkpoints_pruned`, `answer_routed` (which way an answer reached its asker: the waiting `ask` or the queue), `conversation_read` (a reader's cursor moved), `messages_pruned` (the archive dropped rows by retention or the cap), `topic_messages_pruned` (topic retention dropped kept topic messages past a topic's age or count, with the exact count), `topic_cursors_advanced` (an agent acknowledged kept topic messages; each named topic's cursor now stands at the sequence given), `agents_pruned` (retention forgot the records of agents that ended before its window and held nothing, each said as `agent_removed` first).
- Input delivery: `input_delivery_reported` (a receiver's ready, received or paused report was recorded), `input_held_for_typing` (a receiver is holding its next input while the person types in its terminal, until the time it names).
- Input bindings: `input_bound` (an external controller became, or resumed being, the sole consumer of an agent's queued input), `input_unbound`, `input_controller_ended` (the bound controller, or a process launched to replace it, is gone), `input_controller_launched` (the daemon started the binding's launch descriptor, with the attempt number), `input_controller_launch_failed`, `input_restarts_exhausted` (the episode's launches are used up), `input_restarts_reset` (a person asked for the controller to be started again), `input_resumed` (a provider session that came back as a new process was joined to the record holding its thread's queue; the new record's id is an alias of it).
- Sessions: `session_resumed` (the same for a session without an input binding, matched by its `session_id` at registration: the ended record that finished last took up the new process, with every retired id — the fresh one and any earlier ended life — the session and the pid; a claim that resolved a record the fold has since retired is granted to the one that stays); `session_duplicates_folded` (historical: live records of one provider session in one process folded into the one that stays, with the retired ids, the session and the pid; no longer emitted, since a session has one record from registration, and kept so stored events decode); `session_relaunched` (an ended session's record was made managed again, with its conversation id, before the daemon started its tool with `--resume`).
- Projects and the board: `project_paused` (the person told a project's agents to hold, with the reason), `project_resumed`; `task_created`, `task_pulled` (with the `task:<id>` lease the pull took and, for a takeover of a card whose holder's lease lapsed, `from` whom), `task_moved` (with the lane), `task_updated` (with the fields that changed), `task_archived`, `task_unarchived`, `task_commented` and `task_comment_changed` (with the comment, and `deleted`) — each with the card, its project and who — `board_changed` (a board made, renamed or reshaped), `rule_changed` and `rule_fired`, `tag_changed` (with `renamed_to`), `role_changed` (a role in a project's palette made, changed or taken out, with `tools` when its tools changed), `plan_changed`, `project_file_changed`, and `memory_added` / `memory_forgotten` (a project's memory kept or forgotten).
- Coordinator transfer: `daemon_transfer_offered`, `daemon_transfer_readdressed` (the offer names the successor process that was actually started), `daemon_transfer_accepted`, `daemon_transfer_aborted`.
- Usage: `usage_recorded` and `usage_reconciled` (see [Token usage](#token-usage-by-agent-model-and-provider)).

## Process supervision

`run` defaults to closed stdin and captured stdout/stderr; `--tty` instead supplies a controlling terminal with attach input/output. Pipe log lines carry timestamps and stream tags; terminal log lines carry an `out` tag and retain line boundaries. The child inherits the daemon's environment plus `spec.env`. It is deliberately *not* given the CLI caller's environment, so secrets don't silently travel through the registry; pass what the agent needs with `-e`. On daemon shutdown every managed agent receives SIGTERM. Exit persistence may finish before its session owner receives acknowledgement and releases its lock. Shutdown therefore waits for held owner locks within the same eight-second shutdown budget and repeats the non-recursive cleanup sweep. A held lock or unacknowledged exit report remains protected when the budget expires.

The log writer batches queued lines through a 64 KiB buffer, avoiding a separate
blocking file operation for each short line. It flushes when the queue empties,
before rotation, and at EOF: a quiet live agent's last line remains visible,
and log completion is still required before reporting exit. Flush failures
remain failures; buffering does not promise filesystem crash durability.

Supervision retains terminal/pipe readers, the input writer and log writer. After
the child and its group finish, it closes terminal input and waits for output
EOF and the final log flush before publishing `agent_exited`, releasing leases
or considering restart. A capture task failure stops the owned producer with
the TERM/KILL grace period and emits `agent_output_failed {agent,
reason}` before the exit event; the exit retains the actual process status.
`agentdocker events` renders the separate incomplete-log reason. Output tasks
are cancelled if supervision is dropped, rather than left detached.

**What a session leaves on disk, and for how long.** Captured output is bounded: the owner rotates `logs/<id>.log` at 8 MiB, moving what it holds to `logs/<id>.log.1` (replacing the earlier move) and starting over, so one agent keeps at most 16 MiB however long it talks; `logs` reads the earlier half first when everything is asked for or the live file cannot fill the tail asked for, and a follower holds the file it reads, so a rotation between two of its reads loses nothing: it drains the file it holds, and when the name has moved to another file (told apart by identity, not size) drains the held one once more for what was written just before the rename, then reads the new one from its start; a rotated half it has already read is not shown again; one rotation per read is followed. `rm` deletes both halves, and the controller log, with the record. On the minute tick the daemon also removes any log under `logs/` whose agent is not in the registry once it is an hour old (`<id>.log`, `<id>.log.1`, `<id>.controller.log`), so a record forgotten while the daemon was down leaves nothing behind (validation logs are named for their validation record and stay with it). The daemon's own `agentd.log` and each `logs/<id>.controller.log` are written through inherited append-mode descriptors nobody coordinates, so they are trimmed in place instead: past 4 MiB the file is copied to `<name>.1` and truncated, and a line written in the instant between the copy and the truncation is lost — they are diagnostic logs, bounded, not evidence. The desktop app removes its `/tmp/agentdocker-ui-<key>` activation directory, lock file included, when it exits, as the daemon removes its socket directory (see the wire protocol); a launch that took the instance lock on a file removed under it meanwhile (`Lock::is_at`) takes it again on the file the name has now, three times at most, so two windows never both think they are the one for a daemon. Retained desktop builds are pruned on request (`desktop prune`).

For supervised native commands, the daemon first leaves out the same session variables, then overrides `AGENTDOCKER_HOME`, `AGENTDOCKER_SOCKET`, `AGENTDOCKER_AGENT_ID` and `AGENTDOCKER_AGENT_NAME` with its own context after applying `spec.env`. It removes `AGENTDOCKER_TOKEN_FILE` because these children use the host endpoint, and sets `AGENTDOCKER_NO_AUTOSTART=1` so an unavailable owner fails explicitly instead of starting a replacement from a child. The same rules apply to initial launches and restored commands, including PTY launches. Explicit container mounts use their separate scoped endpoint and credentials.

## Security model

The host control socket is mode `0600` and trusts the owning user. This is not authentication between mutually untrusted same-user processes. Within it, the person's word is checked rather than taken. A request that changes something as the person is refused `forbidden` before it is handled when the process on the socket — the kernel's peer pid, pinned with its birth when the connection was made, so a later process given that pid is not taken for it — or any of its parents is a provider's session process, a Codex server, or a live agent's own process by pid *and* recorded birth (a reused pid is not an agent). Such a request names the person (`user` or their record) as `from`, `agent`, `reader` or a journal digest's reader; is an `answer` or `mark_read` that names nobody, or a `merge_branch` that defaults to them; is the older `role` request; runs, registers or adopts a `human` record; is a `me` that moves the person; subscribes to their stream; or attaches to an agent's terminal, whose input is the person's keyboard there. A `me` without a location only looks the person up. One path is let through by name: a supervised Codex terminal's controller queuing a `send` from the person to its own agent, which is what the person typed there — only when the sender is the agent's own registered process (pid and birth, never anything under it), the kernel names its program as the record's launch command's file, invoked as the CLI, and its arguments as `codex-input`; and a client's `run` that asks for a supervised Codex terminal is itself refused `invalid` unless its command resolves to this daemon's companion `agentdocker codex-input` (the CLI beside it, by its resolved path: `agentd` is the same file, but started by that name it is the daemon) and it carries no `LD_*` or `DYLD_*` setting; the command is rewritten to that real file at admission, so a link changed afterwards changes nothing, and the session owner passes no inherited loader setting to such a controller. The daemon's own launches (a card's agent) build that command themselves. A session owner takes a controller connection only from the daemon — the process serving its home's socket, by pid and birth as `daemon_process` answers there, or the successor a handover in flight names — never from a program because of its name, so a process that reaches the owner's socket cannot type into the agent's terminal. The walk up the parents ends outside at the first process, at a parent that has ended or is proven another user's (a different owner; on Windows a token this user cannot read proves nothing and is refused), or at a "parent" born after its child; another user's parent's birth is read from the kernel's process table where the per-process query cannot describe it (root's `login` above every macOS terminal), so the walk goes on past it; a parent whose birth cannot be read is passed only when it has ended or is another user's; this user's running process that cannot be read, a gap the walk cannot explain, or a connection whose process has ended is refused, never granted. Reads are not refused, and the daemon's own process is trusted. A peer found inside is remembered by pid and birth; one found outside is believed for ten seconds, and only while no agent has come to stand among the parents it was walked through. A harness's own daemon started with `AGENTDOCKER_PERSON_CHECK=off` skips the check; the variable is read at start and nothing changes it after. An agent's word is checked as well, so one agent cannot speak as another. A request that acts as an agent is refused `identity_mismatch`, before it is handled and with nothing done, unless the process on the socket runs inside that agent's own session or is the person's. It acts as an agent through: `from` of `send`, `ask`, `post_question`, `merge_branch`, `pause`, `resume_project`, the board's, comments', rules', tags', roles' (`role_assign` included), memories', plans' and files' requests, and of `answer_secret_review` and `cancel_secret_review`; `agent` of `claim`, `renew`, `release`, `release_all`, `journal_add`, `task_pull`, `task_move`, `task_update`, `task_archive`, `task_unarchive`, `checkpoint`, `resume`, `handoff`, `commit`, `worktree_create`, `integrate`, `validate`, `observe`, `cancel_question`, the channel, contest and review requests (`code_verdict` aside, which has its own check above), and an adapter's own reports and reads (`heartbeat`, `report`, `report_activity`, `report_adapter`, `report_provider`, `record_emission`, `deregister`, `delivery_queue`, `inbox`, `ack_inbox`, and `subscribe` with `agent`); an `answer`'s `from` or a `mark_read`'s `reader` that names an agent; and a journal digest that advances an agent's cursor. Inside the agent's session: one of the agent's own processes, each by pid *and* recorded birth, is the sender or stands among its parents — its recorded process, its session owner, its input controller (bound, or launched by the daemon and not yet bound) and the provider process its binding names; for a Codex terminal started by the companion CLI's `codex` or `codex-native`, that launcher, which starts the terminal and beside it the Codex server its tools run under and its receiver; and, for a Codex agent whose record names its provider profile, that profile's shared app-server among the sender's parents, which runs the profile's terminal conversations' hooks and MCP servers and whose process tree cannot tell them apart, so one conversation there can act as another's of the same profile. That server is proven by what the kernel says of the process, never by its arguments, which any process may set: its executable is Codex's own copy in that profile's package cache (`<profile>/packages/app-server-daemon/releases/<version>/bin/codex`), and it is the process the kernel names on the other end of the profile's control socket (`app-server-control/app-server-control.sock`, found as the control client finds it); where either cannot be shown — on Windows there is no control socket — there is no such allowance, and the person check's trace decides. The walk goes up from the sender by each process's own parent and birth, read from the kernel a step at a time, and ends at the first of those, at the first process, at a parent that has ended or is another user's, or at a "parent" born after its child. So the agent's hooks, its MCP server, its tools' shells and every session they started act as it — several sessions acting as one agent from inside its session included, and a sub-agent it launched as it too. Otherwise the person check's trace decides: outside every agent's session, the person's — the app, their own terminal — may act as any agent, as they may stop or rename one; from inside another agent's session (another agent's process, a provider's session process nobody registered, a Codex server that is not the agent's), or from a process whose parents cannot all be read, it is refused. A record with no process of its own (a connector's browser agent, a Codex conversation known by its key alone) has no session to be inside, and is the person's to act as. As with the person's word, a process that leaves every session — one whose parent has exited, or a window an agent opens through the system's launcher — is taken for the person's. On every platform a connection whose process cannot be identified (no peer pid, or the process has ended and its pid is another's) is refused: on Unix the kernel's peer credentials name the process and on Windows the named pipe's client process id, and a platform that can read neither process births nor parents refuses. The person (`user` or their record) is the person check's, a name that resolves to nobody is the handler's to answer, the acting name is replaced by the record's id before the request is handled, and the daemon's own process is trusted. Not checked here: requests an input binding's token speaks for (`bind_input`, `upgrade_controller`, `report_input`, `provider_inbox`, `unbind_input`, `resume_input`), the secret reviews' owner requests (`open_secret_review`, `poll_secret_review`), which name their exact owner process, `report_commit`, which its witness token and the session of one of the worktree's owners speak for and whose `agent` the daemon fills from that session, a request that names an agent only as what it acts on (`stop`, `remove`, `rename`, `role`, `role_assign`'s `agent`, `worktree_remove`, `resume_provider`, `retry_controller`, `attach`, `grant_access`, `import`), launches, and registrations, whose `pid` has a check of its own. A `register`'s `pid` is checked in the same way, and so is an `adopt`'s, since the process either names becomes the anchor of an agent's session: the registration is refused `identity_mismatch`, before it is handled and with nothing done, unless its sender may name that process (`acting::naming`), each process by pid and birth as the kernel reports them and the named process's birth read when the request is checked. A process no live agent holds may be named when it is the sender or one of its parents — the sender runs inside it: a hook names its provider past the shells between them, an MCP server its host, `agentdocker register --pid $$` its shell — or when the sender stands among its parents — the sender started it: `agentdocker codex` names the Codex terminal it started beside its server. A process live agents already hold as their own (a recorded process, session owner, input controller, or the launched controller or provider process a binding names) may be named only from inside the session of every agent that holds it, exactly as a request acting as each of them may be sent — so what a Codex terminal's companion launcher started beside the terminal may name it — and never for having started it: registering it is acting as that agent, so a process could otherwise take over the identity of any agent it had started. From under a Codex profile's shared app-server, proven as above by its image and by serving the profile's control socket (so not on Windows), a Codex terminal no live agent holds may be named too, as that server's MCP server and hooks name a terminal conversation's terminal: only in a `codex` spec naming the profile (`provider_profile`) and the conversation (`session_id`), and only when the named process's own arguments resume that conversation (`procinfo::codex_resumes`). A terminal a live agent holds is named from there only as the agent check allows, when that agent's own record names the same profile, or names none and the kernel shows its terminal, the process named, is a client of that proven server (a Unix-domain connection whose other end the server holds): never on the profile and thread the registration names, so a record that names no profile (an adopted or MCP-first terminal) is not the shared server's to bind on their word, and a sender under that server then counts as inside the record's session. Otherwise the person check's trace decides: the person may register any process; from inside an agent's session, or from a process that cannot be traced, it is refused. A registration that would resume an ended record of the session its `session_id` names (see `register`) does so only from outside every other live agent's session, judged as the agent check judges it, when the sender may act as every ended record itself, or from the person; otherwise it is refused `identity_mismatch`, nothing written and no record made, so a process inside agent A's session never becomes ended agent B. A registration whose labels name a Codex conversation (`provider_profile` and `session_id`) known by its key alone joins that record only when its sender may act as it, as the agent check finds it — from under that profile's proven shared server — or is the person: the record the labels select is authorized under the lock that joins it, never the labels, and otherwise the registration is refused `identity_mismatch` with nothing done; a registration that names a conversation no record holds makes a fresh one as before. What the kernel said when the request was checked goes on with it and is held to under the same state lock that joins, folds or inserts the record: if the named process is not the one checked any more (its pid has another birth), or an agent has come to hold it since whose session the check does not admit — a record made for a terminal after a check that found nobody holding it, say — the registration is refused `identity_mismatch` there, with nothing done. `details` carries the `pid`, and the `agent` and `name` holding it when one does. A registration without a `pid` claims no process and is not checked. A harness's own daemon started with `AGENTDOCKER_AGENT_CHECK=off` skips both checks; like the person check's variable, it is read at start. A client's `run`, `run_container`, `register` or `resume_session` spec never brings a `role` or `launched_by` label in: roles are given by `role_assign` only, and a record keeps the roles it holds; `launched_by=user` is the daemon's to write, on a launch whose process the same trace finds outside every agent's session (a launch is never refused for it: one from inside a session runs as asked, unmarked), and on a registered session whose process's parents reach the top through one of the person's session roots with no agent's session anywhere among them (see `register`, which also writes `agent` and `unknown`). `launched_by` is attribution, for showing and filtering (`mine`), never authorization: a window an agent opens through the system's app launcher (`open`, `osascript`) is a child of launchd like the person's own and is not told apart from it, so nothing grants or refuses anything on it. The native receiver hook socket has the same boundary: kernel peer credentials and provider ancestry associate a caller with a session, but cannot distinguish a real provider hook from an unsandboxed same-user descendant calling it. The nonce only correlates a response. A fabricated call can stall a reserved input; context return alone cannot acknowledge it. Invocation isolation is not provided by a same-user-readable secret. State and log directories are created with mode 0700, and database/log files with mode 0600. Existing owned 0755/0644 state is narrowed without truncation; foreign ownership, writable-by-others paths and symlink/hard-link file targets are refused. CLI and GUI autostart apply the same policy before opening the daemon log. Explicit home aliases are canonicalized before validating the state directory; managed internal files never follow symlinks. The separate `container.sock` also has mode `0600` but requires a scoped token: first `authenticate`, then exactly one operation, then close. Host request frames are bounded to `IMPORT_BYTES + 64 KiB` (8 MiB + 64 KiB, including the newline); the handoff bundle itself is limited to 8 MiB. Restricted-endpoint request frames are bounded to 1 MiB and restricted connections time out after 30 seconds. Tokens are stored hashed, scoped to one running agent and physical checkout, and checked for revocation/expiry on every operation. Only mapped path claims/reads, own inbox/lease operations, inspection, direct project-peer messaging, and mapped journal note writes/reads are allowed. Journal digest readers are bound to the token's agent, and cursor advancement is limited to that agent. Host process control, validation execution and credential administration are unavailable. Missing tokens never fall back to host authority. Credential grants and revocations commit their state with the corresponding replay event before reporting success. Failed writes or corrupt credential storage return `storage_unavailable` and disable coordination until restart; event failure leaves no partial grant/revocation. Existing leases survive revocation until normal release/expiry/observed exit. Engine sockets and the host control socket are never container mounts. The remote connector is the only network listener and is not the daemon: loopback only, behind the person's tunnel, OAuth-protected, admitting only the vendors' callbacks, with a messaging-only tool surface and no access to files, leases or process control ([contract](REMOTE-CONNECTOR.md)).

### Outbound TLS

Every outbound HTTPS client is built by one function, `agentdocker_host::http::Policy::agent`. It takes what is each client's to choose — its deadline, how many redirects it follows, whether it refuses anything but `https://`, whether a 4xx or 5xx is an error or an answer, its `User-Agent` — and never how a peer's certificate is verified: that is always the platform's own verifier against the roots the system trusts (`rustls-platform-verifier`: the keychain's trust settings on macOS, the certificate store on Windows, the system's CA bundle on Linux), with verification on. An endpoint behind an authority the person or their organisation added to the system is reached, a self-hosted one included, and the Mozilla set ureq bundles is never used. A body is bounded where it is read, since ureq bounds a body per read. The clients built there, and the limits each keeps (its own section has the rest):

| Client | Where | Deadline | Redirects | Addresses |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Webhook sinks | `agentd`, `daemon/webhooks.rs` | 10 s | none | `https://`, or `http://` to this machine, checked when the file is read |
| Supermemory mirror | `agentd`, `daemon/tasks/memory_mirror.rs` | 15 s | none | the same |
| Screen judge (TypeSafe) | `agentd`, `daemon/judge.rs` | 10 s | none | the same |
| Client ID Metadata Document | connector, `connector/cimd.rs` | 10 s | none | `https://` only, refused by the client before connecting |
| `openai` egress feed | connector, `connector/net.rs` | 10 s | none | the same |

`agentdocker desktop update` is the one outbound fetch not made there: it runs the operating system's own `curl` (HTTPS only, after a redirect too, at most five, bounded in size and time), so it trusts what that `curl` trusts. What it installs does not rest on the transport alone: each archive must match the byte count and SHA-256 the feed names, and the installer checks the payload itself ([downloaded desktop updates](#downloaded-desktop-updates)).

## Worktrees and integration

The commands are `worktree-create --as <agent> <new-path> --branch <new-branch> [--from <ref>] [--seed-build <dir>]…`, `worktree-diff --as <agent>`, and `integrate --as <target-agent> <source-path> --validation <id> [--apply]`. Worktree creation starts at the agent's HEAD, or at `--from`, and keeps existing files; the new checkout's commits are witnessed as authorship through a post-commit hook (see the review gate under [Channels](#channels)), and `worktree-create` says on stderr when they cannot be. `--seed-build` brings build output git does not track (a Cargo `target`, `node_modules`) from the agent's checkout into the new one, so its first build starts warm: on APFS a clone of the whole directory is near-instant and takes no space until either side changes a file; elsewhere it is reflinked or copied, and on Windows it is not seeded yet (see `worktree_create`). Because times are kept, a build tool that compares them rebuilds what the new checkout's sources changed rather than trusting output newer than them. The seed comes from the agent's working copy whatever `--from` names. Register or run the source session in the new checkout separately. Independent physical checkouts have independent write leases even when their relative paths overlap.

Integration requires linked worktrees, clean source/target trees and passing validation whose content identity and HEAD still match the source. Preview returns a diff; `--apply` claims the physical target checkout and runs a no-commit, no-fast-forward merge. The target lease stays held for review, including conflicts. The caller uses Git to inspect, commit or abort, then releases the lease. No automatic commit, force reset, branch deletion or worktree purge is performed.

`run --isolate` (or `isolate = true` in an `Agentfile.toml` entry) gives a managed agent its own writable layer without a separate command: before the process is spawned the daemon adds a linked worktree of the repository the working directory is in, under `<home>.worktrees/<project>/<name>` on branch `agent/<name>` — with the agent's id appended to both when an earlier run left them behind — points the agent's working directory at it, announces `worktree_created`, and witnesses the commits made there as `worktree_create` does (`worktree_witnessed`). If a launch fails before any writer can start, the daemon attempts normal Git cleanup of the new checkout and branch. Changed/dirty checkouts and advanced branches are retained; `worktree_cleanup` reports what was removed or kept. Engine-uncertain launches retain their checkout and protection. The agent is grouped with the repository like any worktree, its `join` line names the worktree and branch, and the worktree stays after the agent exits so its work can be integrated; it is removed only on request (`worktree_remove`). Image runs require `--mount-checkout`; linked Git metadata is mounted separately with its original directory layout, and validation mounts both source and Git metadata read-only.

A worktree made for a session copies in the files the project's `.worktreeinclude` names: one git pathspec per line (`#` comments; a line starting with `-` is ignored), matched against files git ignores or does not track in the main checkout, copied under the same relative paths, symlinks left out, at most 256 MiB. Nothing is written through a symlink the new checkout carries at the file's path or on its way — each folder is opened from the one before it without following a link, and the file is made new, owner-only until its bytes are in — and nothing already there is replaced. On Windows includes are not copied, and the worktree says so. A verbose agent record over MCP names what `run -e` set and never its values. A missing or empty file copies nothing; a copy that fails in part is logged and never fails the worktree. The name is the one other session managers read, so one file serves them all.

`overlap` is the ledger read across checkouts: paths that more than one physical checkout of the project changed, each with the agents attributed there, how often, and the last change and its HEAD, newest checkout first — merge conflicts before they happen, without diffing. `agentdocker overlap [--project] [--since N] [--as AGENT]` and the MCP `overlap` tool (this agent's checkout against the others). Semantic overlap — the same symbol touched from two sides — is not detected.

## Sandboxes and container engines

A sandbox is a property of an agent's runtime, and a container is one optional way to get one: nothing in the core, the CLI, the hooks or the MCP server depends on a container engine, and AgentDocker is complete without one. What follows is the design of that optional adapter.

Docker and Podman are equal targets. AgentDocker is a native host daemon. It delegates image builds and container execution to an installed engine; its own responsibility is physical checkout identity, observed working state, authentication, and verified recovery. The agent runtime (`codex`, `claude-code`, or another adapter) is separate from the container engine (`docker` or `podman`). Apple's `container` and other execution adapters are not supported.

AgentDocker provides worktree operations, a separate authenticated container endpoint, managed image build/launch, authenticated mounts, Podman VM transport and image-bound validation; see [Container engines](CONTAINER-ENGINES.md).

The shared engine interface covers availability/capability checks, image build/inspection and managed container lifecycle, logs and exit observations. Implementations invoke the selected engine with structured arguments. Engine selection is explicit and persisted; a failed engine must never silently switch to another engine or the host. Record the engine, container ID, resolved image ID/digest and platform with the agent. A client process exiting does not prove the container stopped: engine inspection must establish termination before releasing its protection. An unavailable engine leaves status uncertain and protection governed by existing lease TTLs.

Build support uses a common Dockerfile/Containerfile and explicit context, with per-engine handling for unsupported features. Podman accepts both formats, but its `buildx` compatibility does not cover all Docker Buildx features ([Podman build reference](https://docs.podman.io/en/latest/markdown/podman-build.1.html)). Build provenance records the source content identity, build recipe, engine/version, target platform, and resulting immutable image identity. Build success is distinct from test success; validation evidence also needs the image identity and command before it can be reused across container sessions.

The host control socket is never mounted in a container. `grant-access` creates an expiring credential scoped to one running agent and one physical checkout, writes the secret to a private token file, and supplies only `container.sock`. The CLI uses `AGENTDOCKER_TOKEN_FILE`; the endpoint requires authentication and rechecks identity, expiry and revocation for each operation. Container `/work` paths translate to canonical host paths before lease and read-set lookup. Token revocation denies new requests but does not free a running writer's leases. Runtime-native sandboxes may need their own transport configuration; hooks and MCP are not assumed to run outside every sandbox.

Engine adapters must handle rootless ownership and VM mount reachability explicitly. A host Unix socket is not presumed usable through a macOS VM bind mount. The VM bridge may expose only the authenticated endpoint, with a tested path mapping; no privileged engine socket is made available to an agent. Default container policy is no network, no inherited host environment, no engine socket, and only the selected checkout plus the scoped endpoint and token mounts. Network access and additional mounts are explicit configuration. Worktrees isolate edits; container engines provide the process/filesystem boundary.

## Handoff bundles

An agent handing work to another should not have to write its state down; the daemon already knows it. A handoff is a checkpoint addressed to someone. `handoff {agent, to?, task?, note?, transfer_leases?, key?}` makes the checkpoint through the checkpoint path — same release barrier, same idempotency by key, leases released with it unless they are to move — then assembles a `HandoffBundle { schema, id, from, from_name, to, project, task, note, assumptions, next_steps, checkout, version (the checkpoint's content identity), environment (image and execution provenance), vcs, leases (what the sender held when the bundle was made), transfer_leases, read_set, changes (the sender's ledger rows since it joined, at most 1,000), diff (for a sender in a linked worktree: the tracked patch, cut at 64 KB on a line boundary with the worktree named), unread_inbox, journal (the sender's own entries), journal_cursor, created_at, imported_at }`, stores it as a document under the checkpoint's id with `handoff_sent`, sends `to` a message `kind: handoff` whose payload names the id and the task, and appends a `handoff` journal entry ("codex-1 handed off to gemini-2: finish the parser"). Retrying with the same key returns the same bundle. Acceptance is `resume {…, acknowledge: true}` by the addressee — anyone else is refused — after the usual content verification, and it commits ownership in one transaction: with `transfer_leases` the still-live leases listed in the bundle that the sender still holds move to the recipient (`LeaseTable::transfer_selected`, one `lease_transferred {lease, from, to}` each), the read set is seeded from the bundle so staleness carries over, and the recipient's journal cursor is set to the sender's so it continues reading where the sender stopped. A bundle nobody was addressed to (`agentdocker export --as <agent> > bundle.json`) is the same structure; `agentdocker import --as <agent> < bundle.json` on another host re-homes it — the checkout becomes the importer's, read marks move with it, leases and the cursor stay behind — stores it with `handoff_imported`, and tells the importer; `resume` then accepts it only if the content identity matches exactly, as ever. The bundle carries schema 2; older importers must refuse it rather than drop image evidence. `agentdocker handoff <to> --as <from> --task "…" [--note …] [--transfer-leases]`, `agentdocker handoffs`, and the MCP `handoff` and `list_handoffs` tools.

## Runtime inventory, setup and continuous discovery

`agentdocker runtimes` lists the agent tools on the host: for each known runtime (`claude-code`, `codex`, `gemini-cli`, `cursor`, `aider`, `goose`, `copilot`, `amp`, `opencode`) whether its CLI is on `PATH` or in a standard installation directory and which version, and its desktop registration where one is known (macOS bundles; Linux Cursor/Windsurf/VS Code desktop-entry IDs in XDG precedence order), its config directory, and whether AgentDocker is wired in: hooks installed for Claude Code and supported Codex versions, the MCP server registered in Claude Code's selected profile (`~/.claude.json` by default), Codex's `~/.codex/config.toml`, Gemini's `~/.gemini/settings.json`, or Cursor's `~/.cursor/mcp.json`. `agentdocker setup [RUNTIME...] [--dry-run]` writes the missing registrations idempotently, keeping private numbered backups without replacing earlier backups, and prints what it changed. Direct JSON/TOML edits and hook installation use complete atomic replacements and refuse changed inputs. Hooks count as installed only when all adapter events and the full pre-tool matcher are covered by a verified command; executable paths are shell-quoted. A set with our command on some of the events and not on the others is an earlier version's, and is reported outdated with the events it lacks and the fix, `agentdocker setup <runtime>` (`RuntimeInfo::hooks_outdated`: the `runtimes` table's HOOKS column reads `outdated (…)`, `setup --dry-run` says what it would add, a setup plan notes it, `setup --health` reports the hooks check `outdated`, and the Tools card says *Hooks outdated* and offers Set up); none of them is hooks never set up. Claude CLI registration has a 30-second command limit and cannot treat an unverified existing server as success. Existing reserved MCP entries that are disabled or cannot be verified produce a setup error and remain untouched. Runtime inventory reports `unverified` for these entries and unreadable or malformed MCP configuration, distinct from an absent (`missing`) registration; a verified alias cannot hide a conflicting reserved entry. The CLI and native window retain setup review for either state. A wrapper or arbitrary argument mentioning AgentDocker does not prove MCP wiring. Claude Desktop has a separate setup target from Claude Code, and the presence of VS Code does not prove Copilot CLI or an agent extension is installed. Codex CLI, Codex desktop and ChatGPT have independent rows; no desktop integration is inferred from the Codex CLI configuration. Standard CLI fallback directories let a GUI started without a login shell find installations; they do not change PATH or make a configured bare MCP command executable. Linux user `Hidden=true` entries mask system entries, `NoDisplay=true` remains installed, and desktop-entry `Version` is not an application version. Entry reads are bounded to 64 KiB regular UTF-8 files; `Exec` quoting, escaping and field codes are validated; `Exec` and `TryExec` are never run. These are recognized local registrations, not publisher authentication. Failed inventory returns `unavailable`; the GUI retains its last rows and reports the request error while keeping a working daemon connection. The inventory is host I/O in `agentdocker-host::runtimes`; the daemon serves it as `runtimes {}` so the desktop app and the CLI share one answer.

Discovery is continuous while anyone listens: the daemon scans every five seconds with a bounded process-table command whenever an event subscriber exists (the app, an `events` stream, a webhook sink), since its findings are events and a cache; with none, it forks nothing, and `discover` (so `ps`, `runtimes` and `adopt --all`) scans when asked; `adopt <pid>` inspects that one process without a scan. `discover` and `runtimes` hold mutation admission during coordinator transfer. A background scan finishing behind the transfer fence leaves the cached snapshot and events unchanged, preserving the transition for a later scan if transfer aborts. Concurrent requests join the current scan through an atomic flag and notification; no state lock is held while the scan runs. It reconciles results against the current registry under the same state lock as registration. Sessions are identified by PID and observed start time, so PID reuse emits the old disappearance before the replacement appears. Metadata changes emit a new `agent_discovered {pid, started_at?, runtime, project?, cwd?}`; departures emit `agent_vanished {pid, started_at?, runtime, adopted}`. A failed scan emits `discovery_unavailable {reason}`, preserves its last successful snapshot and never fabricates exits. The next successful scan emits `discovery_available`. These events have ordered persisted replay sequences; the live process cache is rebuilt after restart. `discover` and `runtimes` return `unavailable` when a required scan fails; `discover` answers from the last scan, and `adopt --all` registers every discovered process at once. Nothing is adopted automatically.

## Native desktop app

Local desktop installation uses immutable payload versions and one activation
pointer. `desktop uninstall` removes owned launchers and deactivates the prefix;
`desktop prune` removes verified unused payloads. CLI and GUI previews carry a
plan hash rechecked at apply. Host installation helpers give each managed
binary a shared lifetime pin; cleanup holds the exclusive pin through deletion
and rechecks content identity. Pins outlive deleted versions to prevent inode
replacement races. Active, rollback, legacy unpinned and running versions remain;
an installed daemon or connector user service protects retained binaries and
blocks uninstall, including stopped services that hold no running-process pin.
Unix maintenance checks both definition paths and the manager's retained
registrations: launchd GUI/user domain services, and systemd's typed loaded-unit
and unit-file inventories. A removed definition does not erase a cached service
reference. Unavailable or unrecognized manager output preserves the installation;
queries are bounded and never include service arguments/environment in errors.
Systemd cached working-directory references decode its D-Bus missing-ok `!`
flag and retain the resulting absolute dependency even if it does not yet exist.
Home-relative `~`, malformed flags and other ambiguous paths preserve all builds.
Systemd may cache a negative lookup after a status query for an absent unit.
Maintenance ignores it only when typed cached state confirms not-found/inactive,
no definition or overrides, no command, no job or process and no resource settings.
A missing file on a real cached registration still preserves the installation.
Windows task inspection recognizes only the exact encoded daemon and connector
scripts emitted by the shared registration renderers, using literal extraction
and byte-for-byte reconstruction without executing task content. It verifies
the PowerShell executable and checks both literal and resolvable path spellings.
Opaque wrappers, altered scripts, other executables and malformed encodings
preserve the installation even when no store path appears in the action text.
Service registration and desktop maintenance share a permanent owner-only
`services.lock` in each affected managed store. Registration acquires store
guards in path order and pins both the calling CLI and a separately selected
daemon through definition and manager publication. Maintenance holds its guard
from service inventory through removal; contention refuses before mutation.
Cleanup reserves candidate version pins before querying services and holds
them through deletion. It remembers a busy version even when an older registrar
publishes a service and exits during that query; versions pinned only after the
query could otherwise lose both protections in that interval. Preview opens
only existing version pins; apply creates missing pins and recomputes inventory.
Dry-run registration creates no guard, and maintenance preview opens only an
existing one. Unix inventory recognizes exact emitted service definitions and
compares cached manager arguments and environment before selecting named
versions. It validates the selected executable's immutable payload, including
recognized services in other installation prefixes without interpreting their
launcher-placement settings, then retains explicit
program, tunnel, project/feed, state/log, working-directory and environment
paths with both literal and physical alias dependencies. Unknown definitions,
missing fragments, overrides, unknown environment settings and unavailable or
conflicting cached data preserve whole stores.

On a Mac only the visible launcher answers for the app's name in Launch
Services (`open -a AgentDocker`, Spotlight, the Dock). Every stored bundle has
the same identifier and version, and Launch Services registers a bundle
wherever it sits once it runs — the launcher execs the active release inside
its versioned bundle, which then checks in under that path — so retained
releases cannot be hidden from it. Instead `desktop/launch_services.rs`
settles the registrations at the end of every install, update and rollback,
under the installation lock: `lsregister -u` for each release bundle in the
version store and each extracted update payload under the root except the
active release's own (which the launcher runs and which registers itself again
when it starts), then `lsregister -f` for the launcher, last. Publishing a
launcher forgets the replaced copy in its private staging directory; pruning
forgets each build before removing it; uninstall forgets the removed launcher
and then every retained build. Only the real home prefix registers anything,
and nothing is named but the launcher, its staging copy and stored bundles
physically inside the root: `lsregister` resolves links, so the inventory
follows none, and a stored bundle is named only while every component of its
path below the root is a directory and not a link, rechecked just before its
call. The calls of one change share 60 s, whoever makes them (publication,
pruning, settlement), each call capped at 15 s and at what remains; requests
past the budget are not made. An inventory over 1,000 entries is left as it
is. The outcome of every call is the command's `launch_services` field
(`registered`, `unregistered`, `errors`), never a failure of the change.

Windows maintenance recognizes only exact rendered actions around a content-verified same-store controller and daemon or receipt-checked stable bootstrap. It retains every explicitly referenced immutable version, including connector tunnel/project/feed paths, state/log paths and Task Scheduler working directories. Literal and resolved aliases both count. Unknown programs/options, relative or parent-traversing paths and unreadable inventory conservatively retain the store; no arbitrary script is executed to discover paths.

Windows desktop activation uses a private JSON snapshot in
`AgentDocker/desktop`, pointing only to a hash-named immutable version. Launcher
contract 2 denotes receipt-checked Windows bootstrap forwarding and lifetime
pinning; contract 1 continues to denote the macOS redirect. Windows snapshot
publication uses a same-directory rename with POSIX replacement semantics so
open readers retain their old complete record. It has no delete/copy fallback.
Only read-only atomic snapshot readers accept an opened file whose link count
became zero; the strict private-file APIs do not.

Service registration also resolves and guards selected tunnel executables, project directories, local `@file` feeds and state/log paths across all referenced managed stores. Executable references must exist; future data paths pin their existing immutable version without creating data files. Store locks precede version pins and remain held through manager registration. Project/feed/tunnel paths are serialized absolutely on every platform. Systemd definitions use literal command arguments: the command prefix disables environment substitution, percent specifiers are escaped, and control characters cannot create unit directives. Environment assignments are quoted as complete words. Unreadable or malformed version inventories refuse registration; unknown version names hold the store guard without becoming deletion candidates. Unknown service references retain builds conservatively.

The macOS Applications entry is an intact signed copy whose three entry points redirect to the selected immutable release before dispatch. External launcher ownership and exact retained payload hashes permit replacement without modifying signed metadata. An atomic app exchange follows activation; a failed exchange restores the previous activation. App publication and pointer selection are separate filesystem operations, and a surviving older copy follows the selected release. Package metadata records `launcher_redirect: 1`. Selecting an older payload retains a compatible launcher copy and protects its backing release from cleanup; a fresh legacy-only install is refused before activation. The visible copy may therefore have newer metadata than the selected release after rollback; installation status reports the selected version. These are local host operations, not daemon protocol mutations. They preserve
state/provider configuration and do not replace a running daemon. See
[LOCAL-BUILD.md](LOCAL-BUILD.md) for the commands and [DISTRIBUTION-SETUP.md](DISTRIBUTION-SETUP.md) for the limitations.

`agentdocker-ui` is an Iced native window over the same local socket as the CLI. Its four primary destinations are Projects, Inbox, Connections and Settings. Projects combine daemon discovery and private folder pins, restore the last selection, and retain quiet or unavailable roots. Sessions retain their real project/worktree identity; an attached terminal keeps its agent identity across navigation. Project tabs expose journal activity, channels, coordination and bundled CLI commands. The VT terminal's transport is bounded. Setup, answers, launch/stop and installation dispatch checked worker commands; view construction performs no I/O.

`app/sessions.rs` filters current work, attention and retained history without mutating registry identities. Known Codex Node launchers with a native child are omitted during daemon discovery; overlapping UI discovery snapshots require known PID/birth equality before hiding a registered process. `app/shell.rs` owns state transitions and tasks; `app/view.rs` renders observations. `catalog.rs` atomically saves bounded private preferences with one write in flight. `controls.rs` supplies keyboard-focusable controls and `accessibility.rs` maps their semantics to native AccessKit adapters with physical-pixel bounds. The window draws with the tiny-skia software renderer and does not compile the default GPU renderer or image codecs. CLI-only installs remain independent of GUI dependencies. See [the design contracts](ICED-DESIGN.md) and [desktop guide](DESKTOP-UX.md).

Desktop notifications for messages addressed to the human come from the daemon through the app's notification entry point, so attempts do not require an open window. OS permission or delivery failure can prevent display; display does not prove someone read it. A ready event subscription restores connectivity even when no new agent event arrives, and reconnect refreshes snapshots including the selected journal. Stream lag forces a reported reconnect. The client resolves the canonical daemon home and validates private fallback socket directories. `AGENTDOCKER_NO_AUTOSTART` disables startup attempts; otherwise startup failures report early child exit and reap only the client's own child.

The native window's main command queue holds 32 requests and coalesces queued
snapshot refreshes by kind/project. Dispatch releases the key before I/O so a
change during a request can schedule one follow-up. Reply/event delivery uses
64 slots with backend backpressure; each logic pass drains at most 64 messages,
including when the window is hidden. UI submission never blocks. Queue rejection
preserves answer drafts, clears pending setup/installation controls and reports
user actions that were not admitted. The visible journal retains 200 entries,
console scrollback 256 KiB of UTF-8 tail, and recall 100 complete commands within
64 KiB; oversized commands execute normally but are not stored for recall.
Journal snapshots use their optional durable head to retain newer live entries
without resurrecting pruned rows. With an older daemon a snapshot replaces
the visible journal. These bounds do not cover terminal-input buffering or total
payload bytes/RSS.

## Wait queue, deadlock detection, and what an agent is doing

Waiting for a lease is a recorded fact rather than a retry loop in which every sleeper races for a freed lease, and that gives two more things: deadlock detection and a derived view of what an agent is doing.

- **The queue's order.** `agentdocker_core::WaitQueue` (pure, no clock) records waiting requests in one order across the whole table rather than one per resource, so overlapping keys queue together: higher priority first, then arrival. A claim's `priority` runs from -9 to 9 and is 0 when it does not say; anything else is refused with `invalid`. Every ticket is unique, so the order is total, and the queue is listed (`waiting`) in it. A waiter attempts a claim only when no waiter served before it wants something that overlaps and would exclude it; a newcomer of the same or a lower priority therefore cannot take what somebody has been waiting minutes for. A higher priority can: a stream of higher-priority waiters keeps a lower one waiting for as long as it lasts, and TTLs and each claim's own `wait_secs` still bound every wait. Two shared waiters do not block each other, whatever their priorities, so a queue of readers is not serialised by its own fairness rule — and the number published as `lease_waiting.position` counts by that same rule and that same order, so a shared waiter that may proceed at once is not shown as second in line, and `is_next` is that number being zero. `lease_waiting {resource, requester, position}` is emitted on joining and `lease_wait_ended {resource, requester, outcome}` on leaving — `claimed`, `timeout`, `cancelled`, or `deadlock`. One event covers every way a wait ends, and it is what waiters wake on. The `deadlock` outcome is emitted even for a refused newcomer that never joined, because a wait that never began still ended and a subscriber should not have to special-case the one outcome that skips the queue; for an older wait ended to break a ring it is emitted when the ring is broken, and once only. The deadlock check and the join happen under one hold of the state lock, so two claims cannot each look, find nothing, and then both create the cycle they were both checking for. A ring is broken, and broken exactly once, whichever order the two claims arrive in. Every answer a waiting claim gives — a lease, a timeout, a refusal, the deadlock of a wait ended to break a ring — is decided under the state lock, and its place leaves the queue in that same hold, so a claim that already has its answer can never be picked as a later ring's victim, and a victim never answers anything but `deadlock`. A client that simply disconnects has no answer; an RAII guard gives its place up, so it releases its place rather than starving the queue behind it: the request's future is dropped, and the guard with it. Waiters are connection-scoped and never persisted; a daemon restart drops every waiting client, which reconnects and takes a new place.
- **Deadlock detection and the victim.** `wait::deadlock` is a pure depth-first search over two slices the daemon already keeps — who holds what, and who waits for what — so there is no separate graph to keep in step with the lease table.
