Before a claim agrees to wait, it asks whether waiting would close a cycle; if it would, the ring is broken at one wait in it, chosen by the pure `wait::victim` from the cycle and each wait's priority and arrival: the lowest priority; among equals the youngest, by latest arrival, the newcomer's prospective wait counting as the youngest of all. With equal priorities the newcomer is therefore the victim. Every ring found emits `lease_deadlock {cycle, victim}`, `cycle` naming every agent and resource in the ring and `victim` the step whose wait was ended (absent in events stored by a daemon predating it). A newcomer chosen is refused at once with `ErrorCode::Deadlock` and `details.cycle`. An older wait chosen leaves the queue with `lease_wait_ended {outcome: deadlock}`, and its claim, woken by that event, answers `ErrorCode::Deadlock` with the same `details.cycle`, and `details.held_by` for what it wanted; the newcomer then looks again, since another ring through it may remain, and either waits or is the victim of that one. All of it happens under the one hold of the state lock the check and join already share. The ended claim still holds its leases: the answer tells it to give something up, and the waiter it was blocking gets the lease when it does. TTLs bound how long a deadlock could last; detection makes it instant and explains it.
- **Derived activity.** Fresh provider observations distinguish `working` and
`idle`; current blockers and recent daemon coordination may establish waiting
or working activity. Absent or expired evidence yields `unknown`, never inferred
idle. See [Derived activity](#derived-activity) for precedence and expiry.

## The human as an agent

Human location changes commit the agent record and `human_location_changed` event together. Failed or fenced writes retain the previous location and heartbeat.

Orchestration needs an escalation path, and it lives inside the same model rather than beside it. `agentdocker me` registers the person at the keyboard as a persistent agent named `user` with runtime `human` and prints its id, so `export AGENTDOCKER_AGENT_ID=$(agentdocker me)` is the whole of joining as yourself. It is idempotent — a second call returns the same record, and moves it to the project the shell is in — and the record has no pid, so the liveness sweep has nothing to check and never expires it. From then on a person is addressable like anything else: messages queue in their inbox, `watch --me` streams them, the journal keeps their cursor, and `ps` lists them.

`ask {from, to, question, timeout_secs?}` sends a `question` message and holds the connection until an `answer` naming it arrives, or answers `error(timeout)`. The daemon persists outstanding questions alongside their delivery, because an answer names a question by id and only the question knows who is waiting on it; `answer {message, text}` is therefore a one-argument act whether a person or an agent does it. An answer is an ordinary message as well as the end of a wait, so it also reaches the asker's inbox: an `ask` that timed out still leaves the answer where the asker can read it. It does not reach both at once: while an `ask` waits, the answer is held from every read of the asker's queue (durably queued, invisible, with everything queued behind it held too, so order is kept), and `answer_routed {question, answer, route}` records which way it went, `tool_result` when the `ask` was handed it, `queue` when no `ask` was waiting or the one that was ended first. A handed-over answer stays in the queue, recorded as offered (`uncertain` for a bound controller), because the daemon's written reply is not a receipt: a controller checks the provider's record of the tool call before delivering or acknowledging it, and a legacy reader would rather deliver it twice than lose it to a client that went away before reading the reply. A daemon that restarts has no `ask` waiting, and anything it held goes to the queue. `input_batch` says `answers_routed: true` from a daemon that does this. Disconnecting or restarting loses the original waiting connection, but preserves the question until its recorded expiry. A reply closes the route atomically with the answer message, so reconnecting askers can read the reply in their inbox. Concurrent `answer` requests accept one reply; later attempts return `not_found`. Expiry removes routing state and leaves the original inbox message. `question_opened {question, expires_at}` and `question_closed {question, answer?}` events record the lifecycle; a missing answer means expiry. Pending questions are bounded at 512, with expiry checked during maintenance, reads, sends and restart. These semantics do not restore provider context or transfer a live daemon's child processes.

Delivery to the human: `agentdocker watch --me` streams questions, `agentdocker questions [--me]` lists what is waiting, `agentdocker answer <message id> "…"` replies, and the desktop app has a Questions screen where the question and the box you answer it in sit together. MCP exposes `ask_human`, `answer_question` and `open_questions`; hooks expose nothing, because a model asks in prose.

`post_question` publishes the same durable question without holding its connection;
`cancel_question` closes it only for its asker and emits `question_cancelled`.
A question closes through an addressed recipient's accepted answer, its asker's cancellation or its expiry, never through anyone reading it. Generic replies after closure
remain ordinary inbox messages, but cannot change the accepted answer or satisfy
a cancelled blocking wait. Blocking waits require the exact `question_closed`
answer ID. Losing the answer message stream or the question event stream returns
an error with retained inbox state.
The [managed Codex adapter](CODEX-INPUT.md#questions-and-approvals) records
question routes and separates their responses from ordinary provider input.

The daemon raises desktop notifications for messages addressed to a `human` agent, throttled to one per sender per minute. macOS uses the app bundle's `agentdocker-ui --notify-json JSON` entry point and no AppleScript fallback. The JSON carries display text and an optional action `{home, socket, target: {message, agent, project, channel}}`; identifiers and absolute origin paths are validated separately from message text. Native content stores the action in `userInfo["agentdocker.action"]`. Posting failures leave the inbox intact and emit a bounded diagnostic. Linux uses `notify-send` without destination activation. `AGENTDOCKER_NO_NOTIFICATIONS=1` suppresses posting in ordinary fixtures. Notices are built under the state lock and posted by a separate worker.

The UI registers a retained native notification-response delegate before starting the window. Default clicks enqueue navigation; legacy notices open Inbox and dismissal does nothing. `--open-notification JSON` uses the same activation path. One desktop window per home/socket origin is coordinated with a private lock and endpoint (`/tmp/agentdocker-ui-<origin hash>/activate.sock` on Unix, a named pipe on Windows). The queue holds 32 activations, coalesces adjacent identical requests, rejects pressure without evicting accepted items, and validates origin at the receiver. A click for another origin starts the same executable with that child's home/socket environment. The window refreshes snapshots, resolves message/sender IDs and reveals the question/message without submitting drafts; unavailable destinations produce an Inbox fallback.

## Admission policy and budgets

Leases stop two agents editing one file. Policy stops one agent touching something it was never meant to. `<home>/policy.toml` belongs to the host; `<root>/.agentdocker/policy.toml` belongs to a project.

**A project can narrow the host and never widen it.** That asymmetry is the whole reason there are two files: a project policy travels in a repository and could be written by anyone who can open a pull request, so the host is asked first and its refusal is final. A quota works the same way — a project may lower one, or set one the host did not, but the tighter of the two always wins.

Rules match agents by runtime, name glob, project, and labels; every field left out matches everything. Within a rule, `deny` and `allow` are action patterns like `claim:path:/repo/migrations/**`, `send:project:*`, `run:**`. Three steps decide, and the order is the point: a matching **deny** refuses outright, whatever else is written and in whatever order; otherwise, if any rule covering the agent carries an **allow** list, that agent is in whitelist mode and the action has to appear in one; otherwise it is permitted. A machine with no policy file allows everything, and a file that only denies leaves everything else alone.

Globs are the ones used everywhere else: `*` stays inside a segment and `**` crosses them, so `claim:path:/repo/src/*` is a directory and `claim:path:/repo/src/**` is the tree. A `:` is an ordinary character, so `send:project:*` reads as it looks, and a pattern is anchored at both ends rather than being a substring search.

**Path patterns are canonicalised when a policy loads.** The daemon canonicalises a resource before claiming it, so on macOS the action says `/private/tmp/...` while the person wrote `/tmp/...`; a rule that silently matched nothing would be the worst possible failure for a security boundary, because it reads as protection. Only the literal part before the first wildcard is resolved, walking up to the nearest ancestor that exists — a rule about a migrations directory is usually written before the directory is.

Evaluation is pure: `policy::check(host, project, agent, action) -> Ruling` in core, with no filesystem and no clock, so every rule about precedence is a unit test. Refusals use `ErrorCode::Forbidden` with the rule and action in `details`, and a `policy_denied {agent, action, rule}` event fires, so a refusal is explainable from the event stream alone. `claim` is checked once before its first attempt rather than on every retry, and `send` is checked against its destination. A newly registered project policy is loaded before its first governed action. `run:<agent-name>` is checked before native, tmux and container launch side effects, including isolated worktree creation. Native restore/restart paths recheck after asynchronous preparation and before committing execution. Pending tmux and container launches also recheck before start; a refused pending container retains stop intent for reconciliation. Policy changes do not retroactively stop a running process. These are cooperative daemon admission rules, not an OS sandbox restricting arbitrary external processes.

Policy files are checked on the one-second tick using identity, size and change metadata. Only regular files up to 1 MiB are accepted; symlinks, FIFOs, directories, oversized files, unreadability and malformed TOML are errors. Errors retain the last valid rules. If no valid policy has been loaded, an error refuses policy-governed actions until the file is repaired or confirmed absent. Confirmed removal clears that scope. Reads happen outside the state lock and are bounded in size; filesystem metadata latency is not a hard wall-clock guarantee. `policy_updated {project, rules, quotas, error, using_last_good}` commits before changed admission state becomes visible. Failed event persistence retains prior rules and disables coordination. Diagnostics omit file contents.

**Budgets** ride the lease primitive as a quantitative resource kind. `claim quota:tokens --amount 50000` succeeds while the sum of live amounts fits the capacity set in policy, and fails with a `conflict` naming what is left when it does not. A quota is shared by construction — it is spent, not occupied, so exclusivity would make every budget a lock on itself — and the arithmetic happens under the same lock as the lease table, so two claims cannot both see room for the last of it. `Lease.amount` carries the quantity, zero for every other kind, which is what makes their arithmetic ignore it. A quota nobody set a capacity for is unlimited, so a typo in a quota name loosens nothing that was not already loose.

## Supervision policy and dashboard

Delayed native restart tasks retain their pending slot during a transfer refusal. After abort the task continues; the successor reconstructs work from durable exit state and restart policy, including agents without `restore`. Maintenance shares one pending slot per agent and uses the remaining durable backoff. Policy is rechecked before execution; a storage failure does not schedule work.

**Restart policies.** `run --restart no | always | on-failure | on-failure:<n>`, and `restart = "..."` in an `Agentfile.toml`. The default is `no`, because a supervisor that restarts by default turns a command that fails immediately into a loop. `on-failure` counts, and only counts failures: a clean zero ends it whatever the limit, while a signal, a nonzero code and a spawn that never started all count as failures worth retrying. The decision is pure — `RestartPolicy::restarts(status, already)` in core — so every rule about it is a unit test rather than a daemon run.

Three properties make it safe to leave on. An agent **stopped on purpose stays stopped**: `stop` clears the policy on the record, so the reason it will not come back is visible in `inspect` rather than hidden in the daemon, the same rule `--restore` follows. Restarts **back off**, doubling from a fifth of a second and capped at half a minute, because the other case is a command that fails every time and the daemon should not spend a core discovering that. And the agent comes back **under its own id**, as a restored one does, so its read set, journal cursor, leases and ledger attribution continue to describe it — a restart is the same agent running again, not a new one with the same name. `agent_restarted {agent, pid, attempt}` announces each one, and `AgentRecord.restarts` carries the count, because a reader deserves to know an agent has died nine times.

Only a managed agent can have a policy: the daemon has to own the process to start it again, so an adopted or in-pane agent is left alone whatever its spec says.

Restart completion commits the updated identity, attempt count and `agent_restarted` event atomically. Failed completion stops and reaps its owned process; a cleanup timeout leaves supervision responsible for it. Failed storage does not expose a new Running record or schedule another retry. Failed spawn attempts record their count/status with an exit event atomically. Launch validation also rechecks the restart policy and attempt count after asynchronous preparation. Initial launch, snapshot restore and automatic restart use the same pre-exec gate. Closing the gate or losing its owner denies execution; after activation the PID and exact birth identity are already durable. A failed exec records failure through owned supervision.

**`depends_on`.** Names in an `Agentfile.toml` that must be running before an agent starts. `agentdocker up` orders the file by dependency — preserving the order things were written in wherever a dependency does not decide it — and then *waits* for each dependency to reach `running` (up to 30 s), because ordering alone is not enough: an agent that is `created` has not run its first line, and the one about to start may be its client. A dependency that never started (it was not started itself, its `run` was refused, or `up` was given other names and it has not run), or that has already ended for good, means the agent is not started, and says so. The file is validated when it is read, so a name that is not in it, an agent depending on itself, and a cycle are all sentences rather than a wait that never ends.

**`after`.** `after = "build exits 0"`, or a list of such conditions, starts an entry only once each named entry has exited with status zero. Only `exits 0` is a condition: a malformed one (`"build"`, `"build ends 0"`, `"build exits 1"`), a name that is not in the file, an entry waiting on itself, a ring of waits through `after`, `depends_on` or both, and an `after` on an entry with `restart = "always"` (which never stays exited) are refused when the file is read, each naming the entry it is on. When a named entry ends any other way — a nonzero status, a signal, a stop (whatever it then exits with), a start that failed — the entry is never started, and nor is anything that waits on it, however far down; each says why, naming what it waited on and the failure at the head of the chain: `deploy  not started: broken exited 1`, `announce  not started: deploy was not started: broken exited 1`. A condition that can no longer be met decides at once, even while the entry's other conditions are still waiting. "Ended" means for good: an exit that the entry's restart policy will follow with a restart is not its ending (the same pure `RestartPolicy::restarts` the daemon decides by), and only a managed agent is restarted. `up` learns endings from the daemon's own events — `agent_stopping`, `agent_exited`, `agent_restarted`, `agent_restart_cleared` — subscribed before it starts anything and read in the order they were published, so a stop is known before the exit it ends in, and reads each agent's record when one arrives. A record read may already be a newer run than the events taken so far (restarted, stopped and exited 0 while those events are still on their way), so for an agent `up` watches, success comes from the events alone: an exit 0 counts only as its own `agent_exited` with status 0, taken in stream order with no `agent_stopping` before it (a record read while `stopping`, or with a stop marked on its run, counts as a stop too) and no restart to follow it (only `restart = "always"` restarts after an exit 0; an `agent_restart_cleared` taken first says it will not). A record read says only whether the agent is still live, was stopped or how it failed; one that reads exited 0 with no stop on it is left undecided while the stream is live, until the events say what it was. When no stream can be had — the daemon does not acknowledge the subscription within 5 s — or the one there was ends — a reload hands the daemon to a successor, which ends every stream — `up` says so and reads each watched record once a second, where a stop shows only as the stop marked on the record; an exit 0 seen that way with no stop on it still fails closed (`report  not started: serve exited 0 without its events being seen; the exit cannot be shown to be its own`), while a nonzero or signalled exit is still a failure. `up` does not resume a stream that ended. With an `after` in the file, `up` stays until every entry has started or is known never to, printing each entry's state as it changes (`waiting: build exits 0`, `started`, the ending of what is waited on, `not started: …`) and failing when any entry was not started; Ctrl-C leaves what is still waiting unstarted and what already started running. An entry that is never started has no record, so it emits nothing: the events are those of the agents that did run. With `up <names>`, an entry left out is judged by its live agent (watched from then on), else by the latest record of an agent of that name from this file, else as having not run. Nothing watched that record end, and the event history is bounded, so its ending is read from the record (`Ending::before`). A stop marked on the run that ended is a stop, whatever the agent exited with (`report  not started: serve was stopped`), and a nonzero or signalled ending is a failure whatever caused it. An exit 0 meets an `after` only when it is **the agent's own** (`agentfile::own_run`), which takes all of: *the same agent and run* — `stop_requested` is `false`, written when this run began under the record's id by a daemon that keeps the mark and not turned by a stop since; *supervised* — the record is managed, so the daemon started the process and read its exit status itself (a record that registered itself, was adopted or is kept by contact ends with what it or a liveness check said); *the same launch* — everything that decides what the process is matches the entry as the file gives it now (`agentfile::launch_change`): `runtime`, `provider`, `model`, `command`, `workdir`, `env`, `tty` and `in_pane`, each stored as `run` was given it (the working directory is the physical path on both sides, and the variables the daemon gives every child are added at spawn, not stored), so neither a session relaunched under the record (another command) nor an entry edited since — its environment or working directory alone included — counts, while `labels`, `restart`, `restore` and `depends_on`, which do not change what runs, are not compared; an entry with `isolate = true` always fails closed, because its run worked in a worktree made for it and the record keeps that path, not the checkout the file names; and *not a reused name* — its name and its `name` label are the ones the launch gave it, which a rename (`chosen`) or the daemon giving the name up (`generated`) changes. Anything less fails closed (`report  not started: serve ended before this run; its exit cannot be shown to be its own`), a record without the mark among them. An earlier exit counts for as long as its record is retained; once retention has forgotten it, the entry has not run.

**A stop on the record.** `AgentRecord.stop_requested` says whether a stop was asked of the agent's current run, and, once it has ended, whether that ending followed one: the exit a stop ends in may be 0 (a program that traps the signal), and its status alone would read as success. It is set to `true` in the transition that commits `stopping` with `agent_stopping` (`AgentRecord::mark_stopping`): a `stop`, the daemon's own shutdown, a container stop, and the liveness check finding a managed agent's leader gone while its group lives on. It is kept once the agent has exited, across daemon restarts (it is stored with the record) and for as long as retention keeps the record. It is set to `false` whenever a run begins under the agent's id (`AgentRecord::begin_run`): a new record, a restart by its policy (`agent_restarted`), a restore (`agent_restored`), a session relaunch (`session_relaunched`), and a process proven to run a conversation known by its key or kept by its contact (`agent_started`). Where a fresh process's record is folded into the record that stays (`session_resumed`, and `resume_input` with `input_resumed`), the fresh process's own mark comes with its run in the same transaction. Either way a restarted agent never inherits the stop of the run before. That matters most after the daemon's own shutdown, which stops a managed agent without clearing its restore or restart policy, so the next daemon may start it again under its id. The mark rides on those events and has none of its own. The field is additive: a record written without it (by a daemon that did not keep it) reads as unknown, never as `false`, and is written back without it, so stores need no migration. A stop that finds the process already gone records a signal ending directly, without `stopping`; that ending is never success either way.

**`agentdocker top`.** The fleet, live: agents grouped by project with what each is doing, how many leases each holds, and the wait queue underneath. Not a TUI framework — the screen is a few ANSI escapes and the content is the same rendering the one-shot commands use, so there is one renderer to keep correct rather than two. Redraws are driven by the daemon's event stream, so a lease taken or an agent blocked appears at once, with a slow tick underneath to keep relative times honest. Piped rather than shown on a terminal, it draws one frame and exits, so `top | head` is not a hang.

## Sessions and persistence

`run` uses pipes by default for batch commands and `--tty` supplies a controlling terminal for interactive commands. Attaching to that terminal is separate from discovering or adopting an externally started process.

`run --tty` (or `tty = true` in an `Agentfile.toml` entry) gives the agent a **pty** instead of pipes: `posix_openpt` in the daemon, and in the child, between `fork` and `exec`, `setsid` and `TIOCSCTTY` so the terminal is genuinely its controlling one. `setsid` is used instead of `process_group(0)`, not with it — `setsid` makes the child a process-group leader by itself, so signalling `-pid` still reaches its descendants, and doing both would fail. Everything the agent prints goes two ways: whole lines to the log, so `logs` reads as it does for a piped agent, and raw bytes to a broadcast for whoever is attached.

`agentdocker attach <agent>` connects your terminal to it. The local terminal goes into raw mode under a guard that restores it however the command ends, `SIGWINCH` is forwarded as `attach_resize` so full-screen agents lay out correctly, and Ctrl-] detaches. Attaching and detaching are only a client coming and going: the terminal belongs to the daemon, so the agent neither notices nor stops. A slow reader is told it missed bytes rather than being allowed to stall the agent.

Attaching late shows the screen rather than an empty one: the daemon keeps the last 64 KB each terminal printed, and hands it over with the live stream under one lock, so no byte falls between the two or arrives twice.

Only the terminal-output reader owns the broadcast sender. Session handles hold
weak references, so an attached client cannot keep its own output channel alive
after EOF. An attach racing the reader's exit receives the retained tail and an
already-closed receiver; the server sends `end` after queued output. The CLI
reads an independently reopened, nonblocking terminal through Tokio readiness
notifications. Dropping that read cancels it without a stranded stdin worker or
changes to inherited descriptor flags. The raw-mode guard restores
terminal settings on completion.

A pending managed-agent stop is dispatched before further terminal reports, keystrokes or resizes. An in-flight frame has a five-second write bound; a failed stop write enters reconnection with the desired stop retained. Reconnection must deliver that stop before reattaching terminal output or resuming input; a failed retry discards the connection and remains in recovery. The owner preserves its two-second graceful-stop interval. This scheduling rule prevents ready terminal traffic from starving control dispatch; it is not a wall-clock shutdown bound on a loaded host.

Terminal continuity through daemon replacement rides on the session owner: the master stays with the owner, so neither a daemon death nor a `daemon reload` closes it, and `attach` follows: a connection that closes with nothing said, while a daemon still answers on the socket, is a replaced daemon, and the client attaches again to the same terminal through the successor, saying so on the terminal; a connection the daemon ends with `end` is the agent's terminal closing. `watch` and plain `events` subscribe again the same way (live messages and events between the two subscriptions are not replayed; `events --resumable` is the gapless form), and `daemon reload` waits up to 30 s for a mutation still executing rather than handing its `backpressure` to the user. `daemon reload` hands over only behind the experimental `[experimental] reload` gate (`AGENTDOCKER_EXPERIMENTAL_RELOAD=1` on the daemon also opens it); otherwise it returns `unavailable` without touching the daemon or agents. Snapshot restore creates a new process and terminal.

Native launch uses a stateless host gate around `Command`: the forked child establishes its process group/terminal, reports its PID over an inherited private socket, and waits using only async-signal-safe syscalls. The daemon verifies its birth identity, commits the Running identity and event, then authorizes exec. Until authorization, EOF, cancellation or the 30-second child deadline denies exec. A worker completes Command's exec-error handshake; an owned child wrapper kills/reaps a launch whose asynchronous activation is dropped. No command, shell wrapper or helper application runs before the durable transaction. Normal exit supervision polls the owned child and drains its process group before releasing protection.

The gate closes the unrecorded-command execution window. By itself it provides neither daemon replacement nor vendor conversation resumption.

Snapshot relaunch restarts a stored command; live daemon replacement keeps the running process through a planned upgrade. Neither restores an LLM conversation.

**Snapshot restore** has transactional preparation and startup readiness checks. `run --restore` (or `restore = true` in an `Agentfile.toml`) marks a managed agent as one to bring back; opt-in, because starting a daemon should never spawn processes nobody asked it to, and `agentdocker ps` starts the daemon. On startup, before the liveness sweep can retire anything, such an agent is relaunched **under its own id**. That is the whole of it: the read set, the journal cursor, the checkpoints, the ledger attribution and the leases are all keyed by the agent id, so restoring the identity restores the working set with it rather than handing out a fresh shell in the right directory.

A clean shutdown records restore intent and stops its agents, so their records read `exited`; a crash normally leaves them `running`. An interrupted prepared restore can remain `created` with its durable point. Naturally completed commands have no shutdown point and remain completed. Points older than twelve hours are not automatically relaunched. An agent whose process group is somehow still alive is left alone rather than started twice, and an agent stopped on purpose is not restored — `stop` clears the flag on the record, so the reason it will not come back is visible in `inspect` rather than hidden in the daemon.

Leases need one extra step, because releasing them when an agent stops is correct: once nothing is working on a resource, the resource is free. So the daemon writes a **restore point** for each restorable agent immediately before its own shutdown stops them — the resources they hold, with the mode and note of each — and puts them back on restore, as new leases with fresh expiries. After a crash there is no restore point and none is needed: nothing released anything, so the lease table is still the truth. A restore point older than twelve hours is ignored, because a daemon that has been down for half a day is not resuming a session, and re-taking a stale lease would be claiming a resource for work nobody is doing.

What the agent is told arrives as a `restored` message, so it reaches the agent by whatever route its runtime already reads — the inbox, a hook's injected context, `wait_for_messages`. It carries the checkpoint it last saved and the next steps it recorded, how many observations its read set holds and **which of those paths changed while it was down**, the leases it holds and which of them had to be put back, and where its journal reading had got to. That is the difference from restoring a multiplexer's layout: a restored agent resumes with evidence, not with a directory name. Every restore is announced as `agent_restored`, carrying how many paths went stale.

What restore cannot give back is the *same* process: it relaunches a stored command. Keeping the process itself through a daemon's death is the session owner's work.

**Session owners.** Every `run` starts a small process of the daemon binary, `agentd --session-owner`, and hands it the launch on stdin. The owner prepares the command behind the same launch gate, owns the child, its process group, its terminal or pipes, its 64 KB scrollback and its log, and serves the daemon on the agent's session socket (`<socket dir>/s/<agent>.sock`, beside the daemon's own socket, or in the short private directory when the home is too long for a socket name). The daemon is its controller: it says when the launch record is durable (`activate`), relays keystrokes and window sizes, asks for output from a byte offset, and acknowledges the exit report. The owner obeys only its newest controller (takeover and command acceptance share one lock), denies exec when its controller leaves before activation, reports every byte with its offset so a reconnect replays nothing twice and says when a gap scrolled out of retention, holds an exclusive lock per agent id, and writes its exit report to an fsync'd exit file that stays until a daemon has recorded the exit durably (a command that ends before it is activated is recorded as `failed` with its reason in words — its exit code, its signal, or that the program could not be executed — never the report's fields, and a leave stored by an earlier daemon with the fields spelled out is read the same way, the could-not-be-waited-for case included (`journal::readable_failure`, applied by `JournalEntry::line`, the stored summary unchanged); a command that could not be executed is one such report: the daemon records the failure and acknowledges it at once, so the owner and its lock go without waiting out the owner's own deadline, and a relaunch under the same id — a session brought back after a wrong path — is not answered "did not answer" meanwhile). The record carries the owner's pid and birth (schema 17: an older daemon that rewrote the row would erase that binding, so it refuses the database instead). A daemon that restarts reattaches to each live owner, checking the owner's and the child's pid and birth against the record, and resumes the screen from the last byte it showed; an owner that finished while nobody watched is read from its exit file; a dropped link to a living owner is reconnected rather than treated as an exit, so a daemon hiccup never releases a running agent's leases. Tests run owners in-process; the daemon binary runs them as processes.

**Live daemon replacement** rests on the session owners above and on the **coordinator fence**. A daemon that is about to hand over calls `offer_transfer(successor_pid)`: the last write it makes is a `coordinator` row (one row, the fence) naming the transfer, itself and the successor, with a `daemon_transfer_offered` event in the same transaction. From then on every mutating request answers `ErrorCode::Transferring` before anything is applied, every tick writer (liveness, lease expiry, pruning, retention, policy reload) skips its turn, and reads keep being served from the projection this process still holds. Settling is a compare-and-set on that row: the successor's first act on the same database is `accept_transfer`, which succeeds only for the offer addressed to its pid and still offered; the predecessor's `abort_transfer` succeeds only while the offer is still open. Whichever lands first wins and the other learns it lost, so two coordinators never both write: an accepted predecessor stays fenced and leaves without touching agents, an aborted successor exits without writing. An offer is refused while any admitted mutation is still executing, so its final write follows every side effect that was already under way; a mutation that has written what it will write and now only waits, an `ask` for its answer or a `claim --wait` for its lease, gives its place up first and takes one back before writing again, so a pending question never holds an offer back; every offer, accept and abort is written in the same transaction as the row, so the durable state never lacks its event. A daemon that opens the database while an offer is pending starts fenced: startup recovery corrects memory but every recovery write is held back and runs only as the named successor's first act after acceptance, so a stranger that opens the same database never writes it. A store that has latched a failure cannot offer, because there is nothing trustworthy to hand over. A write the fence skipped is reported to the writer that asked for it, never to a read: `ping`, `inspect` and `list` keep answering from memory while an offer is open.

The third boundary, **successor readiness**, is experimental, behind the `[experimental] reload` gate in `agentd.toml` (`AGENTDOCKER_EXPERIMENTAL_RELOAD=1` on the daemon also opens it). `reload` runs the candidate executable's `--build-info` (`AGENTDOCKER_RELOAD_CANDIDATE` when set; otherwise the release the managed installation has activated since this daemon started, found through `current/payload` from the kernel's resolved path of the running executable; otherwise the daemon's own), bounded to 10 s through the host's process-group runner, and refuses another host or an older state schema before offering anything. It then offers the transfer, and the offer's own refusal keeps its code: `backpressure` while a mutation is still executing, `conflict` over another offer. It spawns the candidate with `--take-over` in its own session holding one end of a socketpair, and sends a FORMAT 2 handover over `SCM_RIGHTS` naming the listening socket, the daemon lock and the container endpoint's listener; the successor holds all three for its life, so no autostart finds the lock vacant and no client finds the socket gone. A daemon whose container listener could not be kept for a handover refuses `reload` rather than hand over a daemon without container access. The successor opens the database *pending*: its schema comes forward, the recorded version does not, so an aborted takeover leaves a database the predecessor still opens. It reattaches every session owner while still fenced, and only then makes its first write, `accept_transfer`, which brings the recorded version forward in the same transaction; acceptance and readiness are one moment, and the *serving* answer follows at once. Only that answer, within 30 s, lets the predecessor go, and it goes without stopping anything: the agents, socket and lock are the successor's. A fenced daemon does not compete for owners: its supervisors stop reconnecting while an offer is open, so the successor's attachment is not superseded, and take the owners back if the offer is aborted. Any other outcome, a refusal, a death, silence, kills the successor's session and takes authority back, unless the store says the successor accepted, in which case it was already serving and the predecessor leaves rather than write beside it; a successor that dies after that is a serving daemon that crashed, which the service manager restarts with the session owners intact. `desktop install`, `update --apply` and `rollback` ask a running daemon to reload once a release is activated and report its answer, so an installation switches the serving daemon in place when the gate allows and says what keeps serving when it does not. A lost or unexpected reload reply is not treated as a refusal: the installer probes again without replaying the mutation and reports `reloaded: null`, the prior `before` observation, and the newly observed `serving` daemon (or `null` when no daemon answers). Without the gate, `reload` returns `unavailable` without mutation.

A complete replacement must preserve child ownership, batch stdout/stderr, PTY input/output and scrollback, append logging, process identities and protection. It must quiesce writes, select the intended installed binary, validate compatibility, and receive successor readiness before retiring the predecessor, with bounded failure recovery. Descriptor transfer alone provides none of these properties.

Attached terminal input and resize, including an initial requested size, take transfer admission through their enqueue operation. Fenced frames are refused without applying them, using the client's advertised handover error capability; read-only terminal output keeps flowing. Typed input is not replayed automatically after a refused or uncertain write.

**Registration resumption** refuses a fresh record once it has input-delivery evidence: a receiver may already have offered its head, and merging older backlog would reorder that offer. A pre-initialization fold is protected by Claude channel locks for both the agent ID and the provider PID/birth generation, so changing to a canonical agent ID cannot admit a duplicate adapter; hooks check that process lock too. A refusal retains both queues and is not reported as a completed handover.

## The app's terminal and command bar

A window that can only look at things is half a product: everything the CLI can do has to be reachable from it. Two different needs, and they want different answers.

**Attaching** uses the managed agent's PTY and the same `attach` protocol as the CLI. The app renders its output through a VT parser and sends keystrokes and resizes back in order. Its input queue admits at most 32 messages and 64 KiB in total; a single input must also fit 64 KiB. Admission accepts the whole input or none, and rejection remains visible until dismissed. Adjacent resizes coalesce without crossing a keystroke. A rejected resize is deferred until a later UI pass; keystrokes are never automatically retried.

The terminal reader limits each newline-delimited response to 256 KiB, including the daemon's encoded 64 KiB replay. OSC control strings are separately limited to 64 KiB across responses: [vte's generic OSC bound](https://docs.rs/vte/0.15.0/vte/struct.Parser.html) does not apply with its default `std` feature. Accepted bytes reach the VT parser unchanged; malformed, incomplete or oversized output ends the attachment with a reason. These are attachment budgets, not a total process RSS ceiling or deletion of daemon logs.

Closing remembers cancellation before a connection arrives, shuts down an installed socket and wakes the input worker without idle polling. Reader completion and writer failure use the same shutdown path; the background reader joins its writer while the UI remains responsive. A pending OS connection/open still inherits the client's transport and startup behavior; this is not a deadline for a saturated listener or daemon autostart.

**Other commands** run through the console, which invokes `agentdocker` and renders its result. Dedicated controls cover common actions such as stopping or adopting an agent, setup, installation maintenance and answers to questions. Console output and command recall have separate history budgets.

What the app must not become is a multiplexer: panes, layouts, and tiling are herdr's and tmux's ground, and building them here would spend our effort on their strength rather than ours.

## Terminal multiplexers

A tmux start commits the record and `agent_started` event before reporting success. A failed commit attempts cleanup only of the pane and process identity this request created. Attach reconnect failures return an error without claiming the agent exited. On Windows, attachment also recognizes a complete Win32 keyboard record for the local Ctrl-] detach key when nested console output enables mode 9001. Other input is preserved byte-for-byte; fragmented records have a 64-byte prefix bound and incomplete prefixes flush after 25 ms without new input, on EOF or before a read error.

AgentDocker does not write one: `tmux` exists, herdr exists, and a multiplexer is not the working set. What it has is an adapter, in two halves. In the first, an agent living in a `tmux` pane, a `screen` window, a `zellij` session or a herdr session is recognised as such, and that is recorded beside its record, shown in `ps` and `discover`, and returned by `inspect`. That makes AgentDocker composable with whatever owns the terminal instead of competing for it: a person reaches the agent with the tool that already has it. An agent in a herdr pane whose hooks have not run in its current process also takes its activity from herdr ([Derived activity](#derived-activity)).

**How it is known, and why that differs by platform.** A multiplexer tells its children who they are through the environment — `TMUX`/`TMUX_PANE`, `STY`/`WINDOW`, `ZELLIJ_SESSION_NAME`/`ZELLIJ_PANE_ID`, herdr's own — which is exact and names the pane. Reading it is where the platforms part. On Linux `/proc/<pid>/environ` is readable for the caller's own user, so the daemon can look for itself. **On macOS it is not**: `ps -E` returns only the command line for a process other than the caller — even one owned by the same user — so a daemon that only looked would find nothing. (What a *privileged* caller sees is not relied on.) The host reads its own environment directly, preserving spaces and value boundaries; another process's flattened `ps` command text is never first-hand environment evidence. The answer is to have it reported first-hand instead — a client registering itself is running *inside* the session, so `register` carries what the client read of its own environment, and the daemon prefers what it can read itself, then what was reported, then ancestry. Ancestry — a `tmux` or `zellij` process between the agent and its shell — is the last resort: true, but it cannot name the pane, so it is recorded as `evidence: ancestry` rather than dressed up as the real thing. For tmux the record also keeps the server's socket (`socket`, the first field of `TMUX`, or for `run --in-pane` the `#{socket_path}` of the server that made the pane), since a pane id names a pane only within its server. Going to an agent in herdr asks herdr which pane holds the agent's own provider session now (`pane.list`, each pane's `agent_session.value` against the agent's `session_id`) before falling back to the pane it registered from: herdr gives a pane moved to another workspace a new id, so the recorded one can be stale. Only one pane holding that session counts; none or several falls back.

Registering from inside a `screen` session records, for example, `{kind: screen, session: "<pid>.<name>", pane: "0", evidence: environment}`, and `ps` shows `screen:0`.

**The window around it.** Registration also records `terminal` on the agent: the application its terminal is in and its terminal device, from the process's ancestry — the nearest ancestor that is a known terminal (Terminal, iTerm, Ghostty, WezTerm, kitty, Alacritty, Warp, a Linux emulator) or any macOS application bundle (an editor's integrated terminal is its editor: Visual Studio Code, Cursor, a JetBrains IDE, Zed). AgentDocker's own programs are never that application: the CLI (`agentdocker codex` wraps Codex in the person's terminal) and any helper beside it are passed over to the terminal they run in, and the daemon (`agentd` or `agentdocker daemon run`, session owners included) or the desktop app ends the walk with no host, since what runs under them is in AgentDocker's own terminal or in none. Its programs are known by their names (`agentdocker`, `agentd`, `agentdocker-ui`) wherever they are installed, by an `AgentDocker.app` bundle at any path (the managed installation's versioned bundles included), and by a bundle under another name whose `Contents/MacOS` holds `agentdocker` or that the reading process runs from. The conventions are pure, in `agentdocker_core::terminal`; the walk (`agentdocker_host::terminal::walk`) reads each ancestor's executable, not its flattened command line, and tells the daemon from the CLI (one file under two names) by the name it was invoked by and a `daemon run` in the arguments it was started with, read whole from the kernel rather than from `ps`, which splits a path with a space in it; one of AgentDocker's files whose arguments cannot be read ends the walk with no host, since it may be either. A multiplexer's server has no such ancestor, so an agent in a pane has none; it is reached through the multiplexer's client instead. The desktop app goes to an agent from this: in a tmux pane it selects the pane and switches a client to it (`select-window`, `select-pane`, `switch-client`); in a herdr pane it sends herdr's own `pane.focus` over the session's socket (under the person's home, as herdr places it: `agentdocker_host::multiplexer::herdr_socket`); then it walks again from that client or the agent itself, brings forward the application found, selecting the exact tab by its terminal device in Terminal and iTerm (an Apple event the person allows once), and otherwise activating the application. A walk that ends at AgentDocker's daemon or app opens the app's own terminal for the session whose process AgentDocker started on the way down, named by its pid and its birth (read after the walk, no later than the birth of the process walked from, which must itself be unchanged) and matched against the session's record by both, so a number reused meanwhile opens nothing; the app says so when no session shows it; AgentDocker is never the application brought forward, and a recorded host that names it is not offered as a place to go. Nothing is typed into the agent.

**`run --in-pane`** is the other half. The daemon asks `tmux` for a new detached session named after the agent, hands the child `AGENTDOCKER_SOCKET`, `AGENTDOCKER_AGENT_ID` and `AGENTDOCKER_AGENT_NAME` through `tmux new-session -e`, reads the pane id it printed and the pane's pid, and **registers** what tmux started. A person then reaches it with `tmux attach -t <name>`.

Registered, not supervised — that division is the whole design, and it decides everything else about the path. tmux owns the process, so there is no captured log (the output is on tmux's terminal, and `tmux capture-pane` is where it lives), the agent ends when its command ends and the ordinary liveness sweep notices, and `stop` signals the pid tmux reported. What the agent does get is everything that matters: an identity, the project, leases, a read set, a journal cursor, and the pane on its record so `ps` says where to find it. `--in-pane` needs a `workdir` — tmux has to be told where to start — and tmux 3.2 or newer, since `new-session -e` is how the agent is handed its own id; both are checked before anything is created, so "tmux is too old" is an answer rather than an agent that started and failed. It refuses to combine with `--tty` (two terminals for one agent, and tmux is providing the one), with `--restore` (a daemon that did not start it cannot bring it back), or with `--image-build` (a container is started by the engine, so there is nothing for tmux to own). The CLI refuses all three at the argument level and the daemon refuses them again, because MCP, an `Agentfile.toml` and the protocol all bypass the CLI.

## Token-lean output

Everything an agent reads from us costs it input tokens, and an agent reads `ps`, `journal`, `stale` and `channels` many times per session. [rtk](https://github.com/rtk-ai/rtk) compresses *shell* output before an agent sees it, which is real but orthogonal: our MCP results never pass through a shell, so nothing outside AgentDocker can shrink them.

AgentDocker's own output is therefore lean: MCP tool results are compact JSON rather than pretty-printed, and `whoami`, `inspect_agent`, `list_agents`, `list_leases` and `list_channels` answer with a projection — for an agent, its id, name, runtime, status, project and branch, not the pid, process group, host and four timestamps the daemon keeps for itself — with `verbose: true` to opt back into the whole record. Absent fields are omitted rather than sent as `null`. `whoami` also says whether the agent's project is paused (`project_paused`: `false`, or its reason, who asked and since when, from `pauses`; left out when the daemon cannot say), so an agent checking a delayed lifecycle notice needs no shell — a sandboxed Codex would otherwise ask the person to allow `agentdocker pause --list`. The CLI keeps its tables, which are for humans. Where we run somebody else's command and rtk is installed, `logs --compress` and `validation <id> --compress` offer a compressed *view* of a retained log — never a compressed log, because a validation log is evidence and evidence is kept whole. The file on disk is not touched, and a host without rtk, or an rtk that fails, gets the whole log and a line saying why.

## Derived activity

Compact agent projections also include `provider_availability` when recorded
and derived `input_readiness`; verbose records retain both. Readiness is
`session_ended`, `unreachable`, `unverified`, `paused`, `stale`,
`awaiting_first_receipt` or `verified`. Unreachable is a live session that
nothing of its current process has ever reached AgentDocker from — no hook or
MCP contact, no receiver, and no read of its own queue (`inbox` or
`ack_inbox` records `queue_read` for the current process) — two minutes after
it started
(`input::unreachable`). A terminal Codex that names no thread, a runtime with
no adapter here, or a session started before setup is in this state. Senders see it as the `unreachable`
send issue ("Can't receive messages") with what would connect the session,
never "may wait"; a browser agent, the person and a process of unknown birth
are never called unreachable. Verified requires a fresh receiver report and a prior receipt from
the same process generation. It does not confirm a particular message or
override provider limits. Hook/MCP contact alone cannot establish idle wake.

**One readiness decision.** `readiness::decide(agent, now, provider_block)`
is the one place the provider block, the receiver evidence and the kind of
session are put together; the send snapshot, `delivery`'s waiting states,
`ps` (its INPUT column and `--input-details`), the MCP projections and every
desktop surface render its result rather than assembling their own. Its
states, first match wins: *ended*; *provider blocked* (a block takes
precedence over current receiver evidence); *paused*; *silent* (a receiver
that reported for this process and went quiet); *unreachable*; *awaits
check-in* (a browser agent from the connector, or a session known by its key
alone that nothing has been heard from for `SESSION_PRESENCE`: it reads when
it is next used); *waits for prompt*; *ready*. Each has exactly one fix per
runtime:

| State | Claude Code | Codex | OpenCode | Other runtimes |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| ended | `reconnect`: its resume command when a resumed terminal comes back under its record, `agentdocker reconnect <id>` when only the daemon can bring it back, else `none` | `codex_resume` when a resumed terminal comes back under its record, else `none` | `none` | `none` |
| provider blocked | `wait_for_provider` | `wait_for_provider` | `wait_for_provider` | `wait_for_provider` |
| paused | `review_delivery` | `review_delivery` | `review_delivery` | `review_delivery` |
| silent, waits for prompt | `reconnect` as above, else `setup_then_reconnect` | `launch_live` | `none` (its plugin wakes it) | `read_inbox` |
| unreachable | `setup_then_reconnect` | `codex_resume` as above, else `launch_live` | `setup_then_reconnect` | `read_inbox` |
| awaits check-in | `wait_for_check_in` | `wait_for_check_in` | `wait_for_check_in` | `wait_for_check_in` |
| ready | `none` | `none` | `none` | `none` |

An unreachable Claude Code session is set up first: without
`agentdocker setup claude-code` it has no AgentDocker MCP entry, and the
resume command alone fails. A resume command is offered only for a record a
resumed terminal comes back under (`identity::resumes_from_a_terminal`: a
plain conversation id, an absolute checkout of a project, and nobody else's
process running it — not supervised, contained, paned, restored or bound);
for any other, a plain resume would register a fresh record beside the one
holding the queue. A Claude Code session AgentDocker supervised comes back
through the daemon instead (`identity::relaunches_here`, what `resume_session`
accepts); Codex has no such relaunch, so a supervised or bound Codex record
gets no command. A state's label, the short hint beside a command
or button, and the long guidance all say the one fix; a desktop readiness dot
takes its tone from the state, never from its words.

MCP sends report `accepted_by_agentdocker` with provider receipt and idle wake
unconfirmed. The daemon-wide bus subscriber count is omitted because it says
nothing about the recipient. CLI send prints only the message ID on stdout;
the acceptance notice goes to stderr. Topics and empty broadcasts may have no
queued recipient.

Managed Codex MCP identity checks locate the nearest Codex binary, including
the app-server role hidden from ordinary runtime inventory. The exact
managed role, controller ancestry, kernel birth and physical working-directory
checks authorize that identity; a nested Codex is never skipped to reuse
an outer session. The launch-specific configuration marks each enabled local
AgentDocker MCP entry as required, while preserving disabled entries and tool
approval policy. A failed local connection must not be treated as a verified
reply route through a different account connector.

Successful sends also return optional `recipient_readiness`: the number of exact
durable recipients, the number needing attention, and at most 32 named details.
The snapshot is computed after the same message transaction commits, excluding
the sender and agents outside the actual destination, from the one readiness
decision: every state but *ready* needs attention. Human inboxes do not need a
provider receiver. The snapshot neither acknowledges input nor retries
it, and missing data from an older daemon means unknown. Names are bounded and
control characters removed. Each detail carries the state as its `issue`
(`session_ended`, `provider_blocked`, `delivery_paused`, `receiver_silent`,
`unreachable`, or `no_receiver` for both *waits for prompt* and *awaits
check-in*, which the fix tells apart; `unknown_session` for a record that is
gone) and an optional `fix`: `{"kind": …}` with `reconnect` (and the whole
`command` to paste, which includes a session label only when it is a bounded
ASCII identifier, never shell syntax), `setup_then_reconnect`, `codex_resume`
(and its `session`), `launch_live`, `wait_for_provider`, `review_delivery`,
`read_inbox`, `wait_for_check_in` or `none`. A kind a client does not know, or
a command or session with control characters in it, reads as unknown and the
client points at `ps --input-details`; a detail without `fix`, from an older
daemon, is said without one. A runtime with no input adapter (`custom`,
`gemini-cli`, `cursor`, …) is told the message waits in its queue for the
agent's own read (`inbox`/`watch --as <id>`, `read_inbox`) — never an
instruction to reconnect a receiver that cannot exist. MCP includes the
guidance for that fix with each detail, and `list_agents`, `inspect_agent`
and `whoami` add `fix` to an agent's projection whenever it is not `none`.
`list_agents` decides it among every record the daemon holds — ended ones and
other projects' included, so a quota block an ended session left still holds
the agents that share it, as in `send` and `ps` — and narrows only the rows it
shows, to the caller's project and the person, and to live ones unless `all`;
`inspect_agent` and `whoami` decide from the one record's own block. CLI stderr shows each named issue and its guidance; `ps` has an
INPUT column and `ps --input-details` gives the guidance for every listed
session, without the send snapshot's 32-detail cap. UI advice belongs to the
original composer and expands on demand; it is not persisted as fresh state
after reopening.

Herdr marks every pane working, blocked, done or idle. That is the right question, and for an agent that reports its own turns we answer it better, because we know *why*: an agent waiting on a claim is blocked **on a named resource, held by a named agent**; fresh provider observations distinguish working from idle, and an agent with neither fresh provider evidence nor recent coordination is unknown. Silence is not proof of idle; blocked resource evidence takes precedence. AgentDocker derives that from the working set rather than from terminal output, and it is what `ps`, `activity` and the desktop app show beside each agent. For an agent that reports nothing of its own and lives in a herdr pane, herdr's reading of its terminal is the best evidence there is, and the daemon takes it; for a Codex conversation hosted by Codex's shared app-server, the server's own status is better still.

**Where activity comes from**, first match wins (`State::activity_of`): a finished record is `finished` and one with no process yet `starting`; a place in the wait queue is `blocked` on its resource; then the newest recorded observation — the agent's own (`report_activity` from its hooks, MCP server or CLI), herdr's or Codex's shared server's — when the agent's hooks have run in this process, when it came from herdr or the server and is still current, or when no contact has come since it was made: what it says while it is current, `unknown` once it has expired; an agent whose hooks have run in this process is otherwise `unknown`; contact through the daemon in the last two minutes is `working`; anything else is `unknown`.

**herdr, for agents that report nothing.** A task in the daemon follows herdr for each running agent recorded in a herdr session (`session.kind` `herdr`) that names its provider session (`session_id`) and whose hooks have not run in its current process — Codex without its hooks, Gemini, another CLI. One watcher serves each herdr socket some agent needs: the default session's `~/.config/herdr/herdr.sock` or a named session's `~/.config/herdr/sessions/<name>/herdr.sock`, under the person's home (`agentdocker_host::multiplexer::herdr_socket`; a server started on a socket of its own with `HERDR_SOCKET_PATH` is not followed). It reads `pane.list` and takes the one pane whose `agent_session.value` is the agent's provider session — never a pane by its own or the agent's display name, and none when no pane or several hold it, nor when two such agents claim the session on one socket, which is checked again as each status is recorded — then holds one `events.subscribe` for `pane.agent_detected` and for `pane.agent_status_changed` in those panes, and reads `pane.list` again once the subscription is acknowledged so no change made in between is missed. herdr's `working` is `working`, `blocked` is `waiting` with reason `other` (herdr does not say for what), `done` and `idle` are `idle`, and `unknown` says nothing. Each is recorded as an observation with `source: herdr` and announced as `agent_activity_reported`; the same status is recorded again at most once a minute, and the panes are read again every two minutes (every ten seconds while a session is in no pane yet, since herdr announces nothing when a pane's agent names its session), so a status herdr keeps saying stays current. A detection or release anywhere reads the panes again and subscribes anew if a session moved, since herdr gives a pane moved to another workspace a new id; a status from a pane that does not hold the session is nobody's. A herdr observation lasts five minutes whatever it says, a wait included, so one herdr stopped renewing (the pane released, herdr gone) does not stand for hours; while it lasts it stands over contact, and it is not contact itself (`last_seen` is unchanged). The watcher ends, closing its subscription, when no agent needs its socket any more — the agent ended, its hooks ran, it is no longer in herdr; it reconnects with backoff from one second to a minute when herdr is absent, refuses, or ends the subscription (`events_lost`); and it only reads (`pane.list`, `events.subscribe`), never writes. The screen judge finds an agent's herdr pane through the same binding and the same one-pane check (see **Which screens** under [the screen judge](#the-screen-judge-opt-in)). Bounds: 16 herdr sockets at once, 64 sessions a subscription, a 4 MiB `pane.list` reply and a 64 KiB event line, three seconds for every connection, request and acknowledgement. The request and reply shapes are pure in `agentdocker_core::multiplexer::herdr`, the I/O in `agentd`'s `daemon::herdr`. Not on Windows, where herdr listens on a named pipe.

**Codex's shared app-server, and which word wins.** For a live Codex record keyed by a profile whose shared app-server has its control socket, the daemon's watch ([Codex input](CODEX-INPUT.md#terminals-in-codexs-shared-app-server), `agentd`'s `daemon::codex_threads`) records the conversation's status as its own observation, `source: codex_server`, through the same recorder as herdr's and announced as `agent_activity_reported`. Like herdr's, it is not contact (`last_seen` is unchanged), it lasts five minutes whatever it says, a wait included, and an unchanged status is recorded again four minutes after the watch last recorded it; while it lasts it stands over contact, so the agent's MCP server calling never turns a server-reported idle or wait into working. The same word already on the record is restated as the server's when it is herdr's, or an idle or wait the agent's own adapter reported, so contact and herdr no longer supersede it; a running turn the adapter reported is left as it is until due, since contact reads it the same way and a Codex's hooks report every tool run. The watch ends only the waits it recorded: when the conversation unloads or fails or the connection ends, a wait that is still the very observation the watch made becomes the server's idle; a wait the agent's hooks or herdr reported since is left to its own expiry. Where both speak for one agent — a proven Codex terminal in a herdr pane — the server's word wins: it reports the conversation's own state, a running turn or the approval or question it stopped for, where herdr infers it from what the terminal shows. A server observation replaces a fresh herdr observation at once, and while it is current herdr's is not recorded over it (`ActivitySource::outranks` and `ActivityObservation::keeps_off`, applied by `record_activity` and checked first by herdr's watcher); once it lapses unrenewed herdr's counts again. The agent's own report ranks with neither source: between it and a source's word, the newer stands.

## The screen judge (opt-in)

An agent can stop for the person without saying so: a provider with no lifecycle hooks shows an approval dialog, asks a question in prose, or sits at its prompt over words the person typed and never sent. The screen judge reads such a quiet screen with a TypeSafe System One model (Jev), which answers typed yes/no questions with the probability of yes, and may move the agent into Needs you. It is off unless the person turns it on in `agentd.toml`:

```toml
[judge]
judgments = ["screens"]                      # the one judgment this build knows
key_file = "/absolute/path/to/typesafe.key"   # absolute, a regular file of yours, mode 0600
# model = "jev-1.13.0"                       # the default: a versioned model, not an alias that moves
# url = "https://api.typesafe.ai/v1/systemone"   # the default; https, or http only to 127.0.0.1, ::1 or localhost
# calls_per_minute = 6                       # the default, 1–60
# threshold_percent = 75                     # the default, 50–99
```

With no `[judge]`, or no judgment named, nothing is read for judging and nothing is sent. Judgments named without a usable key — a missing or relative `key_file`, one that is not a regular file of this user, one any other user can read (not mode 0600), one that holds no token — or with an address that is neither `https` nor plain `http` to this machine, an unknown judgment, a model name that is not 1–64 letters, digits, dots, hyphens or underscores, or a budget or threshold out of range, leave the judge `unusable` with the reason, said once in the daemon's log and once as `judge_status_changed`, and again nothing is read or sent. The file and the key are read every five seconds (the key through a handle that follows no symlink), so turning the judge on or off needs no restart. Turning it off, or a file or key it cannot use, takes every standing judgment off with it (`agent_judgment_cleared {agent, reason: judge_off}`), since nothing watches those screens any more. Each change of how the judge stands is announced once, as `judge_status_changed {status, reason?}` — `on`, `off`, or `unusable` with the reason, which names neither the key nor the address — in the same hold of the state lock as the generation it belongs to, so the events keep that order; a read that leaves it as it was announces nothing, though every disabled read stops it again.

**Which screens.** Only screens AgentDocker already holds or is bound to, of live agents: the terminal of an agent the daemon started with one (`run --tty`, and every agent the desktop app launches), drawn from its last 64 KiB of output through a terminal emulator at the window size last asked for (80 by 24 until a client sizes it, and never more than 500 columns by 200 rows, whatever a client asks, since the emulator allocates every cell at once); the tmux pane an agent registered from, when tmux named it in the agent's own environment; and the herdr pane holding the agent's provider session. Pane ids are per server — two servers each have a `%0` — so the server is part of the binding: the socket from the agent's `TMUX` (or, for `run --in-pane`, the one tmux reports for the pane it made) is kept on the record, and the pane is addressed there with `tmux -S`, never at whichever server the daemon's own environment would select; a record without a socket is not read. Before each capture the pane's process (`#{pane_pid}`) must be the agent's process or an ancestor of it, by the process table, and the agent's process must still be the one the record names (its birth unchanged); the pane's process is read again after the capture and must not have changed. A pane replaced under the same id, a server restarted on the same socket or a record that names a pane its agent is not in is therefore never read. Each tmux run is a child with a two-second deadline, killed and reaped past it, its output bounded. A herdr pane's id changes when the pane moves, so the pane id an agent registered with is never read: the pane is found as the herdr status bridge finds it, through the same code — `daemon::herdr::herdr_binding` names the socket (the default or named session's) and the agent's `session_id`; the session is the agent's only while no other live agent claims it on that socket (`sole_claimants`, the rule by which the bridge drops a session two agents claim); and `daemon::herdr::held` reads `pane.list` and keeps the session only where exactly one pane's `agent_session.value` is it — so the judge and the bridge cannot disagree about which pane is the agent's. Both checks are made immediately before each read and again immediately after it, the agent's under the state lock and never held across the read, and the agent's once more in the same hold as a judgment's record; the read is herdr's `pane.read` with `source` `visible`, `format` `text` and `strip_ansi` true: the visible screen as text, as `capture-pane -p` gives a tmux pane's, then the same 30-line tail. The request and reply follow herdr's bundled API schema (`herdr api schema`), and a live reply is kept verbatim in the tests. The reply must be `type` `pane_read` for that pane, source and format, with its `text` and `truncated`; anything else, or a screen cut short to nothing, is no screen. A session no pane or several panes hold, one two live agents claim (before the read or while it is out), one that moved to another pane while it was read, herdr's refusal, a reply out of shape and a herdr that is absent or does not answer are each read as no screen for that pass, and none is the judge's failure: no `judge_failed`, nothing in its `problem` (what each does to a judgment already standing is under **When it ends**). Each herdr request is on a connection of its own within three seconds, as the bridge's are, with a reply of at most 1 MiB. A herdr that does not answer `pane.list` — absent, not taking connections or silent past those three seconds — is asked nothing more in that pass, the reads again before a screen is sent or its answer applied included, so it costs a pass one timeout however many agents it holds, and the next pass asks again; a refused or unreadable reply, a failed `pane.read` or a session no pane holds is that pane's alone and never stops the other sessions on the socket being read. herdr is only read, never written to, and not on Windows, where it listens on a named pipe. Managed terminals are read before any pane, so a tmux or herdr that does not answer holds up only its own panes. Not judged: a tmux pane known only from ancestry; screen and zellij; and any terminal application's window (Terminal, iTerm, Ghostty, an editor's terminal), which AgentDocker never reads.

**When.** A pass every five seconds reads each screen's last 30 lines, with escape sequences stripped and trailing space cut, each line at most 300 characters, blank lines at either end left out. A screen whose lines have not changed for twenty seconds is quiet: a working agent's screen changes at least every second or two (a spinner, an elapsed-time counter, streamed output), so twenty seconds is well past any of those and soon enough that a prompt left open is noticed within half a minute. A quiet screen that is answered is not asked about again until it changes; one whose call failed for a reason that may pass (`unauthorized`, `rate_limited`, `unavailable`, `timeout`, `unreachable`) is asked about again once the minute's backoff below is over, and one `refused` or `malformed` is settled as if answered (see **Failures**). Only an agent its own record shows as working, idle or unknown is asked about: a wait its adapter reported, a block on a lease, a start or an end is never sent. A due screen is read again just before it is sent and again before its answer is applied: one that moved on in between — a prompt answered in the terminal, with no hook to say so — is not sent, or its answer is dropped, and its quiet period starts over. The file and the key are read every five seconds on their own, apart from any pass, so turning the judge off or changing the section or the key is seen while a pass waits on an answer; each change is a new generation, and a pass stops before its next upload and drops any answer it holds from an earlier one.

**What is sent, and to whom.** One `POST` to TypeSafe's System One API (`url`), with the key only in `Authorization: Bearer`, carrying the model, a state of the 30 lines and one fixed sentence saying what they are, and four questions — whether an approval prompt is open, whether the agent is asking the person something, whether work is still running, whether the input area holds words typed and not sent. No agent id, name, project, path or anything else beyond what the screen itself shows. A request has a ten-second deadline for connecting, sending and the answer, no redirects, TLS verified against the roots the system trusts ([outbound TLS](#outbound-tls)), and an answer of at most 64 KiB. Calls are counted over a sliding minute against `calls_per_minute`; a pass that reaches the budget stops, and the screens left wait for the next pass.

**What an answer may do** (`agentdocker_core::judgment`, pure). An answer is read only in the contract's shape: the versioned model that answered and four `noul` probabilities between 0 and 1; anything else is `malformed`. Then `decide`: work read as running at 0.5 or more does nothing; otherwise the likeliest of an approval prompt, a question and an unsent draft promotes when it reaches `threshold_percent` (an approval prompt wins a tie, being a choice too), and nothing else does. The default of 75 was set on staged screens of Claude Code's and Codex's approval dialogs, questions, running work, drafts and empty prompts with their placeholder suggestions: every real approval, question and draft answered 0.79 or more and every wrong yes 0.56 or less. A promotion is kept on the agent's record as `judged` — the reason (`permission`, `question` or `draft`), the probability in thousandths, when the screen went quiet and when it was read, the process, a digest of the judged lines (never the lines) and the model — and announced as `agent_judged_needs_you {agent, judgment}` in the same commit. `judgment::raise` applies it to what the agent is otherwise doing: working, idle and unknown become `waiting {since, reason, judged}`; every other state — a wait its adapter reported, a block, a start, an end — is left as it was. Nothing the model answers can take an agent out of waiting, clear a state or lower one; its answers are advisory and AgentDocker reads no state from an agent's words anywhere else.

**When it ends.** A judgment stands while the same live process is behind the agent and nothing its own adapter reported is newer than the screen it was read from: a report from the agent outranks a reading of its screen at once. The next pass takes it off the record (`agent_judgment_cleared {agent, reason}`, by the pure rule `judgment::review`) when another process is behind it (`process_changed`), whatever its screen; when its screen has changed (`screen_changed`); or when its screen can no longer be read (`screen_unreadable`). A screen lost for a reason of the agent's own takes the judgment off in the pass that finds it so: a second live agent claims its herdr session (found as the pass begins or while a read is out), no one pane holds that session or it moved to another pane while it was read, herdr refused or garbled that pane's read, tmux answered that the pane or its server is not there, the pane no longer holds the agent's process (replaced under the same id, or the agent's process is not the one the record names), or nothing is left to read it from (no terminal, pane or binding). A source that gives no answer at all — a herdr socket silent on `pane.list`, a tmux that could not be run or ran past its two-second deadline, a process table that could not be read — says nothing of this agent's screen, so it takes the judgment off only on the fourth pass in a row that finds it so (`judgment::OUTAGE_PASSES`), a pass that reads the screen starting the count again. A herdr or tmux busy for a moment costs a pass or two and moves nothing, so a standing wait does not drop out of Needs you only to come back with the next answer; one dark for four passes — twenty seconds at a pass every five, the quiet period itself — has gone unseen for as long as it takes to judge a screen at all, and the wait does not outlive its source. Each judgment comes off once, with one event, under the state lock with its record (the agent-side checks — a second claimant, nothing left to read — in the same hold that finds them), however many passes find it over. A judgment taken off because its screen could not be read forgets the screen with it: once the screen can be read again it is watched from the start and, when quiet, asked about again rather than taken as already answered.

**Failures.** A failed call never stops the daemon. It becomes the judge's `problem` in `ping` and `daemon status` until a call succeeds, and `judge_failed {reason, failures}` at most once a minute — `unauthorized` (401 or 403), `rate_limited` (429), `unavailable` (5xx, 529 among them), `refused` (any other answer), `timeout`, `unreachable` or `malformed`, with how many failed since the last such notice; a loss inside that minute is said when it lifts. After an `unauthorized`, `rate_limited`, `unavailable`, `timeout` or `unreachable` failure no call is made for a minute and the screen is asked about again; a `refused` or `malformed` one settles the screen, since the same request would fare the same way. Neither the status, the event nor the log names the address or the key; the log carries each answer's four probabilities at debug level, never the screen.

The desktop app shows a judged wait in Needs you with a chip saying so (see [the desktop guide](DESKTOP-UX.md)); `activity` says *judged from its screen* with the probability, and `events` prints each of the four events.
